ABSTRACT
During 2004-6, important changes have taken place that should lead to a more rapid deployment
of gasification technologies world wide. With crude oil at 50-75 $/bbl and natural gas in the
range of 8-10$/MBtu, power companies, petroleum refiners and chemical producers are
increasingly looking at other sources such as coal, petroleum residuals and tar sands to meet their
fuel and feedstock needs. Concern over the continued availability of natural gas at competitive
prices has led many power companies to initiate studies and projects on clean coal technologies
as a strategic hedge against over reliance on natural gas alone to provide future power needs.
Integrated Gasification Combined Cycle (IGCC) plants can meet very stringent emissions targets
including those for Mercury and CO. Several years of commercial operation have been
2
accumulated on coal based IGCC plants in the United States (US) and Europe, and also from the
IGCC plants based on petroleum residuals that are in service worldwide.
The most significant events pertinent to gasification technology in 2005-6 have been:
• The US Energy Policy Act of 2005 signed into law by President Bush in August 2005. This
bill offered several incentives to encourage the commercial deployment of IGCC and other
advanced coal generating technologies and for the use of coal gasification in other industries.
Projects representing a total potential investment of $58B requesting $5B of credits
($1.65B authorized) submitted Gasification and Advanced Combustion Applications under
Title XIII Investment Tax Credits. Project selection by DOE is scheduled for December1,
2006.
• Three commercial teams: GE Energy/Bechtel, ConocoPhillips/Fluor/Siemens, and
Shell/Uhde/Black & Veatch are offering reference turnkey IGCC plant designs to the US
power industry with price, schedule, performance, and emission guarantees.
• Siemens acquired the former FutureEnergy/GSP gasification technology and announced
plans for its scale-up to a size relevant for IGCC applications. Siemens thereby enters the
IGCC marketplace with its own potential offering.
• Construction is proceeding on the 250 MW enriched air blown IGCC plant in Japan
supported by the Clean Coal Power R&D Co. Start up is scheduled for late 2007. With this
project and technology, MHI also enters the IGCC market place.
• American Electric Power (AEP) and Duke Energy are proceeding with Front End
Engineering Design (FEED) of 600 MW IGCC projects with GE/Bechtel.
• IGCC project FEED packages are also proceeding based on the ConocoPhillips (COP) E-Gas
technology (Rentech/Royster Clark, Mesaba).
• British Petroleum (BP) and Edison Mission announce plans for a 500 MW IGCC at the
Carson, California refinery gasifying petroleum coke with capture of the CO for Enhanced
2
v

Oil Recovery (EOR) and firing hydrogen in the gas turbine. In a separate announcement, BP
and GE revealed plans for 10-15 hydrogen based power plants (including the Carson and
Peterhead, Scotland projects).
• In Europe, Nuon announced a new 1000 MW IGCC project in the Netherlands based on the
Shell technology and RWE announced plans for a 450 MW IGCC that would include CO
2
capture for sequestration.
• The 285 MW air blown IGCC project in Orlando, Florida, based on the KBR transport
gasifier, is proceeding with a FEED and is well advanced with permitting.
• The new power industry initiative “CoalFleet for Tomorrow” launched in November 2004 is
managed by EPRI and is aimed at the early deployment of advanced coal technologies
designed for potential capture of CO. The primary emphasis was on IGCC initially in 2005
2
followed by Ultra Supercritical Pulverized Coal (USC PC) in 2006 and Supercritical
Circulating Fluid Bed (CFB) in 2007. Currently over 50 organizations are participating in
this program including companies representing the majority of US coal plants. The AEP,
Duke, and Southern IGCC projects are [participating as “Early Deployment Projects”
(EDP’s).
• The DOE FutureGen initiative announced in early 2003 is a planned IGCC project that will
incorporate CO capture and sequestration and hydrogen co-production. An industrial
2
consortium, the FutureGen Alliance, led by AEP and the Southern Company, was formed in
September 2005 to manage the project. In response to an RFP, potential sites have been
selected in Illinois and Texas. Following an evaluation of many potential configurations, four
have been down-selected for more detailed evaluation by Worley Parsons as the engineering
contractor for the FutureGen Alliance.
• Continued commercial deployment of large coal gasification projects in China particularly
for ammonia and other chemical synthesis.
• Continued commercial deployment of IGCC and gasification plants worldwide based on the
use of petroleum and tar sands residuals such as petroleum coke, asphalt, vacuum, and
visbreaker bottoms.
Chapter 1of this technical status report covers the current IGCC designs being offered, reviews
the status of gasification technologies and potential improvements, and the lessons learned from
the commercial operating experience at IGCC plants. In 2005, GE and Siemens introduced the 7
FB and STG6-5000F gas turbines, respectively, for IGCC application. Both machines are rated at
232 MW and at higher firing temperatures, higher pressure ratios and improved efficiencies. The
current reference designs from the three IGCC teams are all based on these newer gas turbines.
These designs do not include spare gasifiers. Spares are offered as an option. It has been
suggested that many power companies have lower power demands in the Spring and Fall and
that plant maintenance could be scheduled for those periods so that the spare may not be
required.
Chapter 2 reviews the status of several other gasification technologies that may find applications
in specific markets and/or are currently in earlier phases of development.
vi

The fundamentals of gasification technology and considerations for technology selection are
addressed in Chapter 3. The operating experience and the lessons learned at some specific
existing coal-based IGCC and gasification plants are reviewed in some detail in Chapter 4.
The use of gasification technology for biomass and wastes is discussed in Chapter 5. One
application of interest is their use as partial feedstock to larger coal or petroleum coke based
IGCC plants. Some tests runs have been conducted at the coal IGCC plants. Biomass/waste
atmospheric gasifiers are also used adjacent to existing PC boilers supplying a raw gas to the
boiler furnace. Gasification is also being developed for use in the Forest Products industry. A
new development program has been initiated in Europe aimed at the production of Fischer-
Tropsch transportation fuels via biomass gasification.
A 6th Chapter covers the additional experience being gained from commercial IGCC plants
worldwide based on petroleum residuals such as petroleum coke and various heavy oils. These
plants are usually located adjacent to refineries or petrochemical centers where they typically
supply power, steam and hydrogen to the refinery and also sell power to the grid. Several new
plants are in construction and are entering service.
Gasification is also a key technology for the future use of coal to co-produce power and
transportation fuels including hydrogen as envisaged in the EPRI and US DOE Roadmaps and
the DOE FutureGen initiative. A new Chapter 7 has therefore added to this year’s Annual report
on the emerging field of Coal–to–Liquids (CTL) using Fischer-Tropsch technology. With the
high prices for crude oil and natural gas experienced in 2004-5 there has been an upsurge of
interest in a possible domestic US program aimed at the production of Fischer Tropsch liquid
transportation fuels from the gasification of domestic coal. The Department of Defense (DOD)
has shown interest in pursuing this strategically attractive option of producing consistent high
quality synthetic fuels from secure US domestic resources.
The final Chapter addresses economics and issues pertinent to the commercial deployment of
IGCC technology e.g. the Energy Policy Act and potential CO regulations.
2

1
IGCC COMMERCIAL OFFERINGS AND NEW
PRODUCTS IN DEVELOPMENT
The commercial deployment of IGCC technology has been inhibited in the past because of the
absence of a single point responsibility for the supply of the IGCC technology from coal to
kilowatts. In response, three commercial teams were formed in 2004-2005 to offer reference
turnkey IGCC plant designs to the US power industry with price, schedule, performance and
emission guarantees. The three teams are:
• GE Energy/Bechtel
• ConocoPhillips/Fluor/Siemens
• Shell/Uhde/Black & Veatch
During 2006, three other potential teams have emerged that may soon be in a position to also
offer IGCC coal to kilowatts.
• Siemens with their acquisition of the FutureEnergy gasification technology could become a
potential offer of IGCC technology.
• KBR/Southern Co is considering an IGCC Alliance based on the KBR Transport gasification
technology as to be used at the 285 MW IGCC project in Orlando, Florida.
• The 250 MW enriched air blown IGCC sponsored by the Clean Coal Power R&D Co. is due
to start up in late 2007. Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (MHI), the supplier of both the gasifier
and gas turbine for this project has been actively promoting their technology.
However, these three technologies all need a successful demonstration of their gasification
technologies at a commercial size before they can really be considered as offering IGCC plants
with the necessary guarantees. Accordingly, these technologies will be covered in Chapter 2.
In the 2001-2004 period, many design studies were conducted based on the use of two GE 7 FA
gas turbines each of which were rated at 197 MW in IGCC service at iso conditions. These 7 FA
gas turbines were slightly upgraded versions of the 7 FA gas turbines used in the Tampa and
Wabash IGCC plants that entered into operation in 1995 and 1996 respectively. The current
offerings, however, are based on the use of larger higher firing temperature gas turbines that
have not yet been used in the IGCC application. GE proposes the use of the 7 FB gas turbines
rated at 232 MW in IGCC service. Siemens is offering the STG6-5000F, which is also rated at
232 MW in IGCC service.
1-1

In this section, each of the three major gasification technologies represented by these three teams
will be reviewed with regard to:
• Technical Process Description
• History – Pilot, Demonstration and Commercial Plants
• Causes of Outage at IGCC Demonstration Plants
• Potential Improvements to the Technology
• IGCC Reference Plant Design
• New Commercial Projects in Development
GE Energy (formerly Texaco) Coal Gasification Process
GE Technical Process Description
Throughout the commercial development of the GE Energy (formerly Texaco) gasification
technology three separate configurations have been considered.
In all three designs, coal is mixed with water and pulverized in a wet grinding mill (typically a
rod mill), producing a pumpable coal/water slurry containing 50-70 weight % of dry solids
depending on the inherent moisture and quality of the feed coal. High pressure charge pumps
transfer the coal/water slurry to the gasifier fuel injector. 95% purity oxygen from the Air
Separation Unit (ASU) is compressed to a pressure sufficient for introduction into the gasifier
fuel injector.
The coal/water slurry and oxygen are fed to the single stage entrained flow gasifier, which is a
downflow, open refractory lined chamber. In the gasifier, the coal is converted at high pressure
35-86 barg (500-1250 psig) and temperature 1315-1480°C (2400 – 2700 °F), to yield a raw
syngas composed primarily of hydrogen, carbon monoxide, carbon dioxide and water. This raw
syngas also contains molten slag and some unconverted char. Upon exiting the gasifier the raw
syngas is immediately cooled and/or quenched via one of the three heat recovery schemes:
• Radiant plus Convective Design (R+C)
• Radiant only Design (R)
• Total Quench Design (Q)
Radiant plus Convective Design (R+C)
In this design the gasifier is located vertically above a radiant heat transfer water tube exchanger
and the raw syngas flows downwards through the radiant syngas cooler (SGC). High pressure
steam is generated in tubes built into the heat transfer surface at the perimeter of the cylindrical
gas flow zone. Additional heat transfer surface is provided by radial platens (wing walls) from
the water walls at the perimeter of the vessel. Soot blowers can be provided to remove any
1-2

accumulations on the tube water walls however at the Tampa plant the fouling has been minor
and the soot blowers are hardly used. At the bottom of the radiant cooler slag drops into a water
quench pool prior to removal from the quench vessel via a system of lock hoppers for subsequent
disposal or use. It is usually considered prudent to provide a slag crusher below the discharge
nozzle to break up any large slag pieces so that they do not block the lock hopper operation.
The radiant coolers at the Cool Water and Tampa IGCC plants are large heavy vessels. The
dimensions of the Radiant cooler at Tampa are height 40m (131.2 ft); outside diameter 5.24m
(17.2 ft) and its weight is 650 tons. The overall height of the structure is 77.4m (254 ft).
However, the fouling experienced at Tampa has been much less than expected and therefore new
designs can reduce the height and weight of the radiant vessel with attendant cost savings. This
vessel is the subject of detailed design studies by the GE team.
Gas leaves the radiant cooler at 650-760°C (1200-1400°F) and flows through a transfer duct to
the convective syngas cooler where additional high pressure steam is generated. The raw gas
leaves the convective syngas cooler at 315-425°C (600-800°F) and flows to the gas scrubbing
unit for removal of remaining particulate matter and chlorides. Several different designs of
convective coolers have been installed at various locations including water tube and fire tube
designs and horizontal, vertical upflow and downflow configurations. The Cool Water project
had an upflow duct followed by downflow over boiler tube banks, however fouling was a
persistent problem particularly at the 90° turn from horizontal flow to upflow. At Tampa, the
syngas flow from the base of the radiant cooler is split into two parallel horizontal fire tube
convective exchangers. Fouling of these convective exchangers has also been a significant cause
of outage at Tampa. If convective coolers are to be included in future IGCC designs then the
new design must avoid those features that have led to fouling problems at previously built plants.
It is probable that the split flow design will not be used in the new GE design. Generally, fire
tube designs are lower cost if the fouling can be avoided.
The original split flow design used at the Tampa plant also included two gas/gas exchangers
downstream of each of the convective coolers. One was designed to reheat the clean syngas
before going to the gas turbine. The other was to reheat the nitrogen from the ASU before going
on to the gas turbine. However, fouling of these exchangers was a persistent problem in the
early years of operation at Tampa and they have been removed from service. It is therefore
unlikely that such gas/gas exchangers would be included in new IGCC designs.
Radiant only Design (R)
In this design, coal is gasified and the hot raw syngas flows downwards into the same radiant
cooler described above and is cooled by generating high pressure steam. However, in this design
at the bottom of the radiant cooler both the raw gas and the slag are quenched in water and
cooled. Slag and any unconverted char are removed from the bottom of the quench chamber via
a lock hopper system as previously described. The water-saturated raw syngas then flows to the
gas scrubbing unit for further removal of particulate matter and chlorides.
1-3

Total Quench Design (Q)
As in the previous designs coal is gasified at high temperature and pressure producing a raw
syngas containing entrained molten slag and some unconverted char. In this configuration the
high temperature radiant and convective syngas coolers are eliminated. Instead, the hot raw
syngas flows down into a water quench chamber and cooled to a temperature of 205-260°C (400-
500°F) , depending on the pressure, and flows on to the gas scrubbing unit.
Slag Handling
Slag from the lock hoppers is fed to a slag sump. A screen classifier separates the coarse slag
from the finer material. The coarse slag is typically of low carbon content and can often be sold
for cement manufacture, roof tiles or road fill. The fine slag contains some unconverted carbon
and is discharged to a solids storage tank for recycle back to the coal grinding rod mills. With
petroleum coke the carbon conversion per pass is <90% and therefore a considerable amount of
recycle is usually required.
Gas Scrubbing
In the gas scrubbing section (sometimes called the carbon scrubber), the cooled and/or quenched
raw syngas is washed with water to remove any fine particles of slag or unconverted char that
were not collected in the previous cooling/quenching operation. Hydrogen chloride arising from
the chloride content of the feed coal is also removed in this gas scrubbing unit.
The fly ash waters from the radiant cooler or quench sump, lock hoppers, and gas scrubber go to
a clarifier or high pressure solids settler where the solids and water are separated. In some
plants, a vacuum filter has also been used for this separation. The solids typically have some
unconverted carbon and can be recycled to the slurry preparation rod mills. The recovered
“grey” water is stored and recycled. A blow down stream is removed to keep the chloride
content of the circulating water below 1500 ppmw. This blowdown stream is flashed and steam
stripped and then flows on to waste water treatment. If the plant is to be designed for zero liquid
discharge then the treated water may be vacuum evaporated to produce a salt cake for disposal.
The flash overhead and the stripper overhead gases are fed to the Claus plant where the ammonia
is destroyed and the sulfur in the hydrogen sulfide is recovered.
Low Temperature Heat Recovery, COS Hydrolysis and Mercury Capture
The scrubbed raw syngas is further cooled in the low-temperature gas cooling section. Nearly all
the energy removed from the raw syngas is utilized to generate medium or low-pressure steam,
to heat feed water, or as a source of heat for clean syngas saturation to control gas turbine NO
x
formation and provide additional mass flow to the gas turbine. Water is condensed out of the
raw syngas as it is further cooled. This condensate, containing ammonia and hydrogen sulfide, is
flashed and is fed to a sour water stripper (SWS) for recovery of the water. The flash overhead
and the SWS overhead gases are fed to the Claus plant where the ammonia is destroyed and the
sulfur in the hydrogen sulfide is recovered.
1-4

A COS hydrolysis fixed bed reactor is included at the appropriate temperature typically
150-175°C (300-350°F) and a feed /effluent heat exchanger or steam preheater is included prior
to the COS reactor to ensure that the syngas is above the dew point.(water can damage the
catalyst).
When the syngas has been cooled to about 40°C (104°F) it is passed through a fixed bed of pre-
sulfided activated carbon to capture the mercury and some other trace metals.
The magnitude of the energy recovered in this section depends on the extent of heat recovery in
the high temperature raw syngas cooling sections. The Radiant plus Convective Design will
have much less low-temperature heat recovery than will the Total Quench Design.
Figure 1-1
Schematic Diagram of the GE (Texaco) Gasification Process – Quench Mode
Source: GE (Texaco)
Comparison of the Three Design Configurations
The three design configurations for the gasification and high temperature gas cooling
significantly influence the overall heat integration of the plant. The difference in the high
pressure steam generation between the three designs is readily apparent. As less heat is
recovered in high temperature gas cooling the stream of raw gas to scrubbing contains more
energy which must be recovered usefully in some service other than high pressure steam
generation.
1-5

Figure 1-2
Schematic Diagram of the GE (Texaco) Gasification Process – Heat Recovery Mode (with
Radiant and Convective Syngas Coolers) – (Not to Scale)
Source: GE (Texaco)
The IGCC net efficiency is highest for the R+C configuration followed by the R design and with
the Q design having the lowest efficiency. However, the radiant and convective syngas coolers
are not inexpensive and the capital cost of the R+C and R designs are higher than the Q design in
$/kW when gasifiers of the same size are used in the comparison.. When the Cost of Electricity
(COE) is calculated at the same Capacity Factor (CF) the lower capital cost of the Q design can
result in the lowest COE of the three designs at U.S delivered coal costs in the 1-1.50$/MBtu
range. However, because of its higher heat rate the Q design may not be dispatched at the same
CF as the higher capital cost designs.
IGCC Design for CO Capture
2
Several studies have been conducted with the high pressure 69 barg (1000 psig) GE Quench
design adding the Shift reactor and subsequent CO capture using the Selexol physical absorption
2
process. The Shift reaction can be conducted on the raw syngas before sulfur removal (so-called
Sour Shift). This process is widely used commercially. The Sour Shift also conducts the
hydrolysis of the COS present in the raw syngas. The COE from these Quench IGCC design
studies has generally been evaluated to be the lowest of any of the IGCC designs considered.
Indeed for bituminous coals it has been evaluated to be the lowest COE of any coal technology
incorporating CO capture. The Quench design provides the moisture needed for the Shift
2
reaction in the least cost manner without raising external steam, since it avoids the use of the
expensive syngas coolers. The high pressure assists in reducing the cost of the Selexol process.
1-6

In addition, some of the CO can be recovered by flashing the rich Selexol solvent at pressure
2
thereby reducing the CO compression costs.
2
The R design with and without capture was studied in the DOE NETL report using Illinois #6
coal with a gasifier pressure of 55 Barg (800 psig) the 2x GE 7 FB gas turbines. These
preliminary results indicate that the additional costs of adding capture, the percentage loss of
power output and the increase in heat rate are similar to the previous results with the Q design.
The additional costs and auxiliary power consumption for CO capture are significantly greater
2
for other IGCC designs using lower pressures of 31-41 barg (450-600 psig) than for the 69 barg
(1000 psig) GE Quench IGCC designs.
Jacobs Consultancy has recommended the use of a Desaturator (where the syngas is directly
contact with incoming cold water prior to the Selexol process). This provides more hot water to
the Quench and is claimed to produce a quenched raw syngas that can generate more and better
quality medium pressure steam thereby improving the efficiency. There appears to be some
merit in this scheme however confirmative data is needed from existing high pressure Quench
gasifiers with regard to the amount of moisture that is actually evaporated at these high
pressures. So far, GE has not chosen to address Jacob’s claimed efficiency improvement.
History of GE (Texaco) Coal Gasification Process – Pilot, Demonstration and
Commercial Plants
The Texaco (now GE) process was first developed after the Second World War at the Texaco
laboratories and pilot plants at Montebello, CA under the direction of Dubois Eastman. A
reactor was built to partially oxidize natural gas to syngas in south Texas in the late 1940’s for
Fischer-Tropsch synthesis. The same essential gasifier, a refractory lined downflow reactor, was
also adapted for gasification of heavy oil fractions and for solids, such as coal or petroleum coke,
when fed as pumpable slurry in water (typically 60-65% dry solids). A 100 ton/day coal
gasification unit was built for the Olin operated ordnance plant in West Virginia in 1956-8.
During the 50’s, 60’s and 70’s, Texaco licensed many gasification units with heavy oil
feedstocks. In 1978, a 165 ton/day coal gasifier equipped with radiant and convective syngas
coolers was built at the Ruhrchemie complex in Oberhausen, Germany where the syngas was
used for oxo alcohols production. A larger unit of ~ 800 tons/day with scaled up radiant and
convective coolers was also built at Oberhausen in the mid 1980’s. A 180 mt/d (200 st/d) coal
gasifier of the Quench design was built for TVA at their Muscle Shoals, Alabama location in
1979 for Ammonia production. Two larger 1000 ton/day gasifiers of the Quench design were
built for Eastman Chemical and since 1983 have been producing syngas for methanol, methyl
acetate and acetic anhydride production. In 1984, the first IGCC demonstration project was built
at the Southern California Edison Cool Water station near Daggett, California and operated from
1984 to 1989 producing 100MW net output using a GE 7 E gas turbine. The US Synthetic Fuels
Corporation provided some financial support for the Cool Water project during its operation.
The plant had a 1000 ton/day gasifier with radiant and convective syngas coolers and a spare
Quench gasifier. Ube Ammonia in Ube Japan started operating 4x 500 mt/d coal/petroleum coke
Quench gasifiers for Ammonia production in 1985 that continue in use today. A larger 250 MW
IGCC plant was built under the DOE Clean Coal Demonstration program for Tampa Electric
using a GE 7 FA gas turbine. This plant is essentially a scale-up of the Cool Water project. It
1-7

started operations in 1996 and continues today. The Cool Water gasifiers were later moved to
Coffeyville, KS and are now used for the gasification of petroleum coke and ammonia
manufacture. Two 1000 ton/day quench gasifiers were built at the refinery at Delaware City,
Delaware (now owned by Valero) to run on petroleum coke. The syngas is supplied to two GE 6
FA gas turbines that generate power and steam for the refinery.
GE/Texaco has also supplied several coal gasifiers to China – mostly for retrofit of Ammonia
plants and for Methanol production as shown in Table 1-1. All Ammonia plants will include
CO removal from the syngas and in some cases the CO is reacted with ammonia to produce
2 2
Urea. The first license in china was for the Lunan project that started up in 1993. GE has sold
29 gasification licenses in China of which 19 are for solid feedstocks. The gasifier vessels have
usually been made in China. The most recent start ups have been the Sinopec Jinling, Shenmu
and Sinopec Nanjing projects. The last 8 projects shown in Table 1-1 are taken from a
presentation made by GE at the 2006 Gasification Technologies Conference held in Washington,
DC. However, GE did not include any information on the gasifier sizes or the total feed capacity
of these 8 plants.

1-8

Many other Texaco Quench type gasifiers using heavy oil have also been built for synthesis and
IGCC service. These plants are described in more detail in Section 5.
Causes of Outage at GE/Texaco IGCC Demonstration Plants
The major causes of outage at the Tampa 250 MW IGCC plant are shown in Table 1-2.
The coal water slurry feed pumps at Tampa have generally been very reliable. However,
decisions were made during design that eliminated some features that would have improved
availability. There is therefore no spare slurry feed pump and no oxygen storage at the Tampa
plant. Pump failures have caused some outages but these have generally been short enough that
a hot restart of the gasifier could be achieved.
The fuel injector tip life for the coal water slurry fed gasifiers (60-90 days) is much lower than
for the dry coal fed gasifiers (> 1 year). The failure is usually due to stress corrosion cracking at
the injector tip (where especially hardened metals are used). The injector life (and also the
refractory life) has improved as the plant matures and less temperature cycling is experienced.
The plant has developed a hot restart technique for recovery after minor trips with full injector
replacement during the hot restart. This technique has markedly reduced the forced outages due
to injector tip failures and other minor outages.
Refractory life for the vertical hot face in the brick lined coal water slurry fed gasifier at Tampa
was historically ~2 years but by 2006 had increased to ~3 years. The bottom neck of the gasifier
is a high wear area. Eastman utilizes a removable cone for the bottom neck area that requires
only 20 hours for change out (plus the time for cool down and reheat). Repairs to the gasifier top
and bottom refractory faces take 12-15 days but to replace the entire hot face refractory including
the vertical section can take 30 –35 days to cool down, remove, replace and warm up. This
obviously constitutes a major cause of outage and unless new longer lasting refractories can be
developed then a spare gasifier is probably necessary to achieve an equivalent availability >90%.
At Eastman Chemical, a spare Texaco gasifier is kept at temperature and they have developed
changeover techniques so that syngas flow is not interrupted. The Tampa plant was designed as
a demonstration unit and has no spare gasifier. When considering the future availability of 600
MW commercial IGCC plants with two trains of gasification and gas turbines the issue of
sparing is of great importance. However, GE has suggested that for companies and locations that
experience lower power demand in Spring and Fall a spare gasifier may not be justified and the
80% availability that should be achievable without a spare should be sufficient.
The circulating water from the slag quench chamber contains sharp fine solids so that erosion is a
constant problem. The use of long radius bends where possible is recommended to promote a
smoother consistent continuous flow and to avoid recirculation pockets. The slag water circuit is
often acidic and needs alkali addition. However, the problem is also compounded by difficulties
encountered in monitoring the pH due to plugging or corrosion of the probes. It is often
necessary to add either acid or alkali as needed to keep the pH in the range that avoids corrosion
and prevents precipitation.
1-9

The carbon scrubber tower has its own circuit and in many cases ammonia is captured to some
extent. In this latter case, pH control may need some acid to avoid calcium deposits in the
circulating lines. In some cases, a change has been made to 316 type or Duplex stainless steel
however in this case the chloride content of the circulating water must be kept <100 ppmw and
as a consequence the blow down stream and waste treatment section must handle a much larger
volume. With low carbon steel the chloride content can be allowed to increase to ~1500 ppmw
If a slag blockage does occur, the outage can last 8-10 days in order to cool down and mine out
the blocked area. It has nearly always been associated with a change in feed coal properties that
unexpectedly changed the slag viscosity. The so-called T250 temperature (the temperature at
which the slag viscosity is 250 centipoises) is typically used to give an indication of the
temperature at which the gasifier should be run to avoid slag blockage problems. It is also used
in estimating an appropriate flux requirement with high ash fusion temperature coals. This
temperature is monitored in most cases but the slag viscosity measurement is time consuming
(~6-8 hours) and there is always a question about whether the sample taken was truly
representative. The problem of variation in ash fusion properties is more likely to arise with
blended coals and particularly any coals or feeds such as petroleum coke that need flux additions.
At Tampa, there were some incidents in 1999 when blends of Eastern and Western coals
produced sudden changes in the wall slag coverage that led to sudden surges in slag flow from
the gasifier.
If a slag crusher is not included (or if space for one is not allowed for) in the original design it is
very difficult to add one later. The inclusion of a slag crusher in the slag discharge line is
recommended for all future IGCC plants particularly if load following and the use of a variety of
coals and coal blends is contemplated.
1-10

The Tampa radiant syngas cooler was designed with soot blowing capability however this vessel
has shown very little fouling and the soot blowers are rarely used. The reduced fouling in this
vessel has led to an outlet temperature about 166-222°C (300-400°F) lower than design. New
plants with a radiant cooler design will therefore have the choice of either reducing the size and
height of the vessel or having the lower outlet temperature and more heat recovery. The fouling
and corrosion of the gas/gas exchangers was an early problem at Tampa. These horizontal fire
tube exchangers heated the clean syngas and nitrogen against the raw syngas leaving the
convective syngas cooler thereby putting more useful energy into the gas turbine (Brayton
1-11

Cycle). Persistent fouling led to their removal from service in 1997 with a consequent decrease
in net plant efficiency.
It appears that the fouling is somewhat coal dependent and the deposits are often enriched in
alkali metals and iron. A mixture of Potassium Alumino-silicate and Pyrrhotite (FeS) appears to
produce a relatively low melting eutectic.
After the removal of the gas/gas exchangers from service at Tampa, fouling of the horizontal fire
tube convective syngas coolers became a significant cause of outage. The formation of deposits
was often initiated during startup that would continue to build and sometimes become detached
and produce blockage of the inlet tube sheet. Modifications to start up procedures combined
with modifications to the inlet gas flow path (aided by the use of the Computational Fluid
Dynamic (CFD) modeling program Fluent) improved the situation but this fouling has continued
as a significant cause of outage. It has been observed that the fouling rapidly increases above
690°C (1275°F) and so some additional heat transfer area has recently been added to the Radiant
cooler platens to reduce the outlet temperature below that temperature.
Depending on the value of efficiency new designs have the option of either eliminating the
convective coolers and using a larger Radiant cooler, using the Tampa modified convective
cooler design, perhaps adopting a downflow firetube design, or a more expensive water tube
design. Another alternative is to adopt the Texaco Quench design (with neither radiant nor
convective coolers) – a lower cost but lower efficiency option. GE Energy has chosen the
radiant only configuration for their reference plant design.
Exposure to moisture during downtime tends to concentrate chlorides and other corrosion
products at the scale metal interface that can cause subsequent spalling of the chromium oxide
rich scales. This spalling may be the cause of accelerated corrosion rather than aqueous
corrosion during downtime alone. The presence of sulfides in the scales is suspected as being
the cause of susceptibility to downtime dew point corrosion. Therefore, measures (e.g. purging
with heated nitrogen) need to be taken to avoid the conditions that could lead to downtime
corrosion.
Potential Improvements to the GE Technology
The GE /Texaco technology is well suited to bituminous coals and petroleum coke for which
water slurry loadings of 60-65 weight % dry solids can be achieved. However, with coals of
high moisture content or high ash content the achievable energy density of the slurry is reduced
leading to higher oxygen consumption per unit of product and reduced gasifier efficiency. Since
it is anticipated that the majority of new US coal plants are likely to use lower rank Western
coals such as Powder River Basin (PRB) and lignite there is a great incentive to improve the
performance of this technology for these low rank coals. This can most likely be affected by
changing the method of feeding the gasifier in some manner that avoids the higher oxygen use
and improves gasifier efficiency. Several schemes have been suggested (Holt 2002) including
flash drying of the slurry, slurrying the coal in liquid CO , use of a dry coal feeder (e.g. the
2
Stamet pump) etc. GE has indicated that they are working on the problem with Basin Electric
Power Cooperative and envisage the use of “enhanced slurry hardware” just prior to injection.
1-12

The largest source of outage for GE/Texaco is the 25-30 days necessary for periodic refractory
replacement every 2-3 years. New longer lasting refractories are therefore obviously of great
importance, and test sections of a new refractory based on work conducted at the DOE
laboratories in Albany, Oregon have been installed at Eastman and Tampa electric.
The height of the gasification structure is obviously a major cost. The gasifier sits atop the
radiant cooler which in turn is located above the water quench chamber and the lock hoppers.
The height of the structure at Tampa is 77.3 m (253.5 ft). The possibility of reducing the height
of the radiant vessel has already been mentioned. Another significant cost saving and likely
operational improvement would result from replacement of the lock hoppers with a continuous
slag removal system (as used with E-Gas).
The circulating black and grey water circuits required a great deal of maintenance attention.
Another possible improvement would be to use a hot (or warm gas filter) for the particulate
removal rather than the carbon scrubber. This would serve to keep much of the particulate
matter out of the water circuit with attendant O&M savings.
The potential improvements to the overall GE technology are shown in Table 1-3.

GE Gasifier Sizes
Larger gasifiers would also produce significant economic improvements in some applications in
the current marketplace. Furthermore, future trends in the marketplace, as listed below, all point
the need for larger gasifiers to match the fuel requirements of the downstream equipment in a
rational multi-train manner:
• The larger more efficient gas turbines such as the GE H and Siemens 6000 G, when adapted
to the IGCC application will have higher fuel requirements,
• The 50 Hz IGCC market requires gasifiers sized to meet the fuel needs of gas turbines which
are ~1.4 times those needed for 60 Hz turbines,
• For the Coal-to-Liquids (CTL) technology using Fischer Tropsch (F-T) synthesis economy of
scale is very important. Larger gasifiers able to match the syngas requirements of the large
F-T reactors (10,000-20,000 barrels/day) will require the gasification of 6000-12000 tons/day
of a typical bituminous coal.
The sizes of some representative GE gasifiers are shown in Table 1-4. The fuel requirement of a
GE 7 FB gas turbine is ~ 2050 GJ/Hr (1950 MBtu/hr) LHV and in the GE standard IGCC design
this is supplied by an 1800 ft3 gasifier at ~600 psig.

However, for the Carson IGCC project with BP/Edison Mission is based on GE Q gasifiers and
the current design has three 900 ft3 gasifiers supplying two 7 FB gasifiers. It is not clear why
three gasifiers are required. Based on the Eastman experience two 900 ft3 1000 psig Q gasifiers
should be able to supply 4000 MBtu/hr LHV of syngas – enough for the 2x 7 FB gas turbines.
Based on the Negishi experience (admittedly based on Asphalt), they should be able to supply
even more than this amount.
It is true that in a slurry fed reactor, some of the reactor volume is taken up with evaporation of
the slurry water. However, two 1200 ft3 Q gasifiers should be able to supply the needs of the
2x7 FB gas turbines.
It has however been noted that the carbon conversion per pass for petroleum coke (to be used at
Carson) is only ~ 90% and it would seem that the reactor for such feedstocks would benefit from
more residence time. This could be accomplished by lengthening the reactor at a given diameter
(a higher L/D ratio) to increase conversion on scale-up.
Many of the new markets for gasification will have the need for downstream shifting of the raw
syngas. The Q design is the least cost method of providing moisture to the syngas for the shift
reaction. The high pressure Q gasifiers as used at Eastman and Negishi are advantageous for
capture, synthesis and high throughput. Larger gasifiers of 34-51 m3 (1200 or 1800 ft3 )
(Tampa size) at 1000 psig could supply enough syngas for the future 60 Hz and 50 Hz gas
turbines and reduce the number of gasifiers needed to match the syngas requirements of large
10,000-20,000 bpd F-T reactors and thereby provide considerable economies of scale.
In Europe, most IGCC projects seem to be considering the incorporation of capture into the base
design rather than adding capture at some later time. In either of these applications the Jacobs
GEM configuration should be considered. It incorporates shift in the design and uses a
1-15

Desaturator to provide higher temperature water to the quench. This gives a better efficiency and
the plant is more capture ready since the ASU, gasification and gas clean up are already sized so
that the gas turbines can be fully loaded when capture is added.
GE Energy/Bechtel IGCC Reference Plant Design for Power Industry
The GE Energy reference plant for the power industry is based on the use of a range of eastern
US bituminous coals with 0.74% to 4% sulfur, up to 14% ash and up to 2500 ppmw of chlorides.
Coal is fed via high pressure feed pumps as a coal in water slurry at 62-66% (dry coal basis)
concentration. The gasification section comprises two gasification trains with GE (formerly
Texaco) gasifiers located on top of radiant syngas coolers that raise high pressure steam for
integration into the steam cycle. Slag is removed from a water bath located at the base of the
radiant cooler via a system of lock hoppers. The syngas leaving the radiant cooler is water
scrubbed, cooled and passed through COS hydrolysis reactors and activated carbon beds for
Mercury removal. The syngas is fed to a physical solvent (e.g. Selexol) acid gas removal (AGR)
process for removal of the sulfur species (HS and trace COS) to low levels such that an SCR can
2
be added if necessary) and the clean syngas is preheated for feed to the gas turbines. The power
block has two 7 FB gas turbines and a single steam turbine. Air is extracted from the gas turbine
compressors to supply about 40% of the air needed for the ASU at ISO conditions. However, it
appears that the 7 FB may be limited in the amount of air that can be extracted at high ambient
temperature conditions. If such conditions typically represent a substantial percentage of the
total demand and/or are likely to occur at periods of maximum demand, then it may be necessary
to design the ASU main air compressor for more air flow so that the plant can maintain output at
the higher ambient temperatures when incremental power may be very valuable.
The configuration of the GE reference IGCC pant can be summarized as:
• Slurry Preparation (2) and Pumping (2)
• Air Separation Units (2) 40% of Air supplied from CT compressor
• Gasification, Radiant Cooler (R), Water Scrubbing, Coarse slag handling (2 )
• Low temperature Gas Cooling (2)
• COS Hydrolysis, Mercury Removal, Acid Gas Removal (Selexol?) (2)
• Claus plant (2) TGTU(1) or Hydrogenation and Recycle to Selexol
• Combined Cycle (2 GE 7 FB, 2 HRSG, 1 ST)
• Plot space left for addition of CO capture
2
• For CO capture add Shift(2) CO removal column to Selexol (2), CO drying and
2 2 2
compression (1)
There is no spare gasification train in the reference design but a spare is offered as an option. GE
has suggested that many power companies could schedule maintenance in the spring and fall
when power demand is lower and could thereby avoid the need for a spare gasifier. GE also
believes that improvements in refractory life can significantly extend the period when
replacement is required. However, the previous historic experience suggests that to meet an
1-16

overall annual IGCC equivalent availability of > 90% that a spare gasification train would be
required.
Other options include coal / petroleum coke blending, higher sulfur and chloride coals, zero
liquid discharge, the addition of Selective Catalytic Reduction (SCR) for further NO reduction
x
into the HRSG.
A performance estimate for the Reference plant, at ISO conditions, and zero feet elevation, on an
Illinois basin coal has been given as:
Coal Feed 4873 mt/d (5372 st/d) dry
Oxygen 4445 mt/d (4894 st/d) pure
Plant Power Output MW
Gas Turbines 464
Steam Turbine 301
Total Gross 65
Aux Power 135
Net Power 630
Heat Rate 9250 kJ/kWh (8845 Btu/kWh) HHV
An efficiency range has been quoted as 38.5 - 40.0 % HHV – presumably spanning the range
from typical Illinois basin (e.g. Illinois #6) to Northern Appalachian (e.g. Pittsburgh #8) coals.
The GE target for the launch reference plants is that the IGCC capital cost should be within10%
of Supercritical plants. In their paper at the 2005 Gasification Technologies conference, the
supercritical greenfield plant was estimated at a Total Plant Cost (TPC) of 1460$/kW for a
central Ohio valley site so that the IGCC target price would be ~1606$/kW. GE also believes
that when emission credits for SO , NO and Mercury are included that the Cost-of-Electricity
x x
(COE) could be at parity with the supercritical PC COE.
However, the marked escalation in equipment, materials and commodity costs that started in late
2003 has continued unabated through mid 2006 and all cost estimates are markedly higher as
documented in Chapter 8, Economics.

AEP has contracted with GE/Bechtel for Front End Engineering Designs (FEED) at two sites,
one in Ohio and one in West Virginia. (A FEED is a level of engineering effort that enables firm
bids to be made at its conclusion. It can cost 10-15 Million $). The FEED for the Meigs County,
Ohio site is due to be completed in November of 2006, and the West Virginia site in December
2006. Air permits for both sites were filed in October 2006. AEP has applied to the Ohio PUC
for rate based recovery of the development costs as expended and this was approved by PUCO in
April 2006. However, this ruling has been challenged by an industrial consumer group and this
is expected to delay the project schedule. An application for a Certificate of Public Convenience
and Necessity (CPCN) was filed in Ohio in March 2006 and is expected to be granted in March
2007. A CPCN was filed in West Virginia in January 2006 and a ruling is expected in June

Worley Parsons to conduct a study of adding a third gasification train to the Mountaineer IGCC
for production of F-T diesel fuel.
Duke Energy (formerly Cinergy) completed an IGCC feasibility study with GE and Bechtel for
the 630 MW reference plant in the second quarter of 2005, and in February 2006, initiated a

Energy plans to install SCRs so long as the Reference Design supports their use. The project
was included in the Integrated Resource Plan (IRP) filed with the IURC in June 2006. In
September 2006, Duke Energy and Vectren filed a joint petition for a CPCN to construct the
Edwardsport IGCC plant. An air permit was filed in August 2006 and Duke Energy submitted
1-18

an application for tax credit under EPAct 2005. The project schedule calls for the initiation of

clean syngas would go to a shift reactor followed by CO removal and methanation of the clean
2

2
produce SNG allows an IGCC plant to keep its power price fixed over a range of dispatch levels.

BP and GE will jointly participate in the previously announced hydrogen power projects in

These two projects provide GE with orders for hydrogen-firing gas turbines in both 60 Hz and 50

The IGCC project at the Carson refinery, located about 15 miles south of Los Angeles, is to be

2

2
(EOR) in oil fields owned by Occidental Petroleum. The project also plans to supply 50-60

Shell Coal Gasification Process
Shell Technical Process Description
The Shell gasification process island comprises a high pressure ASU, a dry coal fed oxygen
blown Shell entrained flow gasifier, gas cooling by recycle gas to about 800°C followed by a
water tube syngas cooler (SGC) for saturated steam raising, a fly ash cyclone and a barrier filter
with ceramic candles at 250°C (482°F), water scrubbing, COS hydrolysis, HS removal by the
2
Sulfinol M or other solvents, gas saturation and water cleanup. The gasification block diagram is
shown in Figure 1-3.
The raw coal is dried and ground. The pulverized coal is then pressurized in two lock hopper
trains, each feeding two horizontally opposed fuel injectors. The gasification using 95% purity
oxygen takes place at about 1500-1600°C (2732-2912°F) and up to 40 barg (600 psig) pressure.
Depending on the reactivity of the coal a small part of the oxygen can be replaced by steam as a
gasification agent. The gasification temperature is chosen sufficiently high to melt the coal
ashes, that emerge as slag at the bottom of the gasifier. However, some of the ash is carried
overhead in the form of fly ash or fly slag. The amount of fly slag is mostly independent of the
coal ash content; however for a 10% ash coal ~20-25% is typically carried overhead. The fly
slag that is separated from the raw gas in a downstream cyclone and filter can often be sold.
However, if the slag has significant carbon content it can be recycled to the gasifier.
The mechanical design of the gasifier is a vertical cylindrical pressure vessel protected by a
membrane wall jacket of tubes in which medium pressure (50 barg) steam is generated providing
the cooling necessary to prevent overheating of the vessel. The fuel injectors extend into the
gasifier vessel through the membrane waterwall. The products of the gasification reactions are
primarily carbon monoxide and hydrogen (typically ~60% CO and 30% H). The hot syngas
2
flows upward out of the gasification zone with part of the molten ash entrained. The remainder
of the molten ash is deposited on the waterwall and flows downward. The waterwall serves as a
thermal barrier and provides a surface for molten ash to accumulate and flow downwards. The
slag layer is not entirely molten because the waterwall temperature is below the ash fusion
temperature. The solid layer of slag which coats the waterwall offers a protective insulating
layer augmenting the thin layer of refractory on the membrane wall. The molten ash on the
waterwall flows down the waterwall and across the bottom gasifier floor to the slag tap, which is
an opening in the center of the floor. The molten slag drips into a water bath in a chamber below
the gasifier. In the slag bath, the slag is cooled and solidifies into a granular frit. At the top
outlet of the gasifier the raw gas is quenched with recycled syngas of about 250°C (482°F)
temperature from 1600°C (2912°F) to about 800°C (1472°F), to ensure that the fly slag is
solidified prior to entering the syngas cooler heat recovery system. The gas quench section is
connected through a steam-cooled duct to the syngas cooler, which further cools the raw gas
from 800°C (1472°F) to 230°C (446°F).
1-20

The syngas cooler is a water tube boiler in which the gas flows from the top downward. The
vessel wall is protected from direct contact with the hot gas by wall heating surfaces. The main
heating surfaces consist of concentric cylinders of helical coiled tubes installed within the wall
panels. To facilitate mechanical cleaning (with rappers) of the cylinders, the tubes are welded
together to form a membrane. The steam conditions in the syngas cooler are 120 barg
(1740 psig) and 400°C (752°F). In principle, the steam conditions could reach 139 barg (2015
psig) 540°C (1004°F). However, it is usually determined that the superheating of the high-
pressure steam can be more economically accomplished in the Heat Recovery Steam Generator
(HRSG) at the gas turbine exhaust rather than in the more aggressively corrosive atmosphere at
the top of the syngas cooler. The saturated steam conditions in the syngas cooler can be as high
as 125barg (1800 psig) and 400°C (752°F), in which case a metal with a high chromium content
would be chosen for the high pressure cooling surfaces. However, in the US, coal prices are
generally lower than in most of the OECD countries and raising steam at a lower pressure of 50
barg may be more economic since a lower cost ferritic material can be selected for the medium
pressure surfaces.
The cooled syngas heating value typically contains ~80% of the heating value of the coal feed.
This is higher than that achievable with the coal/water slurry fed systems. The steam generated
typically represents 16-18% of the coal heating value. The steam make with the slurry fed
systems is a higher percentage of the coal feed.
After the syngas cooler, the syngas is further cooled in an economizer to about 250°C (482°F)
and the fly slag is separated from the cooled raw syngas by a cyclone and a filter vessel
containing porous ceramic filter elements. Periodically, the filter elements are back pulsed with
high pressure syngas or nitrogen to remove the particulate cake formed on the surface of the
elements. The ash is discharged by lock hopper systems and can be sold or recycled if necessary
via the coal milling and drying system.
After fly slag removal, a portion of the “solids free” (<5mg/Nm3) gas is compressed and
recycled as quench gas to the top of the gasifier. The balance of the gas is scrubbed with water
to remove halogens and other water soluble components, preheated by a feed/effluent exchanger
to ensure that the gas is above the dew point, and fed to fixed bed reactors to convert the traces
of hydrogen cyanide (HCN) and carbonyl sulfide (COS) by the following hydrolysis reactions:
HCN + H O = CO + NH
2 3
COS + H O = CO + HS
2 2 2
The gas is further cooled and subsequently desulfurized in a selective acid gas removal (AGR)
process using typically the Sulfinol M solvent, which reduces the sulfur compounds down to
< 15 ppmv. The H S rich solvent is stripped in a regenerator column and the HS rich gas is
2 2
converted into elemental sulfur in a Claus plant equipped with a Shell Claus Off-gas Treating
Unit (SCOT). The Claus plant will typically be operated with oxygen and also removes traces of
NH and HCN resulting from the wastewater stripping columns. Over 99% of the sulfur is
3
recovered as liquid elemental sulfur. The SCOT off gases can be incinerated. Alternatively, the
Claus Off-gas can be hydrogenated and recycled to either the COS hydrolysis or the Sulfinol M
absorber thereby eliminating the Claus Off-gas incinerator as a point source of emissions.
1-21

For low sulfur coals such as PRB the Sulferox process, which produces sulfur directly from the
syngas, may also be used, thereby eliminating the Claus plant.
Figure 1-3
Shell Coal Gasification Process
1-22

History of Shell Coal Gasification Process – Pilot, Demonstration and Commercial
Plants
Shell developed a heavy oil gasification process in the late 1950’s to 1960’s of similar design to
the Texaco heavy oil gasification process and has licensed many units around the world. In the
early 1970’s, Shell started development of a gasifier for solid fuel of a different design. A 6
ton/day process development unit was built at Royal Dutch Shell’s Amsterdam laboratories in
1976 and operated for about 20 years. Shell and Koppers came to an agreement for joint
development of a larger unit and a 150 ton/day unit was built at Deutsche Shell’s Harburg
refinery (near Hamburg) and operated from 1979 to 1982. This gasifier had the appearance of a
pressurized version of the atmospheric Koppers-Totzek gasification process that was in use in
several worldwide locations. Shortly after the conclusion of the Harburg tests, Krupp took over
Koppers and the partnership with Shell was ended. Each of the former partners then proceeded
with their own independent coal gasification process developments. Shell built a 250 ton/day
Shell Advanced Coal Gasification Facility (SCGP-1) at their refinery in Deer Park, Texas that
operated on a wide variety of coals from 1987 to 1992. Krupp-Koppers built a 60 ton/day pilot
plant at Furstenhausen, near Saabrucken, Germany that operated from 1988 to 1992. The Krupp-
Koppers process was called Prenflo and it shared many common features with the Shell coal
gasification process. Both had similar pneumatic conveying feed systems and opposed fuel
injectors, upflow design, and cooling at the gasifier outlet with recycled syngas. Each of these
technologies was scaled up to commercial size in IGCC plants in the 1990’s. Uhde joined with
Krupp for the development and marketing of the Prenflo technology.
The Shell technology was adopted for the 253 MW IGCC plant at Buggenum in the Limburg
province of the Netherlands. The initial plant ownership was by a consortium of Dutch power
companies called Demkolec. The plant started up in December 1993 and has operated with a
variety of international merchant coals and coal blends. More recently some biomass fuels have
also been co-gasified with the coal feed.
The Prenflo technology was selected by the ELCOGAS European consortium for the 300 MW
IGCC plant in Puertollano, Spain. This plant started operation on a 50/50 mixture of local
Spanish coal and petroleum coke in 1998 and continues in operation today.
In early 2002, Shell and Krupp-Uhde jointly announced that the Shell and Prenflo technologies
would no longer be offered separately but would be merged and offered as the Shell Coal
Gasification Technology. For the US market, Black & Veatch has been added to the
Shell/Krupp Uhde team.

Potential Improvements to the Shell Technology
The Shell technology is well suited to a wide variety of coals from anthracite to lignite and for
petroleum coke. All coals must be dried to some extent so that they flow and can be
pneumatically conveyed reliably. For some low rank coals, the drying costs can be significant.
Shell has recently suggested the use of RWE’s MCV (Mechanical Vapor Compression) drying
technology for high moisture coals. A list of potential improvements to the overall Shell
technology are shown in Table 1-8.

The use of lock hoppers becomes more costly and less efficient as the pressure is increased since
more nitrogen per ton of coal is required. Any new feed system that would enable Shell to
operate at a higher pressure without incurring such additional burdens would be greatly
advantageous particularly when CO capture is considered. In those applications, such as
2
chemical synthesis and CO capture, where CO is produced as part of the overall process the use
2 2
of CO as the conveying gas rather than nitrogen would be an advantage since it would result in
2
1-26

less inert gas taking up vessel volume in downstream equipment or the synthesis loop and would
improve the economics.
Another major improvement for plants with CO capture is the use of a partial water quench to
2
400°C (750°F), still retaining the cyclone and hot gas filter, but eliminating the syngas cooler
and recycle gas system and compressor. The capital cost savings should be very significant.
Shell believes that larger gasifiers can be built so that either two gasifiers supply three gas
turbines (~900MW) or, even further, that a single gasifier could supply two gas turbines
(~600MW). This would obviously reduce the capital cost significantly. If this can be
substantiated, Shell would be in a somewhat better position than GE or COP to provide larger
gasifiers that could supply enough syngas for the future 60 Hz and 50 Hz gas turbines and which
would reduce the number of gasifiers needed to match the syngas requirements of large 10,000-
20,000 bpd F-T reactors and thereby provide considerable economies of scale.
Shell IGCC Reference Plant Design
The configuration of the reference Shell IGCC pant can be summarized as:
• Coal Handling (1x100% + 2x 100% conveyors))
• Coal Milling & Drying (3 x 50%)
• Coal Pneumatic Feed Systems (2x 50%)
• Air Separation Units (2x50%) Some Extraction Air from CT Compressor (%?)
• Gasification, Syngas Cooler, Cyclone, Char Filter (2x50%).
• Slag handling (1x100%)
• Low Temperature Gas Cooling (2x 50%)
• COS Hydrolysis, Mercury Removal, Sulfinol M (or other) Acid Gas Removal (2 x 50%)
• Claus Plant (2x66%) Claus Tail Gas Recycle to Sulfinol
• Combined Cycle (2 x STG6-5000F or 2 x GE 7 FB, 2 HRSG, 1 ST
(1800 psig/1050F/1050F))
• Net Output ~ 600 MW
• Plot Space left for Addition of CO Capture
2
A spare gasifier can be included for high syngas availability. For low sulfur coals such as PRB,
another option is to use the Sulferox process for direct removal of the sulfur species as elemental
sulfur from the syngas. This Sulferox unit would replace the Sulfinol M and eliminate the need
for the Claus plants and associated Claus Tail Gas treatment with some resultant capital cost
savings.
At the 2005 Gasification Technologies Conference, Shell presented performance estimates for a
German Lignite of 53% moisture and PRB with 30% moisture. An IGCC heat rate for the
1-27

German Lignite with MVC drying to 12% moisture was projected at 8100 Btu/kWh
(8540kJ/kWh) HHV and for drying PRB to 6% moisture the heat rate was projected was 8200
Btu/kWh (8650 kJ/kWh) HHV basis. More recent studies by DOE, EPRI and others have
estimated somewhat higher heat rates.
At the CoalFleet meeting in Birmingham, Alabama, in November 2005, Shell presented
estimates for the standard 600 MW net IGCC with no CO capture at ~$1600/kW TPC for US
2
Gulf Coast location (coal not specified), and 500 MW net with CO capture at ~$2200/kW.
2
Translating these estimates to standard Midwest location (factor ~1.1) and June 2006 dollars
gives ~$1885/kW and $2595/kW TPC without and with capture, respectively. The results of
more recent studies by NRG, DOE, and EPRI are discussed in more detail in Chapter 8
Economics.
Shell has also stated that the gasifiers could be supplied at larger sizes so that another offering
would be with two larger gasification, syngas coolers and gas clean up trains supplying 3 x GE
7FB or 3x STG6 -5000F gas turbines.(with 3 HRSG’s and one steam turbine). The net output
would be ~900 MW.
As a further alternate, Shell has suggested a single gasification, syngas cooler, gas clean up train
for a two gas turbine plant with a net output of ~ 600 MW. This would represent an approximate
doubling of the throughput of the Shell gasifier at Buggenum.
Commercial Shell Projects in Development
The Shell/Uhde/ Black & Veatch team are actively engaged is several studies with a number of
US power companies but most of them have not been announced publicly. A number of the
studies are aimed the potential use of PRB coals and lignite where the higher efficiency of the
dry coal fed Shell technology may have an advantage over the coal/water slurry fed gasification
technologies of GE and COP.
The first public announcement of US IGCC projects based on the Shell technology was made by
NRG in June 2006. As part of a major plan for 10.5 GW of new generation, NRG plans three
Shell-based IGCC projects at existing plant locations: Huntley, New York, Montville,
Connecticut, and Indian River, Delaware. Each plant would be about 750 MW gross and 630
MW net, and would be designed to handle a range of coals (presumably bituminous but possibly
also PRB sub-bituminous at Huntley) and petroleum coke. The plan also calls for retirement of
968 MW of older, less efficient, higher emitting generating units at Northeast sites. The
announcement stated that the plants would be designed with the capability for capturing carbon
at a capital cost of $1955/kW gross. It appears that this figure is probably a Total Capital
Requirement (TCR) rather than a Total Plant Cost (TPC or EPC) and does not include the cost of
CO capture. In a presentation at the Gasification Technologies Conference in October 2006, the
2
target start update for all three sites was given as 2012 with start of construction in late 2008.
NRG has applied for Investment tax credits under Section 48A of EPAct 2005.
A proposal submitted by Waste Management & Processors Inc. (WMPI) of Frackville,
Pennsylvania in response to the first DOE solicitation of the Clean Coal Power Initiative (CCPI)
was selected by DOE in late 2002. The WMPI project has a Pennsylvania location and plans to
1-28

use the Shell coal gasification process to gasify coal and anthracite waste to make power, steam
and 5000 barrels/day of Fischer Tropsch diesel liquids using Sasol technology. This project has
yet to finalize a contract agreement with DOE. In September 2005, the State of Pennsylvania
agreed to take the majority of the diesel product from this plant and the project has political
support from many quarters in both State and Federal governments. Nevertheless, given this
extended delay, further progress on this project is uncertain.
In June 2005, the Queensland Government’s Stanwell Corporation announced the selection of
the Shell Coal gasification technology for the “Smart State” study of an IGCC plant to be
designed for CO capture and subsequent sequestration. Under an agreement announced by the
2
Premier of Queensland on July 26, 2006, Shell Development (Australia) will provide technical
support to ZeroGen, a Stanwell subsidiary, in the next phase of Stanwell’s proposed project
comprising a test drilling program to determine the geological suitability of proposed rock
formations for sequestration. A demonstration plant of up to 100 MW based on the Shell coal
gasification technology is planned for the Rockhampton site. 420,000 mt/yr of CO2 would be
piped 220 km (140 mi) to deep underground saline aquifers. Shell will be offered a 10% equity
position in the project. The Queensland government has earmarked $300 million in Australian
dollars for the project (about US$230 million). EPRI has provided technical consultancy to the
project.
Nuon (owner of the 253 MW IGCC at Buggenum, The Netherlands) has announced plans for a
multi fuel (coal, biomass and natural gas) 1200 MW “Magnum” plant. Three possible sites were
evaluated in the Groningen, Rotterdam, and Vlissingen areas. The site in Groningen, North
Holland has been selected, near Eemshaven. The new power station will be equipped with
several gasifiers in which the various fuels can be used in mixed form. ABB Lummus Global
has been retained. Shell technology has been selected. A go/no-go decision is scheduled for
mid-2007, with startup targeted for early 2011.
