ConocoPhilips E-Gas Coal Gasification Process
E-Gas Technical Process Description
The COP E-Gas process is a slurry fed, pressurized, upflow, entrained slagging gasifier. The
two-stage operation of this gasifier makes it unique. Wet crushers (rod mills) produce slurries
with the raw feed coal. Dry coal slurry concentrations range from 50 to 70 wt%, depending on
the inherent moisture and quality of the feed coal. The slurry water consists of recycle water
from the raw gas cooling together with makeup water. In the current standard, IGCC design
about 70 percent of the total slurry feed is fed to the first (or bottom) stage of the gasifier. COP
refers to this mode of operation as the “Full Slurry Quench” mode. All the oxygen is used to
gasify this portion of the slurry. The first stage is a horizontal cylinder with two horizontally
1-30

opposed burners. Alternatively a vertical Cylinder may be used in the future. The highly
exothermic gasification/oxidation reactions take place rapidly at temperatures of 1400-1500°C
(2500-2700°F). The coal ash is converted to molten slag, which flows down through a tap hole.
The molten slag is quenched in water and removed in a novel continuous-pressure
letdown/dewatering system.
The hot raw gas from the first stage enters the second (top) stage, which is a vertical cylinder
perpendicular to the first stage. The remaining 30 percent of coal slurry is injected into this hot
raw gas. The endothermic gasification/devolatilization reaction in this stage reduces the final gas
temperature to about 1000-1050°C (1830 -1900°F).
Char is produced in the second stage. However, the yield of this char is relatively small because
only about 30 percent of the coal is fed to the second stage. Char yield is dependent on the
reactivity of the feed coal and decreases with increasing reactivity. The char effectively absorbs
the hydrocarbon liquids produced in the top stage. The char is removed in a hot dry filter and
recycled to the gasifier first stage, where it is readily gasified.
The gasifier is lined with uncooled refractory layers. The hotter first-stage section of the gasifier
also includes a special slag-resistant refractory. The hot raw coal gas from the second stage is
cooled in the waste heat recovery (syngas cooler) unit, which consists of a fire-tube boiler. Fire-
tube boilers cost markedly less than comparable duty water-tube boilers. This is because of the
large saving in high-grade steel associated with containing the hot high-pressure synthesis gas in
relatively small tubes.
Two-stage operation improves the efficiency, reduces oxygen requirements, and enables more
effective operation on slurry feeds
The E-Gas gasification process accepts solid feed which can contain varying amounts fixed
carbon, volatile matter and ash. During the gasification of the solid fuel a raw particulate laden
synthesis gas (syngas) is produced as well as a residual solid stream containing the ash content of
the feed. The ash of the feedstock exits the bottom of the gasifier as a water slurry and is
dewatered in the slag handling system as shown in Figure 1-4 and Figure 1-5.
1-31

Figure 1-4
ConocoPhillips E-Gas Gasification System Diagram
Gasification
The E-Gas gasifier consists of two stages, a slagging first stage, and an entrained flow, non-
slagging second stage as shown in Figure 1-5. The first stage is a horizontal, refractory lined
vessel in which fuel is partially combusted with oxygen at an elevated temperature 1400-1500°C
(2500 -2700°F) and pressure up to 41 barg (600psig). Oxygen and preheated slurry are fed to
each of two opposing mixing nozzles, one on each end of the horizontal section of the gasifier.
E-GAS has developed its own proprietary design for this mixer/burner. Oxygen feed rate to the
mixers is carefully controlled to maintain the gasification temperature above the ash fusion point
to ensure good slag removal and high carbon conversion. The fuel is almost totally gasified in
this environment to form synthetic gas consisting principally of hydrogen, carbon monoxide,
carbon dioxide and water. Sulfur in the fuel is converted to primarily hydrogen sulfide (HS)
2
with a small portion converted to carbonyl sulfide (COS). With appropriate processing
downstream, over 99% of the total sulfur can be removed from the feedstock.
The slurry concentration used in the design for bituminous coal is approximately 65% dry solids
to water ratio. Mineral matter in the fuel forms a molten slag which flows continuously through
a tap hole in the floor of the horizontal section into a water quench bath, located below the first
stage. The solidified slag exits the bottom of the quench section, crushed and flows through a
continuous pressure-letdown system as a slag/water slurry. This continuous slag removal
technique eliminates high-maintenance problem-prone lock hoppers and completely prevents the
escape of raw gasification products to the atmosphere during slag removal. The slag/water slurry
1-32

is then directed to a dewatering and handling area. The raw syngas generated in the first stage
flows up from the horizontal section into the second stage of the gasifier.
Figure 1-5
Gas Gasifier Diagram
The second stage is a vertical refractory lined vessel in which additional slurry is reacted with the
hot syngas stream exiting the first stage. The fuel undergoes devolatilization and pyrolysis
thereby generating more syngas with a higher heat content since no additional oxygen is
introduced into the second stage. This additional fuel serves to lower the temperature of the
syngas exiting the first stage to 1000-1050°C (1850-1900°F) by the endothermic nature of the
devolatilization and pyrolysis reactions. In addition to the above reactions, the water reacts with
a portion of the carbon to produce carbon monoxide, carbon dioxide and hydrogen. Unreacted
fuel (char) is carried overhead with the gas.
The slag/water slurry flows continuously into a dewatering bin. The bulk of the slag settles out
in the bin while water overflows into a settler in which the remaining slag fines are settled. The
clear water from the settler is passed through heat exchangers where it is cooled as the final step
before being returned to the gasifier quench section. Dewatered slag is loaded into a truck or rail
car for transport to market or its storage site. The slurry of fine slag particulates from the bottom
of the settler is recycled to the slurry preparation area.
1-33

Syngas Cooling and Particulate Removal
The next two steps in the processing are to cool the syngas and then remove the particulate for
recycle to the gasifier. Because of the high temperature of the syngas at this point in the process
cooling is accomplished by producing steam. With cooling preceding the particulate removal
step, the filtration of the particulates can be accomplished in a temperature range more forgiving
to the particulate removal unit. The hot raw syngas with entrained particulate matter exiting the
gasifier system is cooled from 1040°C to 400°C (1900 to 750°F) in the syngas cooler. The
syngas cooler is a vertical firetube heat recovery boiler system with the hot syngas on the tube
side. This unit generates saturated high-pressure steam, up to 130 barg (1885 psig). Steam from
the high-temperature heat recovery system is super heated in the gas turbine heat recovery
system for use in power generation. Alternatively, syngas can be superheated to more modest
temperatures of 400 -420°C (750-788°F) in the syngas cooler.
Figure 1-6
Gas Syngas Cooling and Particulate Removal Diagram
The syngas leaving the high temperature heat recovery unit passes through a two-step
cyclone/barrier filter particulate removal system to remove solids from the syngas. The barrier
filter vessel contains numerous porous candle filter elements on which the particulate collects.
The syngas flows through the elements and exits the unit as a particulate-free syngas. Particulate
removal efficiency is better than 99.9%. Periodically the elements are back-pulsed with high-
pressure syngas or nitrogen to remove particulate cake formed on the surface of the elements.
The particulate cake falls to the bottom of the vessel and is pneumatically transferred along with
the cyclone catch to the first stage of the gasifier with high-pressure recycled syngas, steam or
nitrogen. With the char recycled to the gasifier, near complete gasification of the carbon content
of the feedstock is obtained. The particulate-free gas proceeds to the low temperature heat
recovery system.
The syngas leaving the filter is water washed to remove ammonia and chlorides, preheated by a
feed effluent exchanger and sent to a COS hydrolysis unit. After further cooling, the syngas
1-34

passes to the MDEA (MethylDiethanolamine) acid gas removal (AGR) process for HS removal.
2
An ion exchange membrane process is used for removal of the heat stable salts (HSS) from a
blow down slipstream of the MDEA solvent. If deep sulfur removal is required in order to meet
the criterion for use of an SCR then the Selexol process can be used instead of MDEA. The
clean syngas is then saturated and superheated by an additional 50-80°F (28-44°C) for delivery
to the gas turbine combined cycle.
The HS rich gas from the MDEA unit is sent to a Claus plant. The Claus plant tail gas after
2
hydrogenation is compressed and recycled to the first stage of the gasifier. The blow down from
the water scrubber goes to a sour water stripper the overhead from which is sent to the Claus
plant. If zero liquid discharge is required a vapor recompression evaporator can be used to
recover condensate quality water and some salt.
History of the E-Gas Coal Gasification Process – Pilot, Demonstration and
Commercial Plants
The E-Gas process was originally developed by the Dow Chemical company. In 1979, a test
program was initiated on a small (12 ton/day dry lignite) pilot plant based on Dow’s two stage
concept. The first phase of testing was with air blowing and it continued for 38 months. In
1983, the pilot plant was converted to oxygen blowing. A water tube syngas cooler was also
installed and tested. In 1980-1981, Dow licensed and built an air blown Texaco Quench
gasification plant that was operated for a while in conjunction with a Westinghouse 191 gas
turbine at the Dow complex in Plaquemine, Louisiana. A larger prototype of the Dow process
was started up in 1983 rated at 550 tons/day dry coal basis in air blown mode and 1200 tons/day
dry basis with oxygen. The air for the initial operation of the prototype gasifier was supplied by
extraction from the compressor section of the 191 gas turbine and the syngas was fed back to the
gas turbine. In mid 1984, the prototype unit was converted to oxygen blowing and the unit was
shut down in July 1985 when sufficient information for the design of a commercial sized gasifier
has been obtained.
A new project was launched based on the gasification of Powder River Basin sub-bituminous
coal (~2200 tons/day as received or 1580 tons/day dry basis) at the Plaquemine, Louisiana
location supplying syngas to two Westinghouse 501 D5 gas turbines with an IGCC output of 155
MW. This project, called the Louisiana Gasification Technology, Inc. (LGTI), was operated
from 1987 to 1995 under subsidy from the Synthetic Fuels Corporation and later the Treasury
Department. A new company, Destec, was formed in 1989, and the gasification technology was
transferred from Dow to Destec.
In 1991, Destec and Public Service of Indiana (PSI) submitted a proposal (the Wabash River
Coal Gasification Repowering Project) in response to the DOE Clean Coal Technology
Demonstration Program Round 4 solicitation. The project was selected to demonstrate the
operation of the gasification process with bituminous coal and its integration with a new GE 7FA
based combined cycle with the steam from the HRSG used to repower an aged steam turbine to
achieve improved efficiency and reduced emissions. The gasification island was owned by
Destec and supplied syngas to the combined cycle power block owned by PSI. The Wabash
project started operations in October 1995 and completed the DOE demonstration program in
January 2000.
1-35

In June 1997, Destec Energy was purchased by the NGC Corporation and in June 1998, NGC
changed its name to Dynegy Inc. In January 2000, Global Energy acquired Dynegy’s
gasification assets including the technology and the Wabash gasification plant. The acquisition
of the E-Gas technology by ConocoPhillips (COP) from Global Energy was completed in August
2003. As part of the acquisition arrangement with Global Energy, fifteen COP technical and
management personnel remain located at the Wabash site and test programs for improvements in
the technology are conducted there under COP and DOE/NETL programs.
After completion of DOE program the Wabash plant switched from Indiana coal to petroleum
coke feedstock. The plant shut down in 2004 due to a business dispute between Global Energy
and Cinergy (PSI). A new 50/50 ownership agreement between Global Energy and the Wabash
Valley Power Association (WVPA) was concluded in 2005, and a new MOU with Cinergy for
purchase of the syngas was announced. The plant restarted in June 2005. WVPA may also later
purchase the combined cycle power block from Cinergy/PSI.
COP licenses several process technologies including Petroleum Coking. In 2004, COP and Fluor
announced an agreement to jointly provide E-Gas Technology customers both project
development support for planned projects, conceptual and detailed engineering and turnkey
contracts for construction of solid fuel gasification facilities for IGCC and other applications. A
further arrangement has been made between COP, Fluor and Siemens to offer EPC term sheets
with COP process guarantees and O&M support for specific projects. This team of COP, Fluor
and Siemens was selected for the pre-FEED engineering of the 2x600 MW Mesaba project in
2005 and is currently conducting FEED planning work for that project.
COP has also entered into an arrangement with Kiewit and WorleyParsons and that team is
currently conducting a FEED for the Rentech Energy Midwest project for East Dubuque, Illinois.
This project will use E Gas to retrofit a 1000 tpd Ammonia plant and produce 2000 bpd of F-T
liquids and ~ 60 MW of power.

Wabash has developed a scheme for injector replacement within 18 hours. These developments
markedly reduce the forced outages due to injector tip failures and other minor outages. Wabash
has also reported that injectors have had up to 4000 hours of operation with a current average of
~2500 hours. This is longer than the general GE injector life experience. Wabash does use some
pre-heating of the slurry whereas GE does not. Injector life in slurry fed gasifiers is limited by
stress corrosion cracking of the tips and the cause has been speculated to be the brief existence of
droplets at the tip. It could therefore also be speculated that preheating the slurry diminishes the
possibility of droplets existing at the tip.
1-37

Refractory life for the vertical hot face in the brick lined coal water slurry fed gasifiers at
Wabash and TEC is typically ~2 years. At Wabash, the refractory area in the vicinity of the slag
tap is the most prone to wear and repairs are made at each outage opportunity. The Wabash
plant is provided with a spare gasifier and although it is not currently tied in with the rest of the
downstream processing the connection and changeover could be effected in relatively few days
(particularly with some warning).
When considering the future availability of 600 MW commercial IGCC plants with two trains of
gasification and gas turbines the issue of sparing is great importance. For availability over 90%
E-Gas would probably require a spare gasifier. However, E-Gas presented a paper at the 2002
Gasification Technologies Conference suggesting that for companies and locations that
experience lower power demand in Spring and Fall a spare gasifier may not be justified and the
80% availability that should be achievable without a spare should be sufficient.
Slag tap blockage has occurred occasionally at all of the coal based IGCC plants. If a blockage
does occur the outage can last 8-10 days in order to cool down and mine out the blocked area. It
has nearly always been associated with a change in feed coal properties that unexpectedly
changed the slag viscosity. The so-called T250 temperature (the temperature at which the slag
viscosity is 250 centipoises) is typically used to give an indication of the temperature at which
the gasifier should be run to avoid slag blockage problems. This temperature is monitored in
most cases but the slag viscosity measurement is time consuming (~6-8 hours) and there is
always a question about whether the sample taken was truly representative. The problem of
variation in ash fusion properties is more likely to arise with blended coals and particularly any
coals or feeds such as petroleum coke that need flux additions. At Wabash, there have been four
such incidents. The inclusion of a slag crusher in the slag discharge line is recommended for all
future IGCC plants.
It appears that the syngas cooler fouling is somewhat coal dependent and the deposits are often
enriched in alkali metals and iron. A mixture of Potassium Alumino-silicate and Pyrrhotite
(FeS) appears to produce a relatively low melting eutectic. Wabash differs from the other three
plants in that it uses a downflow firetube SGC design. There is also an additional factor with the
E-Gas technology because of the survival of trace tar components in the outlet gas derived from
the second stage coal feed, the coking of which sometimes leads to deposits that can break free
from surfaces and plug the inlet tube sheet. The use of CFD modeling enabled Wabash to make
gas path modifications to prevent larger particulate matter from fouling the inlet tube sheet.
However, deposits do continue to form within the SGC tubing. A device has been installed that
enables periodic tube cleaning in place. With coal as feedstock Wabash schedules two outages
per year for SGC cleaning however with petroleum coke feed they experience less fouling and
hope to move to a longer period between scheduled SGC cleanings. It is interesting to note that
at both Wabash and ELCOGAS the SGC deposits at certain locations have shown significant
enrichment in Germanium, Ge, and Arsenic, As. The Germanium could be worth recovering for
use in the electronics industry.
Wabash experienced early problems with candle filter fouling and breakage but after gaining
some further insights from a slip stream filter test program have greatly improved the
availability. Metallic elements are currently used because their availability experience (current
estimated life 9000 hours) has been better than earlier installed ceramics. However, tests on
newer ceramic candle filter elements in the slip stream filter have shown more satisfactory
1-38

performance and if a longer life of 20,000 hours is justified they may switch back to ceramic
elements. The Wabash filter operates at ~350-400°C (660 -750°F) whereas the filters at NUON
and ELCOGAS operate at 240-250°C (460-480°F). Irrespective of the details of the filter vessel
design it has been proven that some restriction of the lateral movement of suspended filter
elements is necessary to avoid excessive flexing and breakage. Blowback cleaning systems must
be designed to prevent build up of bridges between candles that might also restrict their
movement and lead to failure.
The COS hydrolysis reactor also converts some of the CO to formic acid (HCOOH) and oxalic
acid (COOH COOH) which lead to the formation of heat stable salts (HSS) that can deposit out
and foul the AGR absorber and stripper trays and increase solvent usage. The solution now
adopted at the Wabash and Tampa plants, which use MDEA as the AGR solvent, has been to add
an online ion exchange membrane system to continuously regenerate the solvent and remove the
contaminants from a blow-down slipstream taken off the circulating solvent.
In 1996-7, EPRI conducted a durability surveillance program on the gas turbine at the Wabash
site. The switch from fuel oil to syngas was found to be routinely and evenly achieved and in
general the gas turbine performance and blade temperature profiles were smoother with syngas
than with fuel oil. However, there were some early problems on the gas turbine side with syngas
leaks in an expansion bellows and combustor liner cracking. The bellows was redesigned and
replaced and the liners replaced in July ’97. In addition, modifications were made to the spacer
between the second and third turbine wheels of the 7 FA (this was a fleet problem for the GE F
turbines and was unrelated to syngas operation). An inspection of the liners in May ’98 after
~6000 hours of operation showed some beginnings of cracks in the liners and it was estimated
that there was ~2000 hours of remaining life. This was consistent with GE’s estimate of ~8000
hours liner life.
In mid-March 1999, damage occurred to rows 14 through 17 of the air compressor rotor and
stator of the combustion turbine (CT) assembly, which resulted in a 14 week unscheduled outage
for the project. This failure was unrelated to the IGCC syngas application. PSI Energy
conducted a root cause failure analysis. The CT was returned to service on June 22 1999. In
July 1999, a water spray system was added to the air intake of the CT to allow more air through
the machine when the turbine is temperature limited. This system is only used during peak
demand, when it increases the total power output by 5-10 MW.
The addition of natural gas combustion capability to the 7 FA resulted in hot spots due to poor
mixing from the additional natural gas port at the center of the circle of syngas ports. Continuing
problems with the CT and HRSG resulted in extended periods in 2001-3 where there was no
requirement for syngas.
The HRSG has been subject to tube leaks. A change to the main steam piping support system
was made in 1997 to allow needed expansion during startup conditions. This alleviated the
problem to some extent but tube leaks have been a continuing problem. This HRSG is the only
bottom supported HRSG that Foster Wheeler has built. A changeover to a top supported system
is planned for Fall 2006.
1-39

Potential Improvements to the COP E-Gas Technology
A list of potential improvements to the overall E-Gas technology are shown in Table 1-11

Warm gas clean-up for sulfur removal Improved efficiency
The E-Gas technology is well suited to bituminous coals and petroleum coke for which water
slurry loadings of 60-65 weight % dry solids can be achieved. However, with coals of high
moisture content or high ash content the achievable energy density of the slurry is reduced
leading to higher oxygen consumption per unit of product and reduced gasifier efficiency. Since
it is anticipated that the majority of new US coal plants are likely to use lower rank Western
coals such as Powder River Basin (PRB) and lignite there is a great incentive to improve the
performance of this technology for these low rank coals. This can most likely be affected by
changing the method of feeding the gasifier in some manner that avoids the higher oxygen use
and improves gasifier efficiency. Several schemes have been suggested (Holt 2002) including
flash drying of the slurry, slurrying the coal in liquid CO , use of a dry coal feeder (e.g. the
2
Stamet pump) etc.
The E-Gas gasifier at Wabash typically runs with 10-15% of the feed to the second stage. For
their standard IGCC reference plant design COP has selected an increase to 30% of the feed to
the 2nd stage a mode they call “Full Slurry Quench”. This will increase the methane content of
the syngas and reduce oxygen consumption.
At the current design pressure 41 barg (600 psig), there are throughput limitations of ~ 4400 stpd
PRB coal or 1950 MBtu/hr LHV syngas. Standard IGCC design features “Full slurry quench”
1-40

with ~ 30% coal fed to 2nd stage with consequent higher methane content that inevitably limits
carbon capture capability and suitability for most synthesis applications.
COP has also suggested a tall cylindrical design capable of design pressures up to 1000 psig that
would operate with 100% feed to the second stage and with the recovered char fed to the bottom
first stage. COP has entitled this as an “Entrained Slagging Transport Reactor” (ESTR). This
concept is shown schematically in Figure 1-7.
Figure 1-7
COP Entrained Slagging Transport Reactor (ESTR)
The proposed improved ESTR is currently envisaged as tall cylindrical design (perhaps of two
diameters). If operated in this mode the lower gasifier outlet temperature 700-760°C
(1300-1400°F) gives higher gasifier efficiency with higher methane content and reduced oxygen
usage but there would likely be an increased risk of tar survival. The high methane content of
the syngas from this mode of operation would not be suitable for high CO capture or for
2
synthesis. However, for CO capture, hydrogen production, Methanol and Fischer-Tropsch
2
synthesis this high pressure tall cylindrical design could be operated in the mode typically used
at Wabash (only 10% to the 2nd stage). The higher pressure results in reduced costs of CO
2
capture and is advantageous for hydrogen production and high pressure synthesis e.g. Ammonia.
The higher pressure also enables the gasifier to process higher feed rates of low rank coals
including lignite. The size would also then be suitable for the 50 Hz market and future larger gas
turbines in both 50 and 60 Hz markets.
1-41

COP E-Gas IGCC Reference Plant Design
ConocoPhillips (COP), Fluor and Siemens are working on the IGCC reference plant design for
bituminous coals and petroleum coke. These designs are offered with full EPC overnight pricing
and forecasted completion costs, and EPC contract terms with guaranteed price, schedule and
performance. A plant design for sub-bituminous coals is also offered and is included in the
Mesaba project. The configuration of the reference E-Gas IGCC pant can be summarized as:
• Coal Handling (1x100% Conveyor, 2 x 70% Rod mills))
• Slurry Preparation and Pumping (2 x 50%)
• Air Separation Units (2x50%) Some extraction Air from CT compressor (%?)
• Gasification (Full Slurry Quench), Syngas Cooler, Cyclone, Char Filter (2x50%).
• Slag handling (1x100%)
• Low temperature Gas Cooling (2x 50%)
• COS Hydrolysis, Mercury Removal, MDEA Acid Gas Removal (2 x 50%)
• Claus plant (2x66%) Claus tail gas recycle to Gasifier
• Combined Cycle (2 x STG6-5000F, 2 HRSG, 1 ST (1800 psig/1050F/1050F))
• Plot space left for addition of CO capture
2
There is no spare gasification train in the reference design but a spare is offered as an option.
COP has suggested that many power companies would have the option of scheduling
maintenance in the spring and fall when power demand is lower and could thereby avoid the
need for a spare gasifier. COP also believes that improvements in refractory life can
significantly extend the period when replacement is required. However, the previous historic
experience suggests that to meet an overall annual IGCC equivalent availability of > 90% a spare
gasification train would be required. The Mesaba design feedstocks include Illinois basin coal,
PRB sub-bituminous and up to 50% petroleum coke. For this project three gasifiers have been
selected (2 operating + 1 spare).
Other options include coal / petroleum coke blending, zero liquid discharge, Selexol for deep
sulfur removal (instead of MDEA) and the addition of SCR into the HRSG.
Cost and performance estimates for the reference plant, at 60°F conditions, and 600 ft elevation,
on Petroleum Coke, Pittsburgh #8, Illinois #6 and Wyoming PRB coals were presented at the
2005 Gasification Technologies Conference. The Overnight EPC costs were estimated in 2005$
for a mid-west location and were shown as a range -5% and +15%. The costs include Contractor
profit and Contingency of 20% but exclude start up and Owner’s costs. An update of these cost
estimates is included in Chapter 8 Economics.
The main stack permit targets for the IGCC plants with MDEA and Selexol with SCR are
compared in Table 1-12 to those emissions cited in Wisconsin Electric’s Draft EIS for the Elm
Road Supercritical PC boilers.

COP E-Gas Commercial Projects in Development
ConocoPhillips (COP) and its EPC teams have completed seven scoping and pre FEED studies
and are currently working on six more projects. COP has also supported clients in making four
E-Gas technology based EPACT investment tax applications for the production of power, CO
2
for EOR, ammonia, diesel and SNG.
One of the major projects that applied for the EPACT tax credits was the Excelsior Energy IGCC
project to be located in the Hoyt Lakes area of the Mesaba Iron range in Minnesota. This is
envisaged as a nominal 1200 MW to be conducted in two 600 MW phases. It was one of the two
IGCC projects selected by DOE from proposals submitted in response to the second solicitation
under the DOE Clean Coal Power Initiative (CCPI) program in 2004. The DOE cooperative
agreement for 36 $ Million was signed in 2005. The plant design has three gasification trains (2
operating / 1 spare) and is to be fully fuel flexible from 100% PRB to 100% Illinois 6 to 100%
pet coke and in any blend. The gasifiers are each sized for 4275 stpd as-received PRB. The
power block is based on two Siemens SGT6-5000F combustion turbines. The gross output is
232 MW per gas turbine which together with a 300 MW steam turbine results in a nominal 740
MW gross, 606 MW net. The plant design is essentially the reference plant design with the
addition of a spare train of slurry feed, gasification, syngas cooler and hot dry filter. The spare
train has been estimated to add 5% to seasonal availability or 10% to annual availability.
Minnesota PUC voted to provide $10 million to the project from the Renewable Development
Fund over the next 5 years. Excelsior also has the right to negotiate a power purchase agreement
with Xcel for the majority of the output from the project. Excelsior Energy filed proposed power
purchase agreements for Phases 1 and 2 of the Mesaba Energy Project with the PUC in
December 2005. There is a contested case evaluation at Minnesota PUC. In Phase 1, public
comments are accepted through December 2006 with case completion in January 2007. The
Phase 2 case begins in January 2007 with case completion in July 2007. A joint permit
application was submitted to the PUC in June 2006 and was accepted as complete on July 28,
2006. Air, NPDES, Water Appropriation and Wetlands permit applications were submitted to
the appropriate authorities June- October 2006. State and Federal processes require preparation
of an EIS. A draft EIS is scheduled for February 2007 and a final EIS in June 2007. The
schedule anticipates that the permits will be issued in the 4th quarter of 2007 to be followed by
financial closure 1st quarter 2008 and plant start up in late 2011.

1-44

Additional Gasification Technologies
2
ADDITIONAL GASIFICATION TECHNOLOGIES
The description, history, outage causes, potential improvements, commercial offerings and
commercial projects in development for the major candidate coal gasification technologies
currently being offered in the market (GE Energy, COP E-Gas and Shell) for the IGCC
application are presented and discussed in Chapter 1.
The other coal gasification technology that has been and continues to be used commercially in
several countries is the Sasol Lurgi moving (or fixed bed) dry ash gasifier. However, the coal
feed size limitations and the undesirable tar by-products have generally limited its consideration
for current deployment in OECD countries.
Sasol-Lurgi
The Lurgi dry ash moving (or fixed) bed gasifier was the first pressurized coal gasification
technology that was developed for commercial use in the 1930’s.
It is a pressurized oxygen-blown countercurrent gasifier in commercial use in South Africa, the
US, Germany, the Czech Republic, and China. The largest plants are Sasol 2 and 3 in South
Africa each with 40 Mark 4 (4 m diameter) gasifiers making a total of ~150,000 bpd of Fischer-
Tropsch liquids and a variety of chemicals. The Dakota Gasification plant in Beulah ND has 14
Mark.4 gasifiers and has been in operation for the last 20 years making 150 Million SCFD of
pipeline quality SNG. The plants in Germany and the Czech Republic use some of the gas to
fuel gas turbine combined cycle power plants. The plants in China are mostly for ammonia
production.
Sasol and Lurgi agreed to jointly license this technology in 2003. In the Sasol-Lurgi gasifier,
sized coal (typically in the size range 6-50 mm) is introduced at the top via a lock hopper system
and moves slowly downwards reacting with gases ascending counter-currently through the bed.
At the top, the entering coal is heated and dried, and in turn cools the gas that leaves the reactor.
The dried coal devolatilizes as it descends through the carbonization zone. The devolatilized
coal is then gasified by reaction with steam and carbon dioxide in the gasification zone. In the
bottom zone, oxygen reacts with the remaining char to produce heat by oxidation which drives
the endothermic gasification reactions. In the dry ash mode of operation, excess steam is
injected with the oxygen so that the temperature is maintained below the ash fusion temperature.
The feed coal moisture controls the gas discharge temperature. For high moisture lignite, the
raw gas temperature is ~ 315°C whereas for a low moisture bituminous coal, it is ~ 540°C. The
raw gas leaving the reactor is directly quenched with recycle water to condense the tars and oils.
2-1

The moving bed gasifiers have high cold gas efficiencies, low oxygen requirements and a raw
gas of relatively high methane content. Tars and oils are also produced as byproducts. There is
only a limited ability to process fines in the top feed however briquettes or pellets of fine coal
can sometimes be used. A diagram of the Sasol-Lurgi gasifier is shown below in Figure 2-1
Figure 2-1
Sasol-Lurgi Moving Bed Dry Ash Gasifier
Source: Lurgi GmbH
For bituminous caking coals a distributor and stirrer are included to maintain symmetrical gas
flow and break up any agglomerates. For non-caking coals e.g. the South African coals used at
Sasol, the there is no need for the distributor stirrer.
2-2

Additional Gasification Technologies
The main limitations of the Sasol Lurgi gasifier are the need to use only sized coal and the by
production of tars, oils and phenols that create challenges for safe clean operation and
maintenance.

There are many other gasification technologies being developed by various organizations in
several countries. These are listed in Table 2-1 together with a brief description of their current
status and size.

2-4

Additional Gasification Technologies
British Gas Lurgi (BGL)
British Gas and Lurgi developed a slagging version of the Lurgi moving bed gasifier (BGL).
The initial work on a 100 ton/day 3 ft diameter unit was conducted at the British Gas Midlands
research center near Birmingham 1958-1964. Further development work was conducted at the
Westfield center in Scotland with a 350 ton/day 1.8 m (6 ft.) diameter unit from 1975-1984 and
was followed with a 500 ton/day 2.3 m (8 ft) diameter, unit from 1986 to 1990. A variety of US
coals were run in the 500 ton/day unit. A 12 ft. inside diameter unit is in commercial use at the
SVZ plant in Schwarze Pumpe, Germany gasifying a mixture of various wastes and the local
lignite. (See also Chapter 4.)
Sized coal is introduced through lock hoppers in the same manner as for the dry ash Lurgi
gasifier. The raw gas leaving the reactor is similarly directly quenched with recycle water to
condense the tars and oils. However, in the slagging version, there is no bottom grate and
instead steam and oxygen is introduced through a series of tuyeres at the bottom of the gasifier
and molten slag is removed from a pool in the base of the gasifier.
There is only a limited ability to process fines in the top feed however, as with the dry ash type,
briquettes or pellets of fine coal can be used. The slagging version also offers the opportunity to
recycle the tars and oils to extinction and the gasification of coal fines through introduction in the
tuyeres can be practiced. A diagram of the BGL gasifier is shown in Figure 2-2.
Figure 2-2
British Gas Lurgi (BGL) Gasifier
2-5

A high pressure (70 barg), 1.2 m diameter, 200 tpd, version of the BGL gasifier was tested at
Westfield, Scotland in the late 1980’s. As expected, this showed a higher yield of methane than
the previously tested lower pressure units.
The Allied Syngas Corporation located in Wayne, Pennsylvania has an exclusive license to
commercialize the BGL process in North America. Their business model is to provide syngas
“over the fence” for a variety of industrial and electric refueling applications. The standard
commercial model “BGL 1000” is to produce a nominal 1000 Million Btu/hour of clean medium
Btu syngas from
~ 1000 tons/day of coal.
Advantica (UK) and Envirotherm Gmbh (Germany) are joint licensors of the BGL technology
outside the US. A 2.3 m diameter BGL gasifier has been supplied to a client in China with long
term experience in fixed bed gasification. The feed will be low rank coal and the existing gas
processing train will handle the products. It is scheduled for commissioning in late 2006.
Siemens (formerly Future Energy/GSP/Noell)
This process was first developed by Deutsches Brennstoffinstitut Freiberg for the gasification of
the local lignite and other solid fuels. Starting in 1985, a 700 mt/day dry coal fed down flow
entrained flow reactor lined with a refractory covered water cooling screen was used for the
gasification of the local salty lignite. This dry coal fed technology was previously described in
the literature as the GSP (Gaskombinat Schwarze Pumpe) technology. For a while it was
marketed by Babcock Borsig Power (BBP) as the Noell technology under a non-exclusive
license. FutureEnergy Gmbh took over the gasification know-how from BBP including the
various Noell entrained gasifier designs and the test facilities and pilot plants in Freiberg in 2002.
In 2005, Sustec Technologies (Switzerland) became the 100% owner of Future Energy and of the
former SVZ Schwarze-Pumpe plant.

Siemens future plans also call for further scaling up the gasifier to 1000 MWth at the Schwarze

Figure 2-3
Siemens (former Future Energy/GSP/Noell) Gasifier
The gasifier sketch shown in Figure 2-3 shows a spirally wound cooling screen used for ash
containing solid feedstock’s (coal) and also liquids such as tar or residual oils. The cooling
screen is covered with a SiC castable and a layer of molten slag is deposited. The gasifier can
use either a partial or total quench depending on the application. A reactor with a cooling wall
for use with very low or zero ash is also offered.
Mitsubishi Heavy Industries (MHI)
A 250 MW IGCC demonstration project based on the scale-up of the 200 mt/day Mitsubishi
gasifier tested at Nakoso, Japan in 1989-94 to ~ 1700 mt/day is being constructed in Japan. A
consortium of all the major Japanese power utility companies (the Clean Coal Power R&D
Company) is providing sponsorship with additional major financial support from METI
(Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry). Construction is underway and start up is scheduled
for late 2007. The gasifier will be air blown with the addition of some enriched air from a
cryogenic ASU that supplies primarily nitrogen for coal conveying, purging and start
up/shutdown. The syngas is projected to have a heating value of 1100 kj/NM3 (135 Btu/SCF).
There will be a syngas cooler, hot gas filter and cold gas cleanup using COS hydrolysis and
MDEA for AGR removal. The Mitsubishi gas turbine, a 701 DA, is of an earlier vintage than
the F class and has a firing temperature of ~1200°C and ~130 MW output. The HRSG will
include an SCR unit. The design heat rate is 8125 Btu/kWh LHV basis or 42% efficiency. The
low emissions targets are 8 ppmv SO , 5 ppmv NO and particulate matter < 4 mg/Nm3.
2 x
2-8

Additional Gasification Technologies
The gasifier is a two stage upflow dry coal fed design with a membrane wall and no refractory.
The bottom first stage operates in combustion mode and only coal is supplied to the second
stage. Char from the cyclone and hot gas filter will be recycled. The top outlet temperature has
been shown as 704°C (1300°F) and at this temperature there is some concern about the possible
existence of tars that could foul the downstream equipment. However, in the flowsheets shown
in the public presentations, there are no extra provisions for treating such a possible eventuality.
Figure 2-4
MHI Dry Coal Fed Two-Stage Entrained Flow Gasifier
Source: S.Kaneko – Clean Coal Power R&D Co.Ltd
EAGLE (JPower – Japan)
The EAGLE gasifier is an oxygen-blown dry coal fed two stage upflow entrained flow reactor
with a membrane wall. A 150 mt/day pilot plant gasifier has been built at the Wakamatsu
Research Facility of JPower (formerly the Electric Power Development Corporation (EPDC)) in
Japan. A smaller pilot plant ~ 50 mt/day of similar design was tested earlier in the 1990’s under
the name of HYCOL. A test program on the 150 mt/day was started in March 2002 with funding
support from METI and NEDO. The first stage operates in oxygen rich mode at ~1600°C.
Unlike other two stage gasifiers the two stages are not separated by a narrower throat. Instead,
the two stages are really two levels of feed injectors angled to promote residence time and
mixing. The upper part (second stage) operates in a lean oxygen mode fed with coal and
recycled char. There is a throat section above the upper injectors where recycle gas is introduced
to reduce the raw gas temperature below the ash softening temperature. The pilot plant also has
a syngas cooler, cyclone and hot gas filter for particulate removal, water scrub, COS hydrolysis
and an MDEA AGR section. There is a small 8 MW gas turbine and the facility is intended to
test Solid Oxide Fuel Cells in a later phase. The gasifier testing seems to have been fairly
successful and a scale up to 1000-1500 mt/day is being discussed.
A sketch of the EAGLE gasifier is shown in Figure 2-5.
2-9

Figure 2-5
EAGLE (JPower) Gasifier
ECUST (China) Coal/Water Slurry
The East China University of Science & Technology (ECUST) has developed it own version of a
slurry fed downflow entrained gasifier. It differs from the GE/Texaco design by having four
horizontally opposed side entry fuel injectors at the top of the reactor. A better carbon
conversion is claimed as a result of intense mixing and the fact that the direction of fuel injection
is not directed at the gasifier exit throat. Modeling suggests that for a single injector directed
downwards to the throat some of the incompletely converted coal will exit before participating in
the back mixing circulation up the walls of the gasifier.
In 2000, a 22 mt/day testing started on a coal /water slurry fed downflow entrained flow pilot
gasifier equipped with multi opposed fuel injectors. A commercial gasifier of 750 mt/day at
6.5 MPa started up in December 2004 at the Hua-La Heng-Sheng Chemical Co. in Dezhou,
Shandong. A 700 hour run ended in July 2005 with refractory, injectors and quench ring
reportedly in good condition. Through August 2006, there had been a total of 6600 hours
operation. The claims include a syngas yield 9% higher than GE (Texaco) at same oxygen
consumption and 98% carbon conversion versus GE ~95%.
Another commercial project has two ECUST gasifiers rated at 1150 mt/day at 4 MPa started up
in October 2005 at the Yonkuang Cathy Coal Chemical Co. This project claims to be the first
coal based polygeneration plant in China producing 240,000 mtpy of Methanol and 71.8 MW of
power.
2-10

Additional Gasification Technologies
The ECUST flow scheme shown in Figure 2-6 is taken from a presentation made at the 2006
Gasification Technologies conference. Besides the four opposed horizontal burners there are
several other interesting differences from the GE/Texaco flow scheme. The raw quenched
syngas goes through a cyclone prior to the water scrubber thereby considerably reducing the
particulate loading in the scrubber. Direct heat exchange between the grey water and the vapor
from the high pressure flash of the slag water results in high heat recovery efficiency and
stability in the slag water treatment section.
Figure 2-6
ECUST Presentation at Gasification Technologies Conference 2006
Source: ECUST Presentation at Gasification Technologies Conference 2006
ECUST (China) Dry Coal Fed
The ECUST program is also in the process of developing a dry coal fed entrained gasifier with a
membrane wall. A dry coal fed pilot plant gasifier was tested towards the end of 2004. In June
2005, a 48-hour test run using CO as transfer medium (not Nitrogen) was conducted at 3.0 MPa
2
and 1350°C. It showed an improved syngas yield over the use of Nitrogen.
A 1200 t/d gasifier is planned at Tengzhou Lunhua Phoenix Fertilizer Co. in Shandong Province.
A new 30-40 t/d pilot gasifier with membrane water wall is to be built at Yankuang Lunan
Fertilizer plant with a planned start up in 2006.
2-11

China GreenGen Project - Dry Coal Fed Entrained Flow Gasifier
The Thermal Power Research Institute (TPRI) is funded by the 5 major Chinese Power
Companies. TPRI is planning a longer term GreenGen project somewhat similar in concept to
the US FutureGen. The gasifier concept shown in the GreenGen presentation seems in some
respects to borrow from the JPower EAGLE concept .i.e. dry coal fed two stage upflow entrained
gasifier with a membrane wall. The plan calls for 1000 mt/day gasifier to be constructed starting
2006 for a 2008 startup, a 2500 mt/day gasifier in 2011 as part of a 300 MW IGCC in 2012 then
a 3000 mt/day gasifier in 2017 as part of a 400 MW IGCC in 2018. Incidentally, Huaneng (the
largest of the five Coal Power companies) also joined the US FutureGen Consortium in October
2005.
Pratt & Whitney Rocketdyne
The Rocketdyne division of Boeing has been developing the concept of a dry coal fed single
stage downflow entrained gasifier at 1000 psig pressure. In the 1980’s, Rocketdyne was
involved in a DOE funded program for a high pressure hydrogasifier aimed at high methane
production. The team has developed concepts for the high pressure feeding and intensive
injector mixing based on their knowledge of rocket technology. This technology and the
engineering team were acquired by Pratt & Whitney from Boeing in 2005. Initial tests of the
proposed liner have been completed at CANMET’s laboratories. An 18 stpd pilot is to be
constructed at GTI’s facilities in Illinois to demonstrate a single injector in a full length gasifier.
A 400 stpd cold flow test facility is being constructed at EERC in North Dakota to test the flow
splitter concept and later to test the dry solids feed pump. The proposed 400 stpd gasifier design
is only 1 ft. diameter and is claimed to be scaleable to 3000 stpd at 3 ft. diameter by replicating
injectors. The gasifier concept is illustrated in Figure 2-7.
Figure 2-7
Proposed Pratt & Whitney Rocketdyne
Source: Pratt & Whitney Rocketdyne
2-12

Additional Gasification Technologies
KBR Transport Gasifier
The Kellogg, Brown and Root (KBR) transport gasifier is a fluid bed gasifier designed to operate
gas high velocities of 10-15 m/sec. with the objectives of higher throughputs, better mixing and
heat transfer. A KBR transport pilot plant that can feed 30-60 tons/day of coal and is capable of
operating either with air or oxygen mode is installed at the Power Systems Development Facility
(PSDF) in Wilsonville, Alabama. The test program, supported by the US DOE, Southern
Company, EPRI and others, was initiated in 1996.
The coal is fed through a lock hopper system into the mixing zone, together with steam and
either air or oxygen, where is also contacted with recirculated solids from the standpipe. The
gasifier operates at 140-240 psig and the mixing zone temperature is in the range of 1500-
1875°F. Syngas and solids ascend into the riser then to a disengager where larger particles are
separated for recirculation. The gas from the disengager passes to a cyclone for further
separation of solids for recycle into the mixing zone via the standpipe and J-leg. The gas is
cooled by a fire-tube syngas cooler prior to removal of the fine particulates in a candle filter
operated at 400-500°C. A sketch of the KBR transport gasifier facility at Wilsonville is shown
in Figure 2-8.
Figure 2-8
KBR Transport Gasifier at the PSDF, Wilsonville, Alabama
Test runs on a variety of coals have been conducted on the gasifier at Wilsonville in both air
blown and oxygen blown mode. The use of lower rank coals of higher reactivity is advantageous
(e.g. PRB sub-bituminous and lignites) and with such coals carbon conversions of >95% have
been achieved.
2-13

A proposal for an air blown 285 MW IGCC project based on the KBR Transport gasifier was
submitted in 2004 by the Southern Company and Orlando (Florida) Utilities (OUC) in response
to the DOE’s second solicitation under the Clean Coal Power Initiative (CCPI). This proposal
was accepted and the contract with the DOE was completed in January 2006. 285 MW IGCC has
a divided ownership. The combined cycle based on a GE 7 FAe+ gas turbine will 100% owned
by OUC while the Gasifier Island will be jointly owned by 65% Southern and 35% OUC. The
design coal is PRB. KBR is responsible for Gasification Island EPC. DOE is providing 235 M$
of co-funding out of an estimated 585 M$ for the Gasification Island. All major contracts are in
place. OUC’s Need for Power application was approved by the Florida PUC May 24, 2006.
NEPA completion is expected in February 2007 and the FEED should be completed by the end
of the 1st quarter 2007.
Figure 2-9
KBR Summary Flow Diagram for Orlando IGCC Project
Source: Southern Company -T. Pinkston Presentation at Gasification Technologies
Conference 2006
The block flow diagram for the Orlando 285 MW IGCC is shown in Figure 2-9. Some air is
extracted from the 7 FAe+ gas turbine air compressor and boosted to supply some of the gasifier
air. Additional air is also supplied to the process air compressor. Fine coal is conveyed into the
gasifier via a lock hopper and nitrogen conveying system. The raw syngas enters a downflow
firetube syngas cooler for steam raising before it passes to the hot gas particulate filter. Recycled
syngas is used to back blow the filter and recover the particulate matter. After low temperature
2-14

Additional Gasification Technologies
cooling, COS hydrolysis, and Mercury removal, the syngas enters a CrystaSulf absorber for
recovery of elemental sulfur. The clean syngas is preheated and sent to the gas turbine. Sour
water recovered from the low temperature gas cooling is stripped and the ammonia is recovered
for use in the SCR. Both a CO catalyst bed and SCR are incorporated in the HRSG.
HT Winkler Gasifier
The Winkler process was an atmospheric pressure fluid bed gasification process that was first
introduced in Germany in the 1920’s. Several commercial plants were built around the world
through to the early 1960’s. The gasifier was a refractory lined vessel and coal (sized < 10mm)
was typically fed via a screw conveyor. The operating temperature was maintained below the
ash melting temperature.
The so-called “High Temperature Winkler” process was developed by Rheinbraun in the 1970’s
for the purpose of gasification at higher pressures up to 30 bar thereby improving output and
reducing compression requirements for syngas in most end use applications such as IGCC or for
synthesis. A 600mt/day oxygen blown demonstration plant at Berrenrath, Germany operated
from 1986 to 1997 providing syngas for methanol production. For the last 10years of operation,
it achieved an availability of 84%. The plant included a syngas cooler and hot gas ceramic
candle filter. A plant of similar size was also built in Oulu, Finland to gasify peat for ammonia
production.
A 160 mt/day pilot plant at a higher pressure of 25 bar (more suitable for the IGCC application)
was built at Wesseling, Germany and a test program of both air and oxygen gasification was
conducted from 1989 to 1992. The parent company of Rheinbraun RWE (Rheinische
Westphalien Electricidadswerke) indicated an interest in a larger IGCC demonstration project
(KOBRA) based on air blown HT Winkler gasifiers, however this project did not proceed
beyond the design stage. More recently interest was shown in the possibility of installing two
1000 ton/day HT Winkler units at the IGCC facility in Vresova in the Czech Republic, where,
potentially, they might replace the 26 existing Lurgi fixed bed gasifiers. However, this project
has yet to materialize. A diagram of the HT Winkler gasifier is shown in Figure 2-10.
2-15

Figure 2-10
HT Winkler Gasifier
Kellogg, Rust Westinghouse (KRW) Gasifier
The KRW fluid gasification process is based on the concept of a higher temperature zone at the
base of the gasifier where the ash reaches its softening point and achieves limited agglomeration
such that the heavier agglomerates can be removed from the base. In this manner, a higher
carbon conversion can be obtained than would be achievable if operated in a non –
agglomerating mode.
The process was developed at a 20 ton/day pilot plant in Waltz Mill, PA with funding from
DOE, GRI and Westinghouse from the mid 1970’s to the mid 1980’s. An air blown 100 MW
IGCC plant based on this technology was built at Sierra Pacific Power Company’s Tracy station
near Reno, Nevada as one of the projects funded under the DOE Clean Coal Demonstration
Program. Coal tests started in 1998 but at the end of the 42 month DOE test program the gasifier
had yet to deliver syngas to the GE 6FA combined cycle. Problems with the gasifier refractory
and the hot gas filter were some of the main problems encountered. The project was shut down
in 2001.
A sketch of the KRW gasifier is shown in Figure 2-11.
2-16

Additional Gasification Technologies
Figure 2-11
Kellogg Rust Westinghouse (KRW) Ash Agglomerating Fluid Bed Gasifier
GTI (formerly IGT) U-GAS Gasifier
Another ash agglomerating fluid bed gasifier was piloted by the Institute of Gas Technology
(IGT) at their Chicago, Illinois facility in the early 1980’s. It operated at about 4 bar pressure.
Four U-GAS gasifiers each of about 100 ton/day capacity, also at 4 bar pressure, were supplied
to Shanghai Coking and Chemical Company in the 1990’s but are no longer in operation. A high
carry over of fines was one of the major problems encountered. The technology is also offered
by Carbona of Finland.
In August 2006, Synthesis Energy Systems reached agreement with GTI (formed from GRI and
IGT) on a global exclusive license for U-GAS for all coals and coal/biomass blends for a 30 year
term. A U-GAS gasifier capable of providing 28,000 nm3/hr of syngas as feed for a 100,000
mtpy Methanol plant (~450 tpd coal?) is scheduled for a July 2007 start up at the Hai Hua
Coking and Chemical plant in Shandong, China.
2-17

HRL Integrated Drying & Gasification Combine Cycle (IDGCC)
In the 1990’s, the Herman Research Laboratories (HRL) in Victoria, Australia initiated work on
an integrated coal drying and gasification process particularly aimed at more efficient use of the
very high moisture content (up to 65%) brown coals of the Latrobe Valley. The gasifier is a
fluid bed gasifier similar to the HT Winkler and the basic concept is to use the hot syngas from
the gasifier to dry the incoming coal in an entrained upflow dryer. Brown coal is fed to the dryer
via lock hoppers. The moist (and partially cooled gas) from the dryer then passes to a gas filter
and the cleaned gas is fired in a gas turbine combined cycle. The Latrobe Valley brown coals are
very low in sulfur and in Australia there are no regulations regarding SO emissions. A 10 MW
2
development unit was built at Morwell in the Latrobe Valley and tested 1996-7. The gas turbine
used was an EGT Typhoon of ~5 MW. Air was extracted from the gas turbine compressor to
supply the gasifier. HRL is pursuing opportunities to scale up the technology.
Hydromax Molten Metal Gasification
Hydromax is a cyclic process using gasification in molten metal bath. The inventor and patent
holder is Alchemix Corporation of Arizona. Diversified Energy is a Hydromax licensee that
represents Hydromax to DOE and other government agencies.
The process envisages the use of two or more molten metal bath smelters operated in a cyclical
manner. The molten metal is an alloy of iron and tin. The coal mineral matter forms a slag layer
in the bath that must be periodically removed. The two parts of the cyclic process are the
reaction of steam and oxygen with Iron to produce hydrogen and iron oxide,
Fe + HO = FeO + H (Endothermic)
2 2
Fe + 1/2O = FeO (Exothermic)
2
followed by the reduction of the iron oxide back to elemental iron, with the production of syngas.
FeO + Coal = Fe + H + CO (Endothermic)
2
Coal + O = H O + CO (Exothermic)
2 2
A 0.3m pilot plant reactor has been used for some batch experiments at CSIRO in Australia. A
presentation on this process was made at the 2006 Gasification Technologies Conference.
2-18

3
GASIFICATION FUNDAMENTALS AND TECHNOLOGY
SELECTION
Gasification Process Selection
The best gasification technology for a given application will depend on the project objectives,
the intended products, the range of coal or other feedstocks to be used and the size of gasifier
necessary to provide economically competitive products. The type of gasifier most suitable for a
given product or feedstock may not be available in a commercial size that would yield an
economically competitive product. The range of objectives and products include:
• Hydrogen
• Methanol or Dimethyl Ether (DME)
• Ammonia
• Fischer Tropsch (F-T) Liquids
• Power Only Without CO Capture
2
• Power Only With CO Capture
2
• Power and Co-production of Hydrogen, F-T, etc.
• Lowest Cost of Electricity (COE)
• Lowest Dispatch Cost
If the application is for hydrogen production or for synthesis of ammonia, methanol or Fischer
Tropsch liquids the preferred syngas would comprise mostly carbon monoxide and hydrogen.
If methane is present in the syngas it does not participate in the shift and subsequent synthesis
reactions and represents a lower yield of product and an extra, essentially inert, volume load that
is an economic detriment. This is particularly the case where the synthesis reaction is conducted
in a recycle loop to increase the conversion of CO + H and where inerts such as methane or
2
nitrogen would build up to unacceptable levels.
If the application is IGCC for power only without CO capture the presence of methane is
2
certainly acceptable and may lead to a higher efficiency and lower oxygen consumption.
However, if the objective was for power only but for maximum CO capture then the presence of
2
methane in the syngas reduces the amount of coal carbon that can be captured by the shift plus
CO capture route.
2
3-1

Generally, gasification at a higher pressure is advantageous for CO capture since a physical
2
absorption system such as Selexol can be used. With physical absorption, much of the CO can
2
be recovered by pressure reduction at small energy penalty; however, with a chemical absorption
such as MDEA the absorbed CO must be removed mostly by steam stripping of the rich solvent
2
at higher energy penalty. However, ironically, if the gasification process produces methane the
yield of methane increases with pressure so that the amount of coal carbon available for capture
is even less. The effect of pressure on methane yield is illustrated in Figure 3-1.
Figure 3-1
Methane Formation with Pressure
Source: R.A.Meyers Ed. Handbook of Synfuels Technology, Part 3, McGraw-Hill, New York.
1984 as included in Chapter 18 of “Steam – Its Generation and Use” Edition 41, The Babcock &
Wilcox Company 2005.
Gasification Chemistry and Reactions
The following reactions are important in coal gasification:
3-2

Gasification Fundamentals and Technology Selection
• Coal Devolatilization = CH + CO + CO + Oils + Tars + C (Char)
4 2
• C + O = CO ((exothermic – rapid)
2 2
• C + 1/2O = CO (exothermic – rapid)
2
• C + HO = CO + H (endothermic – slower than oxidation)
2 2
• C + CO = 2CO (endothermic – slower than oxidation)
2
• CO + HO = CO + H Shift Reaction(slightly exothermic – rapid)
2 2 2
• CO + 3H = CH + H O Methanation (exothermic)
2 4 2
• C + 2H = CH Direct Methanation (exothermic)
2 4
The first six of these reactions are the most important in the entrained gasification processes used
in the current IGCC plants. Methane formation is more evident in lower temperature systems.
High pressures and lower temperatures favor the methanation reactions. However, in most cases
the methane content is higher than would be predicted by equilibrium because methane is also
formed during the primary devolatilization of the coal (this methane has sometimes been called
“prompt” methane).
Under the reducing conditions of gasification, the sulfur in the coal is converted primarily to
hydrogen sulfide, H S, with ~3-10% of the sulfur converting to carbonyl sulfide, COS. This
2
typically necessitates the use of a COS hydrolysis reactor to convert the COS to HS prior to HS
2 2
removal by well known solvent absorption processes widely used in the gas processing and
petroleum industries. Gasification conditions favor the conversion of fuel bound nitrogen to
gaseous nitrogen and ammonia, NH . Higher temperatures favor the further destruction of
3
ammonia to nitrogen and hydrogen so that the ammonia content of the raw syngas is primarily a
function of the gasifier outlet temperature. Small amounts of HCN are also formed but may be
removed in the COS hydrolysis reactor. Tars, oils, and phenols survive in the lower temperature
outlets of moving bed gasifiers and these species contain some of the fuel’s oxygen, nitrogen,
and sulfur as more complex molecules.
Gasification Processes
Three major types of gasification are used today—moving bed, fluidized bed, and entrained
flow. These processes are illustrated in Figure 3-2.
Pressurized gasification is preferred for IGCC to avoid large auxiliary power losses for
compression of the syngas up to gas turbine inlet pressure. Most gasification processes currently
in use or planned for IGCC applications are oxygen blown.
Since synthesis reactions and economics are generally improved by higher pressure pressurized
gasification is also favored for the synthesis application.
3-3

Figure 3-2
The Three Major Types of Gasification Processors
As mentioned above some methane is produced is produced by decomposition of the coals
volatile matter. Some coals may also have methane surface absorbed within the pores. The
survival of this methane in the final product gas depends on the temperature and kinetics. At
lower gasifier outlet temperatures more of this methane survives. Since both moving bed
gasifiers (e.g.Sasol- Lurgi, BGL) and fluid bed gasifiers (HT Winkler, KBR Transport, GTI U
Gas) have lower outlet temperatures (below the ash slagging temperatures) than the single stage
entrained gasifiers (Texaco, Shell, Siemens/Future Energy) the syngas from these gasifiers is of
much higher methane content, representing typically 10-15% of the coal’s carbon content at 28-
35 Barg (400-500 psig). It must also be noted that because of their operating temperatures fluid
3-4

Gasification Fundamentals and Technology Selection
bed gasifiers also have lower carbon conversion and their ash contains more carbon that the slag
from slagging gasifiers.
The production of methane from the methanation reactions shown above is favored by higher
pressure as shown in Figure 3-1. Some actual data on the effect of pressure can be seen in Table
3-1 which shows the syngas compositions from several moving bed and fluid bed gasifiers.

In single stage entrained flow gasifiers, the fine coal particles react with the concurrently flowing
steam and oxygen. Residence time is very short (a few seconds) and the operating temperature is
above the ash fusion temperature to ensure destruction of tars and oils and to achieve high carbon
conversion. The methane content is very low but it can be monitored as an indication of gasifier
temperature. Entrained gasifiers have relatively high oxygen requirements and the raw gas has
high sensible heat content. The various designs of entrained flow gasifiers differ in their feed
systems (dry pneumatic or coal/water slurries), vessel containment for the hot conditions
(refractory or membrane water wall), and configurations for recovery of the sensible heat from
the raw gas.
Some gasifier designs use two stages to improve gasifier cold gas efficiency and to reduce the
sensible heat in the raw gas and to lower the oxygen requirements. In a two stage entrained
gasifier, the coal fed to the second stage reduces the outlet temperature and there is some
methane survival in the syngas. The methane content will increase if a higher proportion of the
coal is fed to the second stage. The typical syngas compositions from several entrained
gasification processes are shown in Table 3-2.
3-5

2nd Stage
Pressure Barg (PSIG) 69 (1000) 34 (500) 31(450) 31(450) 69(1000)
H 37 28 33 30 32
2
