2
Entrained-flow gasifiers have been selected for the majority of commercial sized IGCC project
applications. These include the coal/water-slurry-fed processes of GE (formerly Texaco) (Cool
Water, and Tampa, US) and ConocoPhillips E-Gas (formerly Global, Destec, Dow) (Dow,
Plaquemine, and Wabash plant, USA) and the dry-coal-fed processes of Shell (Buggenum,
Netherlands), Prenflo Krupp-Uhde (Puertollano, Spain), Siemens/Future Energy ( formerly GSP
and Noell) (Schwarze Pumpe, Germany), and Mitsubishi (Nakoso, Japan). The Mitsubishi and
E-GAS processes employ two stages of gasification. The atmospheric pressure Koppers-Totzek
process was developed in the 1950s, and commercial units operated in Greece, Turkey, India,
South Africa, Zambia, and elsewhere, mostly for ammonia manufacture. A major advantage of
the high-temperature entrained-flow gasifiers is that they avoid tar formation and its attendant
problems. Their syngas has little methane and is very suitable for hydrogen and synthesis gas
products. The high reaction rate also allows single gasifiers to be built with large gas outputs
sufficient to fuel the large commercial gas turbines now entering the marketplace. The IGCC
projects based on petroleum residuals, all use entrained downflow refractory lined gasifiers from
either GE/Texaco or Shell. There are also over a hundred of these heavy oil gasifiers in
operation worldwide for the manufacture of ammonia, methanol, hydrogen and other chemicals.
Methane Formation in Gasifiers – Trade-offs and Ironies
Methane in the syngas correlates with higher cold gas efficiency and lower oxygen usage. In an
IGCC, higher cold gas efficiency means that more of the coal’s energy (as syngas) can be used in
the more efficient Brayton gas turbine cycle rather than as sensible heat into the less efficient
Rankine steam cycle.
However, methane in the syngas reduces the percentage of the coal’s carbon that can be captured
via the Shift reaction and subsequent CO removal. There are some limits, both economic and
2
thermodynamic, with regard to how far to take the shift reaction. Most of the CO conversion
(80-85%) can be achieved by high temperature shift. Additional CO conversion can be obtained
in subsequent low temperature shift reactors at increasing energy penalties for each mole of CO
converted.
3-6

Gasification Fundamentals and Technology Selection
Increased pressure further increases methane production and gasifier efficiency and further
reduces oxygen usage. Increased pressure for methane producing gasifiers therefore also further
reduces the percentage of the coal’s carbon that can be captured.
However, gasification at a higher pressure is economically advantageous for CO capture since a
2
physical absorption system such as Selexol can be used. With Selexol physical absorption, much
of the CO can be recovered by pressure reduction to intermediate pressures 3-20 barg pressure
2
(50-300 psig) with considerable savings in CO compression costs, auxiliary power usage and
2
steam consumption. On the other hand, with a chemical absorption system such as those based
on MDEA (MethylDiethanolamine), the absorbed CO must be removed mostly by steam
2
stripping of the rich solvent at much higher energy penalty.
Gasifier Selection for IGCC without CO Capture
2
In the absence of any requirement or regulation for CO capture, the selection for IGCC is more
2
open to all types of gasifier and would be evaluated presumably based on the traditional criteria
of COE and perception of risk and availability. Methane in the syngas is an enhancement to the
syngas heating value. In this respect, the methane producing gasifiers, such as fluid and moving
beds and two stage entrained systems, with higher cold gas efficiencies may show some
advantage. However, each technology must be evaluated on its total characteristics rather than
efficiency alone.
The choice will also be markedly affected by coal type and low rank coals may show better
performance in fluid bed gasifiers than in entrained systems at their current stage of
development.
Gasifier Selection for Hydrogen, Maximum CO Capture and Synthesis
2
If the overall project goal is >90% capture of the coal’s carbon and production of Hydrogen
(e.g. the stated FutureGen project goals), then a single stage entrained gasifier at high pressure
(69 barg (1000 psig)) operating with water Quench would be the preferred technology.
Single stage entrained gasifiers operate at high temperatures (1300-1600°C) so that very little
methane is produced. Nearly all the coal’s carbon is in the syngas as CO and CO and is
2
amenable to capture through the shift and CO removal process steps. The quench mode is the
2
least cost method of putting the moisture in the raw syngas needed for the Shift reaction. Other
configurations would add expensive syngas coolers for steam production and/or rob the steam
cycle. Economic evaluations of the competing gasification technologies with and without CO
2
capture using bituminous coals show a significant COE advantage over PC plants when capture
is required. There is also a particular advantage to high-pressure gasification operation. For the
current lower pressure (31-36 Barg (450-500 psig)) E Gas and Shell IGCCs, there is a lower
advantage over PC plants with amine scrubbing than with the higher pressure Texaco Quench
IGCC. (See also Chapter 8 Economics)
If the application is for hydrogen production or for synthesis of ammonia, methanol or Fischer
Tropsch liquids, the preferred syngas should comprise mostly carbon monoxide and hydrogen.
3-7

If methane is present in the syngas, it does not participate in the shift and subsequent synthesis
reactions and represents a lower yield of product and an extra, essentially inert, volume load that
is an economic detriment. This is particularly the case where the synthesis reaction is conducted
in a recycle loop to increase the overall conversion of CO + H and where inerts such as methane
2
or nitrogen would build up to unacceptable levels.
Entrained Gasifier Improvements
Each of the major entrained coal gasification technologies have several candidate improvements
that could be potentially be demonstrated at FuturGen consistent with the currently stated goals.
Slurry fed gasifiers (GE/Texaco, E Gas) have the advantage of being able to pump the feed to
high pressures. The energy used in pumping is very much less than the energy used in
conveying gas compression. Some schemes have been suggested to use slurry pumping to attain
high pressures and with additional flash drying to give a dry coal feed with the flashed steam
added back to the syngas downstream of the gasifier to provide the syngas moisture for the shift
reaction. Another alternative is to feed the coal as a coal in liquid CO slurry either with or
2
without a flash step. Such improvements could be particularly advantageous for the abundant,
and low cost, low rank coals such as Powder River Basin (PRB) sub-bituminous coals.
Some improvements more specifically related to the Texaco process are improved carbon
conversion per pass (possibly in conjunction with a new improved feed system, and/or larger
volume gasifiers to give more residence time) and a continuous slag let down system to eliminate
the lock hoppers. The replacement of the carbon scrubber by a warm or hot gas filter could
markedly reduce O&M costs associated with the black and gray water circuits around the
scrubber and to improve plant availability. Use of a cooled screen instead of a refractory lining
for the gasifier could also reduce forced outages and improve availability.
A higher pressure E-Gas gasifier of a tall cylindrical design, ESTR (Entrained slagging
Transport) (perhaps with two diameters) has been suggested. If demonstrated this would
probably avoid the size constraint imposed by the current inverted T (sometimes called the iron
cross) design for low rank coals and could enable lower cost CO capture through use of a
2
physical absorption system such as Selexol. Although there is methane production from the
second stage feed, the two stage gasifiers could be run with only minor coal slurry fed to the
second stage and with additional water as quench to achieve the lower outlet temperature and to
produce a syngas of low methane consistent with the needs for hydrogen, high CO capture and
2
for synthesis applications. Operating in this manner could also eliminate the need for a syngas
cooler. E-Gas already incorporates a continuous slag removal process and a hot gas filter in their
process.
The IEA GHG R&D sponsored study with Foster Wheeler Italiana showed that dry coal fed
entrained gasifiers such as Shell and GSP/NOELL become more expensive and less efficient as
long as they stay with the lock hopper pneumatic conveying feed systems. Higher pressures
mean more conveying nitrogen per unit of coal fed. A truly continuous high pressure coal
feeder, preferably on as-received coal or minimally dried coal, would be a marked improvement.
However, a conveying gas would still be required to move the pressurized coal into the gasifier
injectors. In CO capture and synthesis applications, the syngas is shifted and some of the CO is
2 2
3-8

Gasification Fundamentals and Technology Selection
removed. This captured CO can then also be used as the feed coal conveying gas reducing the
2
inert gas in the downstream clean up and synthesis and providing better economics. Perhaps
these organizations could also use their dry coal feed expertise to adopt something like a pumped
slurry flash system as feed at high pressure. Shell currently uses an expensive syngas cooler in
their solid fuel gasification process; however, for hydrogen production and synthesis they could
adopt a lower cost partial quench system. The Shell process already incorporates a cooling
screen (water wall) and a hot gas filter but would benefit from a continuous slag removal system.
Gasifier Sizes
Larger gasifiers would also produce significant economic improvements in most applications in
the current marketplace. Furthermore, future trends in the marketplace, as listed below, all point
the need for larger gasifiers to match the fuel requirements of the downstream equipment in a
rational multi-train manner:
• The larger more efficient gas turbines such as the GE H and Siemens 6000 G, when adapted
to the IGCC application will have higher fuel requirements
• The 50 Hz IGCC market requires gasifiers sized to meet the fuel needs of gas turbines which
are ~1.4 times those needed for 60 Hz turbines
• Economy of scale is very important for the Coal-to-Liquids (CTL) technology using Fischer
Tropsch (F-T) synthesis. Larger gasifiers able to match the syngas requirements of the large
F-T reactors (10,000-20,000 barrels/day) will require the gasification of 6000-12000 tons/day
of a typical bituminous coal
GE
Larger GE gasifiers of 34-51 m3 (1200 or 1800 ft3 (Tampa size)) at 1000 psig could supply
enough syngas for the future 60 Hz and 50 Hz gas turbines and reduce the number of gasifiers
needed to match the syngas requirements of large F-T reactors and thereby provide considerable
economies of scale. Such reactors could also be made with a higher L/D ratio to provide more
residence time while staying within the shipping diameter restrictions necessary for some plant
locations.
COP E-GAS
At the current design pressure of 41 barg (600 psig) for the COP E-Gas gasifiers there are
throughput limitations of ~ 4400 tpd PRB coal or 1950 MBtu/hr LHV syngas. Standard IGCC
design features “Full slurry quench” with ~ 30% coal fed to 2nd stage with consequent higher
methane content that inevitably limits carbon capture capability and suitability for most synthesis
applications. COP has also suggested a tall cylindrical design capable of design pressures up to
1000 psig that would operate with 100% feed to the second stage and with the recovered char fed
to the bottom first stage. COP has entitled this as an “Entrained Slagging Transport Reactor”
(ESTR). See Figure 1-7.
3-9

The proposed improved ESTR is currently envisaged as tall cylindrical design (perhaps of two
diameters). If operated in this mode the lower gasifier outlet temperature 700-760°C (1300-
1400°F) gives higher gasifier efficiency with higher methane content and reduced oxygen usage
but there would likely be an increased risk of tar survival. The high methane content of the
syngas from this mode of operation would not be suitable for high CO capture or for synthesis.
2
However, for CO capture, hydrogen production, Methanol and Fischer-Tropsch synthesis this
2
high pressure tall cylindrical design could be operated in the mode typically used at Wabash
(only 10% to the 2nd stage). The higher pressure results in reduced costs of CO capture and is
2
advantageous for hydrogen production and high pressure synthesis e.g. Ammonia. The higher
pressure also enables the gasifier to process higher feed rates of low rank coals including lignite.
The size would also then be suitable for the 50 Hz market and future larger gas turbines in both
50 and 60 Hz markets.
Shell
Shell believes that larger gasifiers can be built so that either two gasifiers supply three gas
turbines (~900MW) or, even further, that a single gasifier could supply two gas turbines
(~600MW). This would obviously reduce the capital cost significantly. If this can be
substantiated Shell would be in a somewhat better position than GE or COP to provide larger
gasifiers that could supply enough syngas for the future 60 Hz and 50 Hz gas turbines and which
would reduce the number of gasifiers needed to match the syngas requirements of large F-T
reactors and thereby provide considerable economies of scale.
Is 90% CO Capture Really Necessary?
2
Although it is a desirable goal, perhaps, given some of the inherent limitations of the technology
described above, it may not be necessary to achieve 90% capture in some situations. If 70-80%
capture was acceptable then the choice of gasifiers could extend beyond the single stage
entrained quench gasifiers to include the fluid bed and two stage entrained gasifiers with their
higher gasifier efficiencies, and high temperature shift alone could be sufficient.
Advanced Concept Power Blocks
These observations on CO capture limitations via the Shift/CO removal route apply to IGCC
2 2
configurations with a gas turbine combined cycle power block. Some advanced concepts have
been suggested that could potentially capture CO from the power block exhaust flue gas. For
2
such concepts there would be no limit on the acceptable fuel components (e.g. methane) in the
syngas.
The oxygen fired rocket engine proposed by Clean Energy Systems could be fired with clean
syngas of any composition and after condensing the moisture would result in a concentrated CO
2
exhaust stream that could be dried and compressed for transportation and sequestration. A 5
MW demonstration unit is being testing near Bakersfield, California with support from the US
DOE and Siemens.
3-10

Gasification Fundamentals and Technology Selection
Siemens has been developing a Solid Oxide Fuel Cell (SOFC) that incorporates an oxidizing
function (similar to the Oxygen Transfer Membranes (OTM) being developed by Air Products
and Praxair under the DOE program) for completing the combustion of the anode gas to CO and
2
moisture. The SOFC could potentially be supplied with clean syngas of any composition
(providing it was clean enough) and the resultant exhaust stream could be dried and compressed
for transportation and sequestration. A 250 kW SOFC with this OTM feature has been supplied
by Siemens to Shell Hydrogen at a Norwegian location. However, there has been little recent
news about this test unit.
Although such concepts could be tested at some scale on slip streams at a FutureGen facility, it is
rather unlikely that these technologies would have advanced enough to be considered for the
prime power block in any FutureGen project initiated in the next five years.
Gasifier Selection for Polygeneration or IGCC Co-Production
If the requirement for capture could be relaxed then a wider range of gasifiers could be used in a
polygeneration or co-production mode. The configuration could feature shift and CO removal
2
with hydrogen production from some of the syngas by Pressure Swing Absorption (PSA), and
power generation in a power block firing Hydrogen and methane with the PSA filtrate returned
as fuel to the power block either compressed and added to the main H2/CH4 fuel gas or duct
fired in the HRSG.
If some synthesis of Methanol, DME or F-T liquids is required then a once-thru Liquid Phase
LP synthesis reactor (e.g. Air Products design as used at Eastman) could be used and the
methane would then pass through and be part of the syngas fuel to the power block.
In Sasol’s large complex at Secunda in South Africa, the syngas from the Sasol Lurgi reactors is
cleaned up and fed to the F-T reactors. The methane in the syngas passes unchanged through the
reactors but after the F-T liquids are recovered the methane is removed from the F-T off gases by
cryogenic distillation and is recycled to a Steam Methane Reformer to produce more syngas feed
to the main F-T reactors. However, this is a rather special situation that arose from the specific
historical and technical context of the South Africa of the 1970s and early 1980s.
3-11

4
COAL-BASED IGCC DEMONSTRATION PLANTS
Summary of IGCC Demonstration Plant Status
Integrated Gasification Combined Cycle (IGCC) plants based on coal have been developed to
commercial size over the past two decades and have demonstrated that they can meet extremely
stringent air emission standards and are able to achieve high plant efficiencies. Commercial and
near commercial sized plants using IGCC technologies have now accumulated several years of
operating experience which are summarized in this Chapter 4.
The main challenges with regard to the widespread adoption of this technology are: (i)
demonstration of high availability equal to or better than existing direct Pulverized Coal (PC)
plants, and (ii) capital cost reduction to compete with state-of-the-art PC plants. The lessons
learned from operating problems experienced by the various technologies, the risks involved and
the potential improvements are discussed and summarized below.
There are currently two coal based IGCC commercial sized demonstration plant projects
operating in the US and two in Europe. The essential characteristics of these plants are shown in
Table 4-1.
Table 4-1

Spain (now marketed as
Shell)
The US projects were partially supported by the US Department of Energy’s (DOE) Clean Coal
Technology (CCT) demonstration program. The 262 MW Wabash River IGCC Repowering
project in Indiana started up in October 1995 and uses the E-Gas (now owned by ConocoPhillips
(COP)) gasification technology. Its demonstration period ended in January 2000 and since that
4-1

time it has operated mostly on petroleum coke. The 250 MW Tampa Electric Company (TEC)
IGCC project in Florida started up in September 1996 and is based on the GE/Texaco
gasification technology. Its demonstration period ended October 2001 and is currently operating
on coal/petroleum coke blends. The first of the European IGCC plants, the NUON (formerly
SEP/Demkolec) project at Buggenum, the Netherlands, based on the Shell gasification
technology started operations in early 1994 and since then has run a variety of imported coals. In
2002-3, it operated in load dispatch mode to the Dutch grid but in 2004, it reverted to operating
at base load. More recently it has been adding up to 20% various biomass to the coal feed. The
second European project, the ELCOGAS project in Puertollano, Spain, based on the Prenflo
gasification technology of Krupp-Uhde (since 2002 this has been marketed jointly by Shell and
Krupp-Uhde as the Shell technology). It started operations in early 1998 and since then has
operated on a coal/petroleum coke mixture (its design feed).
A third US DOE sponsored demonstration project, the 100 MW Sierra Pacific Piñon Pine project
in Nevada, based on the air blown KRW fluid bed gasification technology, started up in early
1998. However, it never delivered syngas to the gas turbine and the demonstration program was
terminated in December 2000 without the plant ever having operated as an IGCC.
The design and performance of the two US IGCC projects that have operated to date (Wabash
River and Tampa) will be reviewed and compared in some respects to the design and current
experience on the NUON project in the Netherlands and the ELCOGAS project in Spain.
The key design features of the four plants are summarized in Table 4-2. The projects are all
based on slagging entrained flow gasification. The US projects are coal/water slurry fed while
the European projects are dry coal fed.
All four plants have heat recovery from the raw syngas through use of syngas coolers raising
high pressure saturated steam that is sent the gas turbine HRSG for superheating.
The height of the structure is largely determined by the gasifer syngas cooler arrangements and
the slag removal system. In the Tampa plant, the structure height is determined by the vertical
arrangement of the slag lock hopper system, radiant syngas cooler and the gasifier. At the
NUON and ELCOGAS plant, the height is determined by the vertical arrangement of the slag
lock hopper system, gasifier, quench and syngas cooler. The height of the structure at Wabash is
lower because the E-Gas gasifier and syngas cooler are side by side and not vertically stacked
and the slag removal system is a continuous let down through a series of orifices that has much
lower vertical height requirements than the lock hopper systems at the other plants.
The Wabash, NUON and ELCOGAS plants have dry hot gas candle filters for recovery of the fly
slag. Since the dry coal fed gasifiers at NUON and ELCOGAS operate at higher temperatures
than slurry fed gasifiers the fly slag at these plants is of low carbon content and able to be sold
and is not recycled to the gasifier. In contrast, additional coal slurry is fed to the second stage of
the E-Gas gasifier without additional oxygen and this coal is only partially gasified so that the
filter catch is a mixture of char and fly slag that is recovered for recycle to the higher temperature
first stage of the gasifier. The Tampa plant uses a carbon scrubber for removal of the fine slag
which is recovered through high pressure settlers and filtration for recycle to the rod mills.
4-2

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
The US plants are based on General Electric ‘FA’ gas turbines with turbine inlet or firing
temperatures of nominally about 1260°C (2300°F) and equipped with multiple can combustors in
an annular arrangement. The European IGCC projects are both based on Siemens gas turbines
equipped with dual silo combustion chambers with turbine inlet temperatures of
nominally1100°C (Buggenum) and 1260°C (Puertollano). It should be noted that Siemens often
quotes an ISO firing temperature. This is a calculated number that can differ by 60°C or more
(lower) from the firing temperature as defined by GE. The actual firing temperatures currently
used may have been reduced from these nominal temperatures because of concerns (particularly
from GE) about the potential higher heat transfer to the first row blades when the flue gas is of
higher moisture content than for natural gas firing.
The GE turbo-expanders were designed to be able to take the extra mass flow for the syngas
IGCC application and the ASUs in the US plants each have their own dedicated main air
compressors. In contrast, the Siemens turbines were optimized for the natural gas application
and, in order to maintain a balance, use air extraction to supply the air to the ASU. This
ASU/gas turbine integration reduces the auxiliary power usage but lengthens start up times.
Gas turbines differ in output depending on the frequency of the electricity produced, since
combustion turbine rotors are usually designed for a specified tip speed. The US and half of
Japan operate at 60 Hz, and the GE 7FA gas turbine output in an IGCC application is currently
rated at about 197 MW. Europe, the other half of Japan, China, and many other countries
operate at 50 Hz, and in these countries the equivalent gas turbine would be a GE 9FA with an
output in the IGCC application of 285 MW.
The major component and overall design performance of the coal based IGCC plants, compared
with that achieved to date, is shown in Table 4-3.
The availability of these four plants since their start up is plotted in Figure 4-1. For reference
other IGCC plant data is also included in Figure 4-1 from the earlier CoolWater and Dow
Plaquemine projects and also from the Italian IGCC projects based on petroleum residual oils.
For all of the coal based plants the early years showed low availability. None have achieved the
targeted 85% availability. As would be expected the early experience with the IGCC projects
based on petroleum residuals has been better. The specific situations for each plant will be
described later in this chapter. Wabash shut down for business reasons from January 2004 until
June 2005.NUON operated on load dispatch in 2002-3 and both the Tampa and ELCOGAS
plants have had extensive outages for gas turbine overhauls. In summary, today's statistics show
single train IGCC plants some 5-7 % less reliable than pulverized coal plants. The projections
and implications of new designs are that this gap will close, but it is a concern for those planning
new plants. The latest NERC GADS data (for years 2000-2004 operation) shows average PC
equivalent availability was 84.9%. For PCs in the 200 - 299 MW size range (same as most of the
single train IGCCs which are operating) it was 85.5%. As shown in Figure 4-1, commercial data
for the first generation IGCC plants after a number of years is mostly less than 80%.

Availability Statistics for IGCC First-of-a Kind Plants
Lessons Learned from Coal-based IGCC Plants
MW Output
The gasifiers at each of the four plants were successfully scaled up from smaller sizes to
commercial size and have demonstrated that they can produce enough syngas to fully load the
gas turbines. In fact, in some cases the gasifiers probably have extra capacity but the syngas
production is limited by the ASU oxygen supply and other plant limitations.
The net output of an IGCC plant is determined by the gas turbine and steam turbine minus the
auxiliary power demand. Each of the gas turbines routinely deliver their design output for the
syngas IGCC application – 192 MW for the GE 7 FAs at Wabash and Tampa, and 155 and 200
MW (iso) for the Siemens turbines at NUON and ELCOGAS. The IGCC design net MW output
is routinely achieved at Tampa, NUON and ELCOGAS. It would also be achieved routinely at
Wabash if the feedwater heaters in the HRSG following the gas turbine were corrected to
perform as designed (a design fault that has nothing to do with the IGCC application).

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
Net Efficiency
When the ambient temperatures are at the design values the NUON and ELCOGAS plants
achieve their design net efficiencies. The Wabash plant would also routinely achieve its net
design efficiency if the HRSG feedwater heaters were corrected (as mentioned above) so that the
steam turbine could deliver its design output of 105 MW.
However, at Tampa, the net design efficiency has not been achieved due to a) the lower than
design carbon conversion per pass in the gasifier and b) the fouling of the gas/gas heat
exchangers (and their subsequent removal from service). These two issues are described more
fully in the ensuing reports on each individual IGCC plants’ experiences.
Integration
Steam Integration
In all four plants, high pressure boiler feedwater from the HRSG is sent to a syngas cooler
located at the gasifier outlet to raise high-pressure saturated steam that is then returned to the
HRSG for superheating. This steam side integration has functioned well and most of the planned
IGCC plant designs include this feature.
Air Separation Unit (ASU)/ Gas Turbine Integration
In the European IGCC plants at NUON and ELCOGAS, 100% of the air supply for the high
pressure ASU is taken as a bleed from the gas turbine compressor. In this way, the auxiliary
power consumption is lower since there is no separate ASU air compressor and the net efficiency
is higher. This aspect was initially attractive to European designers since they have historically
experienced higher coal costs than the US and where there is therefore a greater desire for
efficiency. This design also came about because of the particular features of the Siemens
turbines, that had basically been designed for the natural gas application, and that therefore
needed the integration to achieve a balance between the compressor and turbo expander gas mass
flows.
However, this integration feature means that on cold start up the gas turbine must be started on
the auxiliary fuel (distillate or natural gas), then air is extracted and cooled to start the ASU and
then when oxygen is produced the gasifier can be started. This leads to a lengthy start up period
during which the more expensive auxiliary fuel must be used. This adversely affects availability
and economics.
In the European plants, nitrogen from the ASU is heat exchanged against the air extracted from
the gas turbine and delivered to the gas turbine combustors to reduce NO formation and to
x
provide more motive mass to the gas turbine. Although the Tampa plant also has a high pressure
ASU, the nitrogen from which is also used in the gas turbine for NO control and motive power,
x
this ASU has its own separate air compressor that supplied 100% of its needs. However, starting
in July 2005, Tampa has taken some extraction air from the gas turbine compressor to
supplement the ASU air supply and to debottleneck the plant.
4-7

In contrast, the Wabash plant uses syngas moisturization and steam injection for gas turbine NO
x
control. The firing temperature has been reduced following GE’s current concern over higher
heat transfer to the first row blades with high moisture flue gas. The corresponding drop in
exhaust temperature was from 590°C (1095°F) to 576°C (1070°F). Wabash also has a
conventional pressure ASU with its own compressor supplying 100% of the ASU airflow.
The general consensus for future IGCC plants is that they should not be designed for 100 %
integration between the gas turbine and ASU. The ASU should have at least 50% of its air
supply from a dedicated air compressor so that the ASU can be independently started and kept
cold. There is still a lively debate about the most appropriate degree of ASU/gas turbine
integration. The permissible amount of air extraction will depend upon the individual gas
turbine compressor characteristics, and extraction may often have to be reduced at higher
ambient conditions and higher elevations. The overall gas turbine characteristics are one obvious
factor however as a generalization the European designers tend to favor somewhat higher
integration.
Environmental Performance
The IGCC plants have demonstrated that they can achieve very low air emissions for SO , NO,
2 x
and particulates. In addition, the costs of Mercury and CO removal from IGCC plants will be
2
much lower than from PC or CFBC plants. The various aspects of IGCC environmental
performance experience are discussed and documented below.
Sulfur Removal
All the IGCC plants have demonstrated low SO emissions below those of recently permitted
2
Pulverized Coal (PC) plants (0.10 lbs/ MBtu (123mg/Nm3). Each of the plants has a COS
hydrolysis unit followed by an Acid Gas Removal (AGR) process (MDEA at Wabash, Tampa
and ELCOGAS and Sulfinol M at NUON). With the higher CO content in the syngas from the
2
slurry fed gasifiers at Wabash and Tampa, the clean syngas contains ~50-60 ppmv of total sulfur
(equivalent to ~ 40 mg/Nm3 or ~0.03-0.04 lbs/MBtu). To take out more sulfur would require
higher removal of CO with subsequent penalties to performance and added costs to the sulfur
2
plant. For the lower CO content syngas from the dry coal fed gasifiers at ELCOGAS and
2
NUON the sulfur content of the syngas has been reported as <20 ppmv at NUON and 12 ppmv at
ELCOGAS. This latter figure is equivalent to <20 mg/Nm3 at 6% oxygen or <0.016 lbs/MBtu.
These results all indicate sulfur removals of over 99%.
For plants that employ the Rectisol process (e.g. the coal gasification plant at Eastman
Chemical), the sulfur removal is almost total with syngas sulfur content at < 1 ppmv.
NO Removal
x
Since fuel bound nitrogen (e.g. Ammonia) is removed from the syngas by water washing the
only IGCC NO emissions are thermal NO produced from syngas combustion in the gas turbine
x x
combustor and are controlled by modulating the peak flame temperature by dilution with
moisture and/or nitrogen. The GE 7 FA gas turbine at Tampa initially used nitrogen dilution
4-8

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
alone. The GE 7 FA at Wabash uses moisturization and steam injection. Both plants achieved
NO emissions of 20-25 ppmv at 15% oxygen, which is equivalent to 0.08-0.10 lbs/MBtu or 100-
x
125 mg/Nm3 at 6% oxygen. In 2002, Tampa added syngas moisturization to ~6-7% which has
reduced the NO emissions < 15 ppmv at 15% oxygen or ~0.06 lbs/MBtu. ELCOGAS uses both
x
syngas saturation and nitrogen dilution and has also reported NO emissions < 100 mg/Nm3 at
x
6% oxygen or 0.08 lbs/MBtu.
The GE 7 FA and Siemens V 94.3 have similar firing temperatures so the NO emission would
x
be expected to be similar. However, the firing temperature of the Siemens V 94.2 at NUON is
lower and therefore, as expected, they have reported even lower NO emissions of 10-20
x
mg/Nm3 at 6% oxygen.
Still lower emissions of NO could be achieved if Selective Catalytic Reduction (SCR) was
x
added in the gas turbine HRSG at a suitable temperature point and at an additional capital cost of
~20$/kW (or less) and additional O&M costs. However, GE believes that with the SCR
ammonia slip the sulfur content of the flue gas must be < 2 ppmv SO in order to avoid
2
deposition, plugging and corrosion of the HRSG finned heat exchange surfaces by ammonium
sulfate and bisulfate. This would be equivalent to requiring < 15 ppmv sulfur in the syngas
leaving the AGR process. This level can be achieved with higher-pressure gasifiers (e.g.
GE/Texaco quench) at 55 –69 barg using the physical solvent Selexol for sulfur removal. The
Shell gasifier with its low CO content syngas can also achieve this level of sulfur removal with
2
either Sulfinol M or MDEA chemical solvents.
If single digit NO emissions requiring SCR are ever mandated this could add extra cost and
x
performance penalties to some gasification processes e.g. E-Gas that have higher CO content
2
syngas. Accordingly E-Gas has recently raised their gasifier operating pressure to 40 barg so
that a physical solvent such as Selexol can be used. They have estimated that addition of Selexol
and SCR would add 45$/kW to their TPC and 110 Btu/kWh to their heat rate. Possible
alternative approaches would be to add a ZnO drum for additional sulfur removal or to run the
SCR sub-stoichiometrically so that there is minimal or no ammonia slip.
Particulate Emissions
Particulates are removed from the syngas by barrier filters and/or water scrubbing prior to AGR
and combustion so that particulate emissions are negligible. Both Wabash and ELCOGAS have
reported PM 10 emissions of < 2 mg/Nm3 at 6% oxygen or ~0.0015 lbs/MBtu from the IGCC.
At Wabash, the emissions from the cooling tower are estimated at an additional 0.01 lbs/MBtu or
12 mg/Nm3.
Chlorine Removal
Trace chlorine occurs to a varying extent in coals. It appears mostly as HCl in the syngas during
coal gasification. The HCl is removed from the syngas in the water scrubbing typically located
before the COS hydrolysis reactor. A blow down stream from the circulating scrubber water is
stripped and the overhead sent to the Claus plant for destruction of the ammonia and trace
cyanide. The stripped water is usually subjected to further waste water treatment. However, at
4-9

Tampa, Wabash and NUON the water is evaporated, sometimes using vapor recompression to
recover condensate quality water for recycle and a salt cake that is disposed of separately. In
some cases the cake may be used on roads but the occurrence of trace elements such as Se and
As has sometimes required disposal in a hazardous landfill.
Mercury Removal
At the time these IGCC plants were designed, there was no requirement for Mercury removal.
However, measurements at Tampa and Wabash have indicated that about half of the Mercury in
the feed is emitted from the gas turbine flue gas. The other half is probably split between the
fine solids and the wastewater but no complete balances have yet been made. At NUON,
measurements have indicated ~60-70% capture in the flyash and ~30% in the sulfur. However,
Eastman Chemical has been practicing Mercury removal from their coal based syngas for the
past 20 years. The syngas is passed through a fixed bed of pre-sulfided activated carbon at
system pressure (900 psig or 62 barg) and ~30°C that removes 90-95% of the Mercury in the
syngas. The beds typically last ~18 months when the carbon supplier takes the spent bed and
replaces it. Since the US EPA plans to order Mercury removal from coal plants it is most likely
that all future coal IGCC plants will have a carbon bed for Mercury removal from the syngas.
Parsons has estimated that the additional cost is quite small at an additional capital cost of
~3.3$/kW and an increase in Cost of Electricity (COE) of ~0.25$/MWh. Arsenic, As and some
other trace metals are also absorbed on the carbon, with varying degrees of efficiency.
Slag and Solid Waste
In the entrained flow gasification processes used in these IGCC plants, the coal ash is produced
as a dense, glass like slag with several potential uses. The dry coal fed gasifiers at NUON and
ELCOGAS operate at higher temperatures than the slurry fed gasifiers at Tampa and Wabash.
The fly slag at NUON and ELCOGAS is very low carbon and is not recycled to the gasifier and
both the fly slag and the bottom slag from these locations are sold for use. The filter catch of
char at Wabash is recycled to the first stage of the gasifier and the net slag product is that from
the bottom of the gasifier. With coal, it typically has a carbon content of ~7% (a little higher
with petroleum coke feed) and it is currently used as landfill cover at no disposal charge. At
Tampa, the carbon conversion per pass is lower than design and the amount of unconverted
material is about double the design value. Additional steps have been taken at Tampa to recycle
the unconverted material at some increase in oxygen consumption. The coarse slag is sold to the
cement industry and the fine slag is recycled to the feed. There will be no additional solid waste
production from IGCC beyond the coal ash/slag unless limestone is required as a fluxing agent
for high ash fusion temperature coals. This contrasts markedly with the limestone requirement
and the solid waste disposal problems with PC and Circulating Fluidized Bed Combustion
(CFBC) plants.
CO Removal
2
This issue is discussed more in the Economics, Chapter 8 of this Report. If CO removal is
2
required from an IGCC plant, the shift reaction is conducted on the syngas whereby the CO is
converted to more Hydrogen: CO + HO = CO + H. The CO is then removed by conventional
2 2 2 2
4-10

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
solvent AGR process e.g. Selexol. Because the CO removal is conducted under pressure prior
2
to combustion the volume of gas that must be treated is less than 1% of that which would have to
be treated in a PC boiler‘s flue gas. In addition, the driving force for absorption, the partial
pressure of CO, is also over 100 times greater in syngas (after shifting) than in PC flue gas.
2
Several studies have therefore concluded that if CO removal is required the additional cost to
2
remove it from IGCC is much less than from PC or FBC flue gas or even Natural Gas Fired
Combined Cycle (NGCC) plants.
CO capture in IGCC plants is favored by high pressure and water quenching of the syngas. The
2
high moisture content of a quenched syngas enables the shift reaction to be conducted with little
or no additional steam from the steam cycle and the high pressure enables the use of a physical
solvent such as Selexol to produce CO at a pressure that requires less auxiliary power for
2
compression to pipeline pressure.
Plant Dynamic Simulation
If a dynamic simulation of the IGCC plant is created early on in project development it can be
used advantageously a) during the design phase and for developing the integrated control system
for the plant and b) to develop an operator training simulator and lesson plans. Dynamic
simulations and operator training simulators were developed and used on the Wabash and Tampa
projects. Their use has been credited with great benefits in shortened start up times and
improved operations. Both of these plants achieved full plant output within six weeks of first
coal feed. Elcogas also developed a dynamic simulation of the plant and although it was not
available at initial start up it was later of significant use for training in the early years of low
availability. The use of plant simulation is therefore strongly recommended for future IGCC
plants.
Operating Statistics, Availability and Causes of Outage
Members of the Gasification Technologies Council and EPRI staff have proposed the following
guidelines as a standard way of reporting the operating statistics of gasification facilities:
The plant is divided into three units so that the operating statistics can be reported for each of
these critical areas of the facility. The units are defined as follows:
• Air Separation Unit (ASU)
• Gasification and Acid Gas Removal Unit
• Product Units
• Power Production block, and/or
• Chemical production
Four Primary and four Secondary categories are proposed. The Primary categories are Product
Not Required, Planned Outages, Unplanned Outages and On-Stream. These categories are
further defined as follows:
4-11

Product Not Required - % of the year that the product from the unit was not required, and
therefore, the unit was not operated. The unit was generally available to run and was not in a
planned outage
Planned Outages - % of the year that the unit is not operated due to outages that were scheduled
at least one month in advance. - Includes yearly planned outages as well as maintenance outages
with more than one month’s notice.
Unplanned Outages - % of the year the unit was not operated due to forced outages that had less
than one month notice. - Includes immediate outages as well as maintenance outages with less
than one month’s notice.
On-Stream - % of the year the unit was operating and supplying product in a quantity useful to
the downstream unit or customer.
The Secondary categories are Forced Outage Rate, Capacity Factor, Yearly Production and
Design Production and are further defined as follows:
Forced Outage Rate – The time during which the downstream unit or customer did not receive
product divided by the time during which they expected product, expressed as a percentage.
Capacity Factor – Yearly production of the unit divided by the design production, expressed as a
percentage.
Yearly Production – The total product actually produced from the unit in a calendar year. For the
gasification unit, the preference is to report production on the basis of total fuel gas LHV.
Design Production – The maximum production that the unit would produce at the design rate
over the calendar year when operated in an integrated manner. This is calculated by multiplying
the average annual daily design rate by 365. Note that the Design Production can change over
time, as the plant is de-bottlenecked.
In the past, IGCC and gasification plant owners have used a variety of terms to report on their
plant operations. Discussions among users and suppliers have produced the guidelines outlined
above and from now on owners are encouraged to report their operating statistics in the above-
defined categories.
Overall Experience
The general experience of the four coal based IGCC plants is that the single train annual
on-stream factor has been ~75-80% although several of the plants have demonstrated that they
could achieve the target of 85% on-stream on a quarterly basis. There are therefore some
grounds for optimism about future improvements in the annual figures. For multi-train
gasification plants with a spare gasifier the syngas on stream factor has been demonstrated at >
95% (Eastman, Ube etc).
The current IGCC plant designs are mostly for plants in the 600-900 MW size range. For
refractory lined gasifiers such as GE/Texaco and E-Gas the replacement of refractory has
historically been needed every 2-3 years and requiring a 30-day outage. For IGCC plants based
on these technologies, a spare gasifier is recommended to achieve a target availability >90%.
4-12

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
However, GE and COP feel that with refractory improvements they could achieve 85% single
train availability. The Shell gasifier does not have this same refractory replacement period and
generally has a higher availability. However, to achieve over 90% availability, Shell probably
also needs a spare gasifier. With the currently high estimated cost of the Shell gasification island
(GI), a spare gasifier is hard to justify, however, with no spare there would be a lower calculated
availability for the Shell IGCC design versus GE/Texaco and E Gas with a spare gasification
train. All three technologies should be able to achieve over 80% availability without a spare, and
in some situations, for example, with lower power demand in Spring and Fall this may be
sufficient.
Air Separation Units (ASU)
Historically ASU’s have experienced high availabilities of ~98%. However, the ASU’s at
Wabash and Tampa have experienced some unusual problems not associated with the IGCC
application. There was a cold box piping failure at Tampa and a moisture related ground fault at
Wabash. Both ASU’s have also experienced problems in operation of the guide vanes on the
main air compressor. Wabash also had a significant outage in early 2006 for a de-rime of the
MAC.
Combined Cycle Block
Both of the US plants, Wabash and Tampa, have also experienced unusual problems in the
combined cycle areas unrelated to the IGCC application. In general, this generation of gas
turbines has experienced an average of 2% lower availability than the previous generation of gas
turbines.
At Tampa, where the back up fuel is distillate oil, there was a failure of the atomizing air
compressor and there have been coking deposition problems in the fuel oil piping. In 2003, the
rewinding of both generators and gas turbine rotor replacement adversely impacted 2003
availability. The rotor problem concerned the first stage disc that holds the blades. Shot peening
of the first stage disc is the recommended solution. This was a fleet problem with the 7 FAs of
this early vintage and was unrelated to the syngas operation. In January 2005, there was a
compressor wreck that caused a 100 day outage. This was due to another fleet problem with this
vintage of 7 FA’s. The cause was compressor discharge case creep. A 45 day outage was taken
in April 2006 to replace the compressor discharge casing. Another of the continuing availability
problems with this generation of GE gas turbines is the requirement to inspect the P-cut R0
compressor blades every 1600 hours.
At Wabash, the power block availability was badly impacted by HRSG tube leak failures due to
expansion issues with the bottom supported unit. (HRSG’s are usually top supported.) There
was a damage failure of the 7 FA compressor at Wabash in March 1999 that was unrelated to the
IGCC application. Some other 7 FA turbines in natural gas service have also experienced a
similar problem. The turbine was returned to operation in June 1999. There have also been
some other problems associated with the addition of natural gas nozzles to the already crowded
combustor cans and there was some downstream impact from debris on the third (uncooled)
stage.
4-13

The Siemens turbines at Buggenum and Puertollano have experienced humming and high burner
temperatures in the large silo combustors. This has been a particular problem at Puertollano
where preventative inspection every 1000 hours, sometimes requiring burner and refractory tile
replacement, was necessary until 2003. In April 2003, Puertollano had to take an extended
outage of ~12-14 weeks due to deformation of the flange between the compressor and
turboexpander. Since the return from that outage the gas turbine has operated without humming
and overheating and with preventative inspection every 4000 hours. Cracks have also been
found in the hot gas path between the compressor and turboexpander at Buggenum. A fault in
the gas turbine main transformer bushing at Puertollano caused significant outages in 2004 and
2005. A fault was also found in 2005 on the inlet guide vanes (IGV) where there was also some
Foreign Object Damage (FOD). In 2006, there was a failure of the position transmitter on the
gas turbine fuel control flap.
Overall IGCC
The causes of plant outages are summarized in the Table 4-4 below followed by some further
notes for each of the outage topics and the currently suggested modifications to minimize the
occurrence and impact of these outages.
Some of the major causes of outage at the four IGCC plants have similarities e.g. fouling and
corrosion of syngas coolers, erosion/corrosion in the circulating slag water loops. But some of
the causes are more specific to the individual technologies e.g. fuel injector tip life and refractory
wear for the coal water slurry fed refractory lined gasifiers of GE/Texaco and E-Gas. With
exception of the general 7 FA fleet problems the general operating experience of the GE 7 FA
gas turbines with can annular type combustors has been reliable with syngas in the IGCC
application. However, the Siemens gas turbines with refractory tile lined silo combustors have
historically been a significant cause of outage at NUON and ELCOGAS although recent
modifications to the burner arrangements and controls have improved operations.
4-14

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
