Coal Feeding
The coal water slurry feed pumps at Wabash and Tampa have generally been very reliable.
However, at Tampa decisions were made during design that eliminated some features that would
have improved availability. There is therefore no spare slurry feed pump and no oxygen storage
at the Tampa plant. Pump failures at Tampa have caused some outages but these have generally
been short enough that a hot restart of the gasifier could be achieved.
The dry coal feed systems require more attention than the slurry systems to keep them
functioning continuously. Maintaining fluidization in the bottom cones can be problematic.
Pulverized coal can agglomerate particularly if the lock hopper vessels cool enough that water
condensation can occur. The coal must be dried and the vessels insulated. In the past, NUON
had problems with the constantly changing blended feedstock composition but this is no longer a
significant issue. Coal feeding at ELCOGAS has sometimes been problematic but it has been
improved since modifications were made in Spring 2001. However, the high and variable ash
content of the local Spanish coal presents some problems in maintaining a consistent feed
quality.
Fuel Injector Tip Life
The fuel injector tip life for the coal water slurry fed gasifiers is much lower than for the dry coal
fed gasifiers (> 1 year). The failure is usually due to stress corrosion cracking at the injector tip
4-15

(where especially hardened metals are used). At Tampa, the injector life (and also the refractory
life) has improved as the plant matures and less temperature cycling is experienced. The plant
operators have developed a hot restart technique for recovery after minor trips with full injector
replacement during the hot restart. Wabash has also developed a scheme for injector
replacement within 18 hours. These developments markedly reduce the forced outages due to
injector tip failures and other minor outages. Wabash has also reported that some injectors have
had over 4000 hours of operation. Perhaps the preheating of the slurry has a positive effect in
this regard. (See discussion in Chapter 1.)
The fuel injectors at NUON have passed their initially anticipated life of 8000 hours and 20,000
hours has been demonstrated.
Refractory Life
Refractory life for the vertical hot face in the brick lined coal water slurry fed gasifiers at
Wabash and Tampa is typically ~2 years. However, more recently Tampa has experienced 3
years life on its refractory. It is not clear what caused this improvement although it had been the
intent to run at lower gasifier temperatures. Perhaps the longer runs that have been achieved as
the plant operation has matured have resulted in fewer temperature excursions for warm up and
shut down and accompanying stresses on the refractory, or perhaps it is lower slag traffic as the
feed moved to a higher percentage of petroleum coke. At Wabash, the refractory area in the
vicinity of the slag tap is the most prone to wear and repairs are made at each outage opportunity.
Similarly, the bottom neck of the Texaco gasifier is a high wear area. Eastman utilizes a
removable cone for the bottom neck area that requires only 20 hours for change out (plus the
time for cool down and reheat). Repairs to the GE/Texaco gasifier top and bottom refractory
faces take 12-15 days but to replace the entire hot face refractory including the vertical section
can take 30 –35 days to cool down, remove, replace and warm up. This obviously would be a
major outage and unless new longer lasting refractories can be developed then a spare gasifier is
probably needed in order to achieve a 90% equivalent availability for commercial plants. At
Eastman Chemical a spare GE/Texaco gasifier is kept at temperature and they have developed
changeover techniques so that syngas flow is not interrupted. Tampa was designed as a
demonstration unit and has no spare gasifier. The Wabash plant is provided with a spare gasifier
and although it is not currently tied in with the rest of the downstream processing the connection
and changeover could be effected in relatively few days (particularly with some warning).
The Shell gasifier is lined with a steam raising membrane waterwall that is covered with just a
few inches of ram refractory. Repairs to the refractory are made at each outage opportunity and
this has not been a major source of outage at NUON. The membrane wall itself has required
very little attention. However, ELCOGAS has recently experienced membrane leaks due to flow
obstructions. The design has been changed.
When considering the future availability of 600 MW commercial IGCC plants with two trains of
gasification and gas turbines the issue of sparing is great importance. For availability over 90%,
GE/Texaco and E-Gas would probably require a spare gasifier. However, both GE and COP
have suggested that for companies and locations that experience lower power demand in Spring
and Fall a spare gasifier may not be justified and the 80-85 % availability that should be
achievable without a spare should be sufficient. Shell may have an advantage in this regard.
4-16

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
Circulating Slag Water
The circulating water from the slag quench chamber contains sharp fine solids so that erosion is a
constant problem. The use of long radius bends where possible is recommended to promote a
smoother consistent continuous flow and to avoid recirculation pockets. The slag water circuit is
often acidic and needs alkali addition. However, the problem is also compounded by difficulties
encountered in monitoring the pH due to plugging or corrosion of the probes. It is often
necessary to add either acid or alkali as needed to keep the pH in the range that avoids corrosion
and prevents precipitation.
In the GE/Texaco system, the carbon scrubber tower has its own circuit and in many cases
ammonia is captured to some extent. In this latter case, pH control may need some acid to avoid
calcium deposits in the circulating lines. In some cases, a change has been made to 316 type
stainless steel however in this case the chloride content of the circulating water must be kept
<100 ppmw and as a consequence the blow down stream and waste treatment section must
handle a much larger volume. With low carbon steel the chloride content can be allowed to
increase to ~1500 ppmw.
Slag Tap Blockage
This has occurred occasionally at all of the plants. If a blockage does occur the outage can last
8-10 days in order to cool down and mine out the blocked area. It has nearly always been
associated with a change in feed coal properties that unexpectedly changed the slag viscosity.
The so-called T250 temperature (the temperature at which the slag viscosity is 250 centipoises)
is typically used to give an indication of the temperature at which the gasifier should be run to
avoid slag blockage problems. This temperature is monitored in most cases but the slag viscosity
measurement is time consuming (~6-8 hours) and there is always a question about whether the
sample taken was truly representative. The problem of variation in ash fusion properties is more
likely to arise with blended coals and particularly any coals or feeds such as petroleum coke that
need flux additions. At Wabash, there have been four such incidents (see later). At Tampa,
there were some incidents in 1999 when blends of Eastern and Western coals produced sudden
changes in the wall slag coverage that led to sudden surges in slag flow from the gasifier.
In the early years of operation, the coals gasified at Buggenum were not specifically selected for
gasification, but were blended for lowest possible costs and general use in the Dutch Pulverized
Coal (PC) power station boilers. These coal blends therefore did not have the preferred ash
compositions for gasification and because of their generally high ash fusion temperatures the
addition of flux was often required. This is no longer the case. There were problems with high
ash coals and certain blends causing slag agglomeration. There is no slag crusher at NUON. If a
slag crusher is not included (or if space for one is not allowed for) in the original design it is very
difficult to add one later. If future plants are to be designed for a range of coals and for rapid
load following, then it is recommended that a slag crusher be used. At NUON, there has also
been a failure of the refractory lined heat skirt below the slag tap. In future units, a cooled
membrane wall will be substituted for the refractory lined skirt.
4-17

The feed at the ELCOGAS plant is very high ash and problems have sometimes been
experienced with slag removal. However, the two slag tap blockages in 2005 were ultimately
caused by water flow distribution problems within the gasifier membrane wall.
The inclusion of a slag crusher in the slag discharge line is recommended for all future IGCC
plants.
Syngas Cooler (SGC) Fouling and Corrosion
SGC fouling has occurred on at least some occasions in all four plants. However, at Nuon and so
far at ELCOGAS, it has not been a major cause of plant outage.
It appears that the fouling is somewhat coal dependent and the deposits are often enriched in
alkali metals and iron. A mixture of Potassium Alumino-silicate and Pyrrhotite (FeS) appears to
produce a relatively low melting eutectic.
The Tampa radiant syngas cooler was designed with soot blowing capability however this vessel
has shown very little fouling and the soot blowers are rarely used. The reduced fouling in this
vessel has led to an outlet temperature about 300-400°F (166-222°C) lower than design. New
plants with a radiant cooler design will therefore have the choice of either reducing the size and
height of the vessel or having the lower outlet temperature and more heat recovery. The fouling
and corrosion of the gas/gas exchangers was an early problem at Tampa. These horizontal fire
tube exchangers heated the clean syngas and nitrogen against the raw syngas leaving the
convective syngas coolers thereby putting more useful energy into the gas turbine (Brayton
Cycle). Persistent fouling led to their removal from service in 1997 with a consequent decrease
in net plant efficiency.
After the removal of the gas/gas exchangers from service at Tampa, fouling of the horizontal fire
tube convective syngas coolers became a major cause (~5%) of outages. The formation of
deposits was often initiated during startup that would continue to build and sometimes become
detached and produce blockage of the inlet tube sheet. Modifications to start up procedures
combined with modifications to the inlet gas flow path (aided by the use of the Computational
Fluid Dynamic (CFD) modeling program Fluent) improved the situation. Fouling increases
markedly above 690°C (1275°F) inlet temperature. Therefore, some additional heat exchange
surface has recently been added to the platens in the Radiant cooler to reduce the outlet
temperature. Depending on the value of efficiency new designs have the option of either
eliminating the convective SGCs and using a large Radiant SGC, using the Tampa modified
convective SGC design, or perhaps adopting a downflow firetube design, or a more expensive
water tube design. Another alternative is to adopt the Texaco Quench design (with neither
radiant nor convective SGCs) – a lower cost but lower efficiency option.
Wabash differs from the other three plants in that it uses a downflow firetube SGC design. There
is also an additional factor with the E-Gas technology because of the survival of trace tar
components in the outlet gas derived from the second stage coal feed, the coking of which
sometimes leads to deposits that can break free from the piping surfaces and plug the inlet tube
sheet. The use of CFD modeling enabled Wabash to make gas path modifications to prevent
larger particulate matter from fouling the inlet tube sheet. However, deposits do continue to
4-18

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
form within the SGC tubing. A device has been installed that enables periodic tube cleaning in
place. With coal as feedstock, Wabash schedules two outages per year for SGC cleaning
however with petroleum coke feed they experience less fouling and hope to move to a longer
period between scheduled SGC cleanings
It is interesting to note that at both Wabash and ELCOGAS, the SGC deposits at certain
locations have shown significant enrichment in Germanium, Ge, and Arsenic, As. The
Germanium could be worth recovering for use in the electronics industry.
Exposure to moisture during downtime tends to concentrate chlorides and other corrosion
products at the scale metal interface that can cause subsequent spalling of the chromium oxide
rich scales. This spalling may be the cause of accelerated corrosion rather than aqueous
corrosion during downtime alone. The presence of sulfides in the scales is suspected as being
very susceptible to downtime dewpoint corrosion. Therefore, measures (e.g. purging with
nitrogen) need to be taken to avoid the conditions that could lead to downtime corrosion.
Candle Filter Failures
Candle filters are used at Wabash, NUON and ELCOGAS. The Tampa plant has no gas filter.
Wabash experienced early problems with candle fouling and breakage but after gaining some
further insights from a slip stream filter test program have greatly improved the availability.
Metallic elements are currently used because their availability experience (current estimated life
9000 hours) has been better than earlier installed ceramics. However, tests on newer ceramic
candle filter elements in the slip stream filter have shown more satisfactory performance and if a
longer life of 20,000 hours is justified they may switch back to ceramic elements. The Wabash
filter operates at ~350°C whereas the filters at NUON and ELCOGAS operate at ~ 250°C.
Since the addition of a bottom fixation construction at NUON in July 1996, which restricts the
lateral movement of the bottom ends of the suspended elements, it has not been necessary to
replace any of the candles. NUON (then Demkolec) elected to stay with the Schumacher silicon
carbide based candles and, unlike the Wabash project (which also originally had a Schumacher
designed filter), has not changed over to metallic elements. Originally Schumacher Type 40
candles were used but they currently use Type 20. There has been very little increase in the 30-
millibar pressure drop and a 5-year life is currently forecasted for the candles (over 20,000 hours
has been demonstrated). The captured fly ash is very fine and very low carbon (<0.2%). It is
currently sold as a replacement for clay but has potential for a higher-grade market.
The experience with the bottom supported LLB design at ELCOGAS is not yet as favorable as
with the Schumacher design at NUON. There is leakage and contamination on the candles and
about 4000 hours seems to be the life limit. Some clean up can be effected with a chemical
solution. It has sometimes been suggested that fine metal dust from the decomposition of Nickel
or Iron Carbonyls could be the cause of this filter fouling but this has not yet been definitively
determined. The filter life is still shorter than expected.
Irrespective of the details of the filter vessel design, it has been proven that some restriction of
the lateral movement of suspended filter elements is necessary to avoid excessive flexing and
4-19

breakage. Blowback cleaning systems must be designed to prevent build up of bridges between
candles that might also restrict their movement and lead to failure.
Acid Gas Removal (AGR) Process Solvent Fouling
All four plants employ COS hydrolysis ahead of the AGR process to reduce the COS content of
the syngas and thereby permit deeper sulfur removal. The COS hydrolysis reactor also converts
some of the CO to formic acid (HCOOH) and oxalic acid (COOH COOH) which lead to the
formation of heat stable salts (HSS) that can deposit out and foul the AGR absorber and stripper
trays and increase solvent usage. The solution now adopted at the Wabash and Tampa plants,
using MDEA as the AGR solvent, has been to add an online ion exchange membrane system to
continuously regenerate the solvent and remove the contaminants from a blow-down slipstream
taken off the circulating solvent.
At Nuon, the HSS content remains low; however, there has been thermal degradation of the
Sulfinol solvent in the regenerator reboiler and the solvent has been periodically cleaned by
vacuum distillation. If the steam temperature is too high, this leads to foaming and if Sulfinol is
carried over to the saturator, its presence in the saturator blowdown can produce problems in the
wastewater treatment.
Gas Turbine Combustor Vibration and Hot Spots
This has not been an issue with the combustion cans arranged in an annular fashion on the GE
7FA gas turbines at Tampa and Wabash. However, combustion-induced vibrations have
historically been a major cause of outage with the refractory tile lined silo combustors in the
Siemens V94.2 and V94.3 gas turbines at NUON and ELCOGAS. GE/EPRI/Shell/DOE
conducted full-pressure full-load syngas combustion tests on the GE F combustors in 1990-2
prior to installation in the US IGCC projects. Unfortunately, Siemens obviously could not
conduct full-scale silo combustion tests before installation, and it was necessary to utilize
multiple empirical “cut and try” design changes in the field to alleviate this problem. Also, at
NUON, in order to minimize auxiliary power usage (and maximize efficiency), the pressure drop
across the fuel gas control valve is only about half that normally used for natural gas. This
probably contributed to some of the problems of maldistribution, lack of combustion control, and
vibrations. The vibration problem is particularly troublesome during the transition from natural
gas to syngas. Combustor design improvements have markedly reduced the vibration problem
and the availability in recent years has been greatly improved e.g. in early 2002, the NUON plant
completed an uninterrupted IGCC run of >2500 hours with a planned shutdown.
At ELCOGAS, there were a number of trial and error changes of the syngas burners in an
attempt to avoid humming and overheating, particularly during fuel changeovers at startup and
shutdown. The development of a functional gas turbine control system has been troublesome
with a large number of control design modifications. In order to minimize the vibration problem,
an asymmetric arrangement of burners is used in each silo however this lead to some locations
being hotter than others. Regular gas turbine inspections were scheduled every 1000 hours to
check for burner overheating and broken refractory tiles. In April 2003, a problem of
deformation of the flange between the compressor and turbo expander was found during the
4-20

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
50,000 hour overhaul. New burners and control system were installed during a three month
outage. The new burners improved the operation and since then the gas turbine has operated
without humming or overheating using preventative inspection every 4000 hours.
Siemens also supplied the gas turbines for the 510 MW Heavy Oil IGCC ISAB plant in Sicily.
These were two V94.2K machines in which extra compressor stages were added to the basic
V94.2 design for better matching to the non-integrated configuration at ISAB. No major
vibration problems have yet been reported with these machines. This suggests that the low-
pressure drop across the gas turbine control valves in the tightly integrated plants at Nuon and
ELCOGAS may have contributed to the problem experienced at these plants.
In their latest gas turbine designs, e.g. the V94.3A, Siemens uses a can annular design; however,
they still retain the use of refractory tiles. It is interesting to note that for the IGCC application in
the US, Siemens is recommending the SCC6-5000F (formerly the Westinghouse 501F). It will
be interesting to see what gas turbines Siemens proposes when they next bid an IGCC
application in the 50 Hz market. The Sannazzaro heavy oil IGCC project in Italy has selected
the V94.2K as used on the ISAB IGCC in Sicily.
GE 7 FA Gas Turbine Problems
The GE 7 FA gas turbines at Wabash and at Tampa have had compressor and other failures that
have generally been similar to those experienced by the GE 7 FA gas turbines of a similar
vintage in natural gas service. These failures are not attributable to the syngas IGCC application.
More information on these failures is given in the specific IGCC plant reports in the following
section.
Specific Coal based IGCC Plant Reports
Wabash River IGCC Repowering Project
Plant Description
The first of the US DOE IGCC projects to start operations (in October 1995) was the Wabash
River IGCC Repowering project at Public Service of Indiana’s (PSI) Wabash River Generating
station near Terre Haute, Indiana. In this project, the Gasification “Island” is currently owned by
a 50/50 venture of Global Energy and the Wabash Valley Power Association, called SG
Solutions (formerly it was owned by Dynegy, NGC and originally Destec). SG solutions
supplies fuel to a GE 7FA combined cycle power block owned by PSI/Cinergy (now Duke
Energy). The gasification island comprises a conventional pressure (5 barg) Air Separation Unit
(ASU) supplied by Air Liquide but operated by Global a coal/water slurry fed oxygen blown E-
Gas™ (formerly named Dynegy and originally Destec and Dow) two stage entrained flow
gasifier, a downflow firetube syngas cooler, gas and water clean up. Slag is removed from the
slag bath at the base of the gasifier via a proprietary continuous letdown system (i.e. not via lock
hoppers). The height of the structure is markedly lower than the Tampa, Buggenum and
4-21

ELCOGAS plants. This lower height together with the lower cost firetube SGC should represent
capital cost savings over higher structures and water tube SGC designs.
The original design of the gas clean cleanup train at Wabash River comprised a hot gas barrier
filter (candle filters), a COS hydrolysis unit, heat exchange cooling of the gas, MDEA acid gas
removal system and syngas saturation. A water scrubbing step was added in late 1996 after the
barrier filter to remove chlorides that had been found to degrade the COS catalyst. The HS rich
2
gas from the MDEA regenerator stripper is sent to a Claus unit where elemental sulfur is
produced for sale and the tail gas is recycled to the gasifier. The power block has a GE 7FA gas
turbine and a HRSG that supplies steam to (i.e. repowers) a pre-existing 105 MW steam turbine.
The NO control at this plant is obtained by saturating the clean coal gasification derived syngas
x
with hot water recovered from low level heat available in the plant and by the direct injection
into the gas turbine of some intermediate pressure steam. The gasification plant delivers 1760
MBtu/hour (HHV) of syngas to the gas turbine at full load.
The gasification plant is located on ~40 acres of land on the existing power plant site that
overlays abandoned coal mines.
Operations
The combined cycle plant was first run on fuel oil in June ‘95 and on syngas in October ‘95. The
commercial demonstration period was announced to start in December 1995 and, by February
1996, the plant had achieved 100% gasification capacity and 100 % of the gas turbine IGCC
nameplate capacity of 192 MW. (The equivalent output of a 7FA on natural gas or fuel oil is
lower at about 165 MW.) The gas turbine has demonstrated emissions of less than 20 ppmv of
NO in the exhaust stack when operating in IGCC mode.
x
The demonstration period under the DOE contract was completed in January 2000. The
production statistics for the demonstration period were:
• Operating time on coal 15,067 hours
• Coal processed 1.406 Million mt (1.55 Million st)
• Syngas Produced 25.2 x 106 GJ (23.9 x 106 Million Btu)
• Power Produced 3.91 Million MWh
• Sulfur Produced 30,300 mt (33,400 st)
The measured air emissions were all significantly lower than the EPA CAAA standards for 2000
– SO 0.01-0.03 Kg/GJ (0.03-0.08 lb/MBtu), NO 0.04-0.06 kg/GJ (0.10-0.15 lb/MBtu), CO 0.02
2 x
kg/GJ (0.05 lb/MBtu) and particulate emissions not detectable. (The main contribution to
particulate air emissions from the whole IGCC area is from the cooling tower drift).
Global Energy bought the gasification plant and the gasification technology from Dynegy in
early 2000 and a new contract for the supply of syngas to the PSI/Cinergy GE 7 FA combined
cycle was then negotiated. Since August 2000, the plant operations have been on petroleum
coke because the delivered cost is only about half that of coal. The gasifier can process ~2500
4-22

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
tons/day of bituminous coal or about 2000 tons/day petroleum coke. With petroleum coke the
plant can supply more syngas than is required by the combined cycle. The petroleum coke is
typically 5% sulfur but they can take up to 7% sulfur. The Hardgrove grindability is a key index
for acceptability of pet coke. Coal slag is used as a flux and it is blended with the petroleum
coke on the conveyor. They have not run a blend of coal and petroleum coke although they have
demonstrated an on-line switch from coal to petroleum coke.
The amount of char particulates recycled from the filter catch to the gasifier is about the same as
when running coal. The carbon content of the slag was typically about 7% with coal but it is
higher with petroleum coke. The slag frit is a glassy sand-like material that passes the required
TCLP, RCRA and UTS tests. The local landfill likes to use it as daily cover and they do not
charge a disposal fee. It has also been used as construction backfill - about 80% of the Nickel
and 99% of the Vanadium in the Pet. Coke appears in the slag but because of the low surface
area and glass like nature the slag still passes all required tests.
The slurry concentration is ~63% dry solids. The slurry feed and oxygen are internally mixed in
the two horizontally opposed injectors. The first stage gasification conditions are ~400 psig
(27.5 barg) and 2800°F (1538°C). Additional slurry injection to the second stage gives a second
stage outlet temperature of 1900°F (1038°C). This is further quenched with water or recycle
syngas to ~ 1700°F (927°C) at the inlet to the syngas cooler (SGC). Gas path changes were
made in 1998 to reduce impingement and a screen was added to keep any larger pieces of loose
piping deposits away from fouling the SGC inlet tubesheet. A Computational Fluid Dynamics
(CFD) program, Fluent, was used to provide insight into the flow path design to minimize
deposition and fouling. (Tampa has also used Fluent for similar purposes). Plating out of
deposits on the SGC tube walls still occurs but a device has been installed that enables tube
cleaning in place. The deposits at certain locations in the SGC were markedly enriched in
Germanium, Ge, as well as Arsenic, As. The composition of these deposits is similar to those
reported in a 2002 paper on SGC deposits found in the ELCOGAS plant in Puertollano.
4-23

Figure 4-2
E Gas Gasifier as used at Wabash
The cooled syngas leaves the SGC at ~750°F (400°C) and enters the candle filter vessel for
removal of char particulates. Back pulsing is conducted with syngas and nitrogen is only used at
start-up. Metallic elements are currently used because their availability experience has been
better than earlier installed ceramics (current estimated life 9000 hours). However, tests on
newer ceramic candle filter elements in the slip stream filter have shown more satisfactory
performance and if a longer life of 20,000 hours is justified they may switch back to ceramic
elements. The filter catch is pneumatically conveyed to the gasifier where it is introduced at an
angle in the top of the first stage. The syngas leaving the filter is water washed to remove
ammonia and chlorides and sent to a COS hydrolysis unit. The catalyst has not been changed
since 1997 and its cost was actually less than the originally installed variety (which had
experienced fouling and poisoning -because the original flow scheme did not include a water
wash prior to the COS hydrolysis unit – the water wash was added in late 1996). After further
cooling, the syngas passes to the MDEA AGR process for HS removal. They use an ion
2
exchange membrane process for removal of the heat stable salts (HSS) and regeneration of the
MDEA solvent from a blow down slipstream. The clean syngas sulfur content can be as low as
4-24

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
50 ppmv but this is mostly dependent on the performance of the COS hydrolysis catalyst. The
clean syngas is then saturated and superheated by an additional 50-80°F (28-44°C) for delivery
over the fence to the Duke Energy owned GE 7 FA gas turbine combined cycle. (Sg Solutions
plans to buy the combined cycle from Duke in 2007).
The HS rich gas from the MDEA unit is sent to a Claus plant. The Claus plant tail gas is
2
compressed and recycled to the first stage of the gasifier. When both compressors are operating
the SO emissions are ~0.03 lb/MBtu. Typically only one compressor is operating and the
2
residual tail gases are sent to an auxiliary boiler in which case the SO2 emissions rise to ~0.08
lb/MBtu. They have sold over 100,000 tons of sulfur to date but the market has been very poor
the past few years.
The blow down from the water scrubber goes to a sour water stripper the overhead from which is
sent to the Claus plant. A vapor recompression evaporator is used to recover condensate quality
water and some salt. The salt has to be disposed of in a hazardous landfill since it contains traces
of Selenium, Se, and Arsenic, As. There is zero liquid discharge from the Gasification Island at
Wabash.
Availability/Reliability
In mid-March 1999, damage occurred to rows 14 through 17 of the air compressor rotor and
stator of the combustion turbine (CT) assembly, which resulted in a 14 week unscheduled outage
for the project. This failure was unrelated to the IGCC syngas application. PSI Energy
conducted a root cause failure analysis. The CT was returned to service on June 22 without
incident and has continued to operate as expected since the rebuild. In July 1999, a water spray
system was added to the air intake of the CT to allow more air through the machine when the
turbine is temperature limited. This system is only used during peak demand to provide an
increase in the total power output 5-10 MW. There have also been some problems associated
with the addition of natural gas nozzles to the already crowded combustor cans with some
downstream impacts from debris.
The HRSG has been a persistent source of tube leaks. A change to the main steam piping
support system was made in 1997 to allow needed expansion during startup conditions. This
alleviated the problem to some extent but tube leaks have been a continuing problem. This
HRSG is the only bottom supported HRSG that Foster Wheeler has built. All the other HRSGs
have been top supported.
Feed water heating problems have limited the steam turbine output to 96 MW, versus the design
output of 105 MW. Auxiliary power requirements for the plant (considered as an IGCC) are 36
MW (mostly for the ASU), so the net power output is about 252 MW, versus a design value of
262 MW. When the feed-water heating problem is fixed the efficiency should be about 41%
LHV (39.5%HHV) basis.
Following the return to service of the CT in June 1999, the plant had excellent operations setting
new records for continuous operation of over 1300 hours and a gasification campaign length of
128 days. On October 26, 1999, there was a fire in a cable tray that caused a further outage until
December 12. After that the unit resumed performance at high reliability until operations ceased
4-25

on March 31, 2000 when the original syngas supply agreement with Cinergy terminated. A new
syngas supply agreement was negotiated in April-May 2000 and in June 2000, the plant resumed
operations. Since August 14, 2000, the plant has been running on petroleum coke. However,
due to some business problems, the plant was not in operation from February 2004 until 2005.
The downtime and availability analysis for the major plant sections and overall IGCC plant for
2000 showed unusual downtime in the ASU and power block areas. The syngas facility was
81% available in 2000 (excluding the downtime between contracts). The longest unplanned
outage in 2000 was caused by a moisture related ground fault that damaged the oxygen
compressor motor terminal box in the ASU. The power block availability was negatively
impacted by continued HRSG tube failures related to expansion problems on the bottom-
supported unit that caused 19 days of unscheduled downtime. There were no major gasification
technology outages in 2000 and no single syngas interruption exceeding 39 hours.
As was the case in 2000, there was a lengthy period in 2001 when product syngas was not
required. PSI/Cinergy has been making some major modifications to their combined cycle unit
to enable them to run on natural gas. A new gas fired boiler has been installed to generate steam
(that would replace the steam from the gasification island) when the gasifier is not running and
supplying steam to the HRSG. Improvements to the HRSG and feed water heaters were also
made in 2000 however they were insufficient to halt the problems.
An analysis of the first three quarters of 2002 operating hours for the Global owned gasification
plant showed Product not required 30.4%, On-Stream 57.1 %, Unplanned Outage 6.5% and
Planned Outage 6%. The product syngas availability (1 – total outage hours/period hours x
100%) was 78.7% and the forced outage rate (unplanned outage/ (unplanned outage + on stream)
was 11%. The major causes of downtime (unplanned outages) in the gasification island were
syngas cooler (SGC) leaks (2.5%), ASU (1.5%) and the first stage gasifier, slag removal system
and sulfur recovery each at ~1%. However, in the fourth quarter of 2002 there was a slag tap
pluggage (attributed to errors in flux/coke ratio control) that caused a 10-day outage.
For the first three quarters of 2003, the gasification unit was on stream 61.3%; product not
required 15.5%, unplanned outages 13% and planned outages 10.3%. The major cause of
unplanned downtime was due to Sodium contamination of the water use for slurry that resulted
in severe SGC tube sheet fouling in less than 24 hours. The syngas availability was 74% or
82.4% when adjusted for product not required.
(However, any plant that experiences long periods of “Product not required “has more
opportunity for preventive maintenance than would otherwise be the case. Therefore, these
numbers must be treated with some caution.)
The addition of natural gas combustion capability to the 7 FA has resulted in hot spots due to
poor mixing from the additional natural gas port at the center of the circle of syngas ports. These
continuing problems with the CT and HRSG resulted in extended periods in 2001-3 where there
has been no requirement for syngas.
The ASU experienced 5-6 days a year of unplanned outages over 2001-2003. These were
attributed to guide vane sticking (as at Tampa), indoor type specifications for what is essentially
an outdoor plant (in Indiana!!) and under sizing of actuators.
4-26

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
The SGC fouling may require two outages a year, however the fouling is less with petroleum
coke and they hope to schedule just a single outage. Shutdowns and start-ups promote fouling.
Since the Wabash plant has a spare gasifier, (albeit unconnected), the contribution of refractory
replacement to forced outage is not as significant an issue as at Tampa. The changeover of
gasifiers would be every 2-3 years but refractory patching, particularly in the slag tap area, is
conducted on each outage.
The process unit reliability in the Gasification Island for the period 2000-2003 is shown in Table

The problems related in the preceding paragraphs have led to the relatively low gasification plant
utilization from 1999 to 2003 as shown in Table 4-6.
As mentioned previously the plant was shut down over a business dispute from February 2004 to
June 2005. Following the formation of SG Solutions the plant was returned to service in June
2005. 2006 was the 2nd year under SG Solutions ownership. The plant has more recently
shown improved Gasifier and Power Block operation with more stable continuous operating
periods. In the period from November 2005 (end of the Fall 2005 outage) to September 2006,
the power block and ASU accounted for over half of the forced outage hours and a
manufacturing defect in the slurry tank agitator shaft was responsible for 15% of the downtime.
Pie charts of the outage causes in this period were shown in the COP Technology Update at the
2006 Gasification Conference (P.Amick). Two-thirds of the unplanned outage time was due to
start-up delays and outage extensions.

Future Plans and Applications
Under the agreement with ConocoPhillips (COP) for the sale of the E-Gas technology, Global
(now Global/WVPA SG Solutions) retained ownership of the gasification plant but maintained a
services agreement for 15 COP technical and management personnel at the Wabash site. An
access agreement was also negotiated for technology advances, DOE projects, slipstream testing
and operator training. However, a business dispute arose between Global and Cinergy and the
plant did not operate from February 2004 until June 2005. In June 2005, a 50/50 venture, SG
Solutions, was formed between Global and Wabash Valley Power Association for ownership and
operation of the gasification island was resumed.
In early 2006, Cinergy was acquired by Duke Energy at which time the combined cycle at the
Wabash site became owned by Duke Energy.
A major overhaul of the Duke Energy combined cycle power block is planned for Fall 2006 with
replacement of the superheater and reheat modules and changeover to a top supported HRSG
unit. There will also be a hot gas path inspection of the gas turbine and a complete overhaul of
the steam turbine. It is anticipated that the entire power block will be sold to SG Solutions in
2007 at which time the entire power plant –coal to kilowatts- will be under the single ownership
of SG Solutions.
COP and DOE/NETL have a program of continuing technology development at the Wabash site.
The targeted development areas include:
• Coal slurry solids improvement
• Slurry mixer life extension
• Alternate fluxant evaluations
• Refractory materials testing
• Increased slurry quench demonstrations
• Slag quality utilization and improvement demonstration
4-28

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
• Gasifier temperature measurement (DOE NETL)
• Syngas cooler coatings (DOE NETL)
• Cyclone –Filter hybrid development (DOE NETL)
• Dry char slipstream unit (DOE NETL)Ceramic membranes (DOE NETL)
• Warm gas clean-up –sulfur and mercury (DOE NETL)
• Ceramic membranes (DOE NETL)
The Wabash gasification plant was awarded a DOE contract for the testing of a 2 MW Molten
Carbonate Fuel Cell (MCFC) on a slipstream of syngas. The two 1 MW MCFC units have been
delivered to the site by Fuel Cell Energy of Danbury, Connecticut. The total cost is estimated at
32.3 Million$. This MCFC test was originally planned to be on a syngas slipstream from Global
Energy's 500 MW Kentucky Pioneer IGCC project however since that project has been delayed
the decision was made to move the MCFC test site to Wabash. There are several commercial
sized IGCC projects based on the E-Gas technology in various stages of study and development
(See Chapter 1). Both coal and petroleum coke feeds are being considered. Under DOE
funding, Bechtel has conducted a variety of design studies on the E-Gas technology including a
new vertical cylindrical gasifier design that should be of lower cost and should enable higher
pressures (e.g. for hydrogen production) to be achieved and the use of fewer gasifiers for low
rank coals. See Section 1 for some further discussion of these studies.
Tampa Electric Company IGCC Plant
Plant Description
This IGCC project was the second of the US CCT IGCC demonstration projects to start up (in
September ’96). In contrast to the divided ownership of the gasification and power islands at
Wabash (N.B. Wabash will probably change to sole ownership by SG Solutions in 2007), the
Tampa Electric Company wholly owns this project. This project represents the logical scale up
of the experiences gained in the pioneer 100 MW IGCC plant at the Southern California Edison
Cool Water station that operated 1984-9. The main components of this plant are a high pressure
ASU (supplied by Air Products), an oxygen blown down flow single stage Texaco entrained
gasifier, associated heat exchange (comprising a large downflow water wall Radiant Syngas
Cooler (RSC) situated below the gasifier, twin parallel horizontal firetube convection syngas
coolers (CSC), two gas/gas exchangers (the latter now removed from service)), gas and water
clean up and a GE 7FA combined cycle.
The gas clean up train comprises a ‘carbon’ (particulate) scrubber, raw gas heat exchange and
cooling followed by COS Hydrolysis and removal of the remaining sulfur species (mainly HS)
2
in a MDEA acid gas removal system. The Tampa project is unique among the IGCC projects in
producing sulfuric acid rather than elemental sulfur for sale. This is due to its convenient
location adjacent to the important Florida phosphate fertilizer industry with its large
requirements for sulfuric acid.
4-29

The combined cycle is based on a GE 7FA gas turbine. In contrast to the steam injection used at
Wabash, this Tampa plant uses nitrogen from the ASU supplied to the head end of the gas
turbine combustors as the primary control of NO formation by dilution of the syngas (a saturator
x
was added in 2002 to further reduce NO emissions). The project entered its commercial
x
demonstration phase in October 1996 and since then the plant has routinely operated at its design
output of 192 MW on the GT and 121 MW on the ST. Auxiliary power requirements are about
63 MW for a net output of about 250 MW. They will occasionally turn down the gas turbine
from 192 MW to 150 MW if required. Lower loadings show the typical rapidly deteriorating
heat rate of a combined cycle. The DOE demonstration period ended in 2001.
Since the completion of the DOE operating period it has been advantageous to use more
petroleum coke because of its lower delivered cost. In order to improve the carbon conversion
with the less reactive petroleum coke feedstock more oxygen is required because of the increased
recycle of unconverted fines. Therefore, since July 2005, air has been extracted from the gas
turbine compressor to augment the supply from the ASU main air compressor.
Current Operations
The plant is currently running a mixture of ~55% petroleum coke and 45% coal. Trucks from
Tampa Electric’s Big Bend Station on Tampa Bay deliver the feed. The Texaco (now GE
Energy) gasifier operates at 2400-2700°F (~1300-150°C). The slag and about 50% of the char is
removed from a water bath at the base of the radiant cooler via lock hoppers. The fouling factor
in the radiant cooler is only about a third of the assumed design value. Accordingly, the radiant
outlet temperature is some 3-400°F (133-220°C) lower than design and the convective cooler
outlet is in turn lower. The fouling and corrosion of the gas/gas exchangers was an early
problem at Tampa. These horizontal fire tube exchangers heated the clean syngas and nitrogen
against the raw syngas leaving the convective syngas coolers thereby putting more useful energy
into the gas turbine (Brayton Cycle). Persistent fouling led to their removal from service in 1997
with a consequent decrease in net plant efficiency.
There are some subsequent problems with fouling of the inlet tube sheet of the convective cooler
but these have been improved somewhat by minor design and operating changes in startup
procedure. There has been less fouling with the petroleum coke/coal mixture currently being
used. The carbon scrubber removes the balance of the char particulate matter and HCl. The exit
temperature of the scrubber is ~330°F (165°C).
About 5% of the coal sulfur ends up as COS in the raw gas. The COS hydrolysis unit converts
most of the COS to HS which is subsequently removed in the MDEA AGR process. However,
2
the COS hydrolysis catalyst also forms traces of formic and oxalic acid from the CO in the gas
and these produce heat stable salts with MDEA that can cause fouling and corrosion of the AGR
absorber trays unless they are eliminated. In order to eliminate this fouling, an ion exchange
membrane has been installed to continuously regenerate a blow down slipstream taken from the
circulating solvent flow and to thereby remove the heat stable salts.
The carbon conversion per pass in the gasifier is lower than design and the unconverted
carbon/slag production is about double the design value. The oxygen needed for full load
without carbon recycle is 2125 KSCFH (60KNm3) and 2275 KSCFH (65KNm3) with 100%
4-30

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
carbon recycle. A new coarse slag screen was installed in late 2003 with much improved results.
It produces a coarse slag of < 2% carbon that is sold for cement and enables full recycle of the
screen underflow to the feed rod mills. In the past there had been a considerable accumulation of
slag at the site but this has now been eliminated through use of the new slag screen. Tampa has
found it advantageous to run the gasifier cooler to extend the refractory life (a 3 year life was
reported in 2006). With petroleum coke this further reduces the carbon conversion per pass to
about 80%. To achieve a target of 95% overall petroleum coke carbon conversion required
additional oxygen. Therefore, since July 2005, air has been extracted from the gas turbine
compressor to augment the supply from the ASU main air compressor. Tampa Electric
previously determined that the added cost of oxygen storage could not be justified on grounds of
increased availability alone.
As part of an agreement with the Florida Department of Environment, in 2002, Tampa installed a
saturator adding ~6-7% moisture to the clean syngas going to the gas turbine. This has enabled
the plant to meet the new reduced NO emissions permit of <15 ppmv at 15% oxygen as a 30 day
x
rolling average.
Reliability/Availability
The gasifier on-stream time averaged ~75% in 2001-3 which when added to “Product not needed
“or “Reserve Shutdown” time gave a gasification island availability of ~85%. The main causes
of outage in the gasification section are refractory wear, injector tip life (both now improved),
convective syngas cooler plugging and leakage and corrosion/erosion in the black and gray water
circuits. Some irritating causes of outages have been attributed to electronic component aging
and obsolescence.
The gasification section unplanned outage rate was about 8 % in the seventh operating year
October 2002- September 2003. The main causes were:
• Convective syngas cooler plugging (9 days).
• Leaks and pluggage in the syngas scrubbing and black water area (7.4 days)
• Raw gas piping leak between the CSC and syngas scrubber (manufacturing defect (no stress
relief) of two new upgraded sections) (6.9 days)
• Failure of main air compressor fourth stage bearing (4.7 days)
• Six slurry feed pump trips (there is no spare). These nuisance shutdowns are usually quickly
recovered through use of the hot restart capability (1.8 days)
However 2004 was the best year to date for the Tampa plant. The sub system availability
(Service hours + Reserve shutdown hours/period hours) for the years 2002-2004 are shown in
Table 4-7.
4-31

Table 4-7
Tampa IGCC Sub System Availability 2002-2004
Sub System 2002 2003 2004 Expected
Air Separation (single train) 96 95 99 96-98
Gasification (single train) 77 78 83 80-90
Power Block 94 89 96 94-96
After the removal of the gas/gas exchangers from service at Tampa, fouling of the horizontal fire
tube convective syngas coolers became a major cause (~5%) of outages. The formation of
deposits was often initiated during startup that would continue to build and sometimes become
detached and produce blockage of the inlet tube sheet. Modifications to start up procedures
combined with modifications to the inlet gas flow path (aided by the use of the Computational
Fluid Dynamic (CFD) modeling program Fluent) improved the situation. Fouling increases
markedly above 690°C (1275°F) inlet temperature. Therefore, some additional heat exchange
surface has recently been added to the platens in the Radiant cooler to reduce the outlet
temperature.
The main cause of unplanned outage in 2003 was the replacement of the 7 FA rotor (and
rewinding of both generators). This was due to a first stage disc problem (that holds the blades)
which has fleet problem with this early vintage of 7 FA’s and was unrelated to the syngas
operation. This obviously adversely affected availability and contributed 24 days of unplanned
outage giving a total of 52 days or 14.2% unplanned outage for the IGCC in its seventh year of
operation. Since the major turbine overhaul in Fall 2003, the plant operated at high availability
(with fuel back-up) until the gas turbine compressor wreck in January 2005 that caused a 100 day
outage. This was due to another fleet problem with this vintage of 7 FA’s. The cause was
compressor discharge case creep. Another 45 day outage is scheduled for April 2006 to replace
the compressor discharge casing.
Normally the gas turbine has a hot gas path inspection about every two years requiring a 2-3
week planned outage stretching to 4 weeks if a full combined cycle inspection is planned
whereas the combustion hardware inspection in the other years is about 10-14 days. Repairs to
the gasifier top and bottom refractory faces take 12-15 days but replacement of the vertical hot
face takes about 4 weeks. The overall time for a cold start is 36-40 hours. The operators have
become skilled at hot gasifier restarts after minor outages and they can now conduct a burner
(injector) to burner (injector) change with the hot restart technique. This reduces the
contribution of burner tip failures to forced outages.
Tampa has a Long Term Service Agreement (LTSA) with GE for the Gas Turbine. Continuing
improvements in the reliability of on line transfers from syngas to distillate oil, as a back up fuel,
increased the power block availability up to 95% in 2002. Tampa is permitted to run up to 10%
of the time on back up fuel. A major planned outage was taken in the second quarter of 2003 to
inspect and maintain the gas and steam turbines including replacement of their insulation. Some
deposits were found to be somewhat enriched in arsenic and selenium so that special suiting is
required for the inspections. At one outage deposits of elemental sulfur were found in the HRSG
(its hard to think how this could happen but perhaps there was a period where the MDEA system
4-32

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
was not working very well and higher HS in the syngas reacted with SO from combusted
2 2
syngas to form elemental sulfur?).
At Tampa, where the back up fuel is distillate oil, there was a failure of the atomizing air
compressor and there have been coking deposition problems in the fuel oil piping. In 2003, the
rewinding of both generators and gas turbine rotor replacement adversely impacted 2003
availability. The rotor problem concerned the first stage disc that holds the blades. Shot peening
of the first stage disc is the recommended solution. This was a fleet problem with the 7 FAs of
this early vintage and was unrelated to the syngas operation. In January 2005, there was a
compressor wreck that caused a 100 day outage. This was due to another fleet problem with this
vintage of 7 FA’s. The cause was compressor discharge case creep. A 45 day outage was taken
in April 2006 to replace the compressor discharge casing. Another of the continuing availability
problems with this generation of GE gas turbines is the requirement to inspect the P-cut R0
compressor blades every 1600 hours.
Since returning from the turbine compressor wreck in May 2005, the gasifier was on line 90.6%
of the time for the five month peak demand period May –September; 6.8% of the time was
needed for cleaning of the convective syngas cooler, and 2.6% for other miscellaneous outages.
During the 45 day outage in April - May 2006, several additional improvements were made. The
gasifier refractory was replaced after a record 3 year life. Additional surface was added to the
Radiant cooler platens to reduce the outlet temperature and reduce fouling of the convective
syngas coolers. A booster pump was added on the slurry feed line to accommodate the addition
of a strainer in that line. A new optic pyrometer from the University of Virginia has also been
installed and tested.
Apart from the 45 day outage the 2006 gasifier was on line 91.2% through September. The
major downtime cause was the convective syngas cooler fouling (7%) and other (1.8%).
With the increased use of petroleum coke the lower carbon conversion per pas has led to an
increase in the amount of fines recycled. This has led to increased corrosion in the rod mills and
the subsequent rod failures led to “entanglements” and some forced outage.
The addition of air extraction from the gas turbine compressor has provided excellent benefit
when most needed at the summer high ambient temperatures, when it has, on average, been able
to provide an additional 19 MW of net power. The maximum design air extraction rate is 1500
KSCFH (12.5% of the ASU capacity) and 1100 KSCFH at high ambient temperatures. About
900 KSCFH is available without additional blower assistance.
Because of the feed cost advantage, Tampa is seeking a permit to use 100% petroleum coke.
Staffing
Polk has 77 permanent staff with 5 teams of 9-10 people. There are 3 laboratory staff and a
contract maintenance team of 15. Staffing for a two train plant was estimated to be about 100. (It
should be noted that two additional GE 7 FA gas turbines have been recently added at Tampa’s
Polk site for peaking duty.).
4-33

Nuon (formerly SEP/Demkolec) IGCC Plant in The Netherlands
Plant Description
The Nuon (formerly Demkolec) plant at Buggenum in the Netherlands was the first of the
European IGCC plants. It started operations in early 1994. The gasification island comprises a
high pressure ASU, a dry coal fed oxygen blown Shell entrained flow gasifier, gas cooling by
recycle gas to about 800°C followed by a water tube syngas cooler (SGC) for saturated steam
raising, a fly ash cyclone and a barrier filter with ceramic candles at 250°C, water scrubbing,
COS hydrolysis, HS removal by the Sulfinol M solvent, gas saturation and water cleanup. The
2
gasification block diagram is shown in Figure 4-3. The power block is based on a Siemens V
94.2 gas turbine combined cycle with a turbine inlet temperature of 1100°C (equivalent to
Siemens ISO of ~1050°C). The NO emissions are controlled by both saturation of the fuel gas
x
with hot water and by the addition of nitrogen from the ASU. Because of the characteristics of
the Siemens gas turbine and Demkolec’s (the original owner) desire for maximum efficiency this
plant was designed with full integration of the ASU and the gas turbine. In other words, the
entire feed for the ASU is supplied (extracted) from the gas turbine compressor.
The ASU, of 1700-tonnes/day oxygen capacity, was supplied by Air Products and is designed to
operate at a pressure of about 10 bar (145 psia) as compared with the usual operating pressure of
5-6 bar (72.5-87 psia). It is fed by air extracted from the gas turbine compressor discharge at a
pressure of 10.4 bar (151 psia). The air is cooled by heat exchange against the compressed
nitrogen and led through a purification system for the removal of water, carbon dioxide and
hydrocarbons using two filter beds with alum and molecular sieve filters. These filters are
cleaned with heated nitrogen gas after which they are cooled. The two beds are used alternately.
The clean air is then further cooled and fed to a dual column cryogenic distillation system.
Reboil and reflux take place in the first column. A small flow of nitrogen, containing 0.1%
oxygen, leaves the top at a pressure of 9.8 bar (142 psia). In the second column 95% oxygen is
separated and leaves the lower end at a pressure of 3.1 bar (45 psia). Most of the nitrogen,
containing less than 1% oxygen leaves the top of the second column at 3.1 bar (45 psia). The
oxygen is compressed to 33 bar (479 psia) for supply to the gasifier burners. The higher purity
nitrogen is compressed to 61 bar (885 psia) for use in the gasification section for coal transport
and back pulsing of the ceramic filter. The control of this system across the load range while
maintaining all three gas stream purities represents a particular challenge. When the gas turbine
load is raised from 60 to 100% the pressure in the ASU rises from 6.9 to 10.4 bar, so that the
vapor in the columns condenses when the gas output needs to increase. The reverse occurs on
turndown.
The raw coal is dried and ground. There are three milling and drying units of which two are in
operation at any one time. The pulverized coal is then pressurized in two lock hopper trains,
each feeding two horizontally opposed fuel injectors. The gasification using 95% purity oxygen
takes place at about 1600°C (2912°F) and 25 barg (365 psig) pressure. Depending on the
reactivity of the coal a small part of the oxygen can be replaced by steam as a gasification agent.
The gasification temperature is chosen sufficiently high to melt the coal ashes, that emerge as
slag at the bottom of the gasifier. However, some of the ash, typically 20%, is carried overhead
in the form of flyash or fly slag. The fly slag that is separated from the raw gas in a downstream
cyclone and filter is currently sold.
4-34

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
The mechanical design of the gasifier is a vertical cylindrical pressure vessel protected by a
membrane wall jacket of tubes in which medium pressure (46 barg) steam is generated providing
the cooling necessary to prevent overheating of the vessel. At the top outlet of the gasifier the
raw gas is quenched with recycled coal gas of about 250°C (482°F) temperature from 1600°C
(2912°F) to about 800°C (1472°F), to ensure that the fly slag is solidified prior to entering the
syngas cooler heat recovery system. The gas quench section is connected through a steam-
cooled duct to the syngas cooler, which further cools the raw gas from 800°C (1472°F) to 230°C
(446°F).
The syngas cooler is a water tube boiler in which the gas flows from the top downward. The
vessel wall is protected from direct contact with the hot gas by wall heating surfaces. The main
heating surfaces consist of concentric cylinders of helical coiled tubes installed within the wall
panels. To facilitate mechanical cleaning (with rappers) of the cylinders, the tubes are welded
together to form a membrane. The steam conditions in the syngas cooler are 120 bar (1740 psia)
and 400°C (752°F). A metal with high chromium content was chosen for the high-pressure
cooling surfaces and a ferritic material was selected for the medium pressure surfaces.
In principle, the steam conditions could reach 139 barg (2015 psig) 540°C (1004°F). However,
for the design at Buggenum it was determined that the superheating of the high-pressure steam
could be more economically accomplished in the Heat Recovery Steam Generator (HRSG) at the
gas turbine exhaust rather than in the more aggressively corrosive atmosphere at the top of the
syngas cooler. Nevertheless, a small isolated test superheating section was installed to provide
information for potential future designs. The gasifier and syngas cooler vessels were both
supplied by Steinmuller.
The cooled syngas typically contains ~80% of the heating value of the coal feed. This is higher
than that achievable with the coal/water slurry fed systems of Texaco and E-Gas. The steam
generated typically represents 16-18% of the coal heating value. The steam make is a higher
percentage of the coal feed with the Texaco and E-GASTM systems.
After the syngas cooler, the fly slag is separated from the cooled raw gas by a cyclone and a
ceramic filter vessel of 4.2 meter (13.8 feet) diameter containing about 900 porous ceramic filter
elements supplied by Schumacher Umwelt und Trenntechnik. The ash is discharged by lock
hopper systems and can be recycled via the coal milling and drying system. However, the fly
slag is of very low carbon content and is currently sold and is not recycled. After fly slag
removal, a portion of the “solids free” (<5mg/Nm3) gas is compressed and recycled as quench
gas to the top of the gasifier. The balance of the gas is scrubbed with water to remove halogens
and other water soluble components, preheated by a feed/effluent exchanger to ensure that the
gas is above the dew point, and fed to fixed bed reactors to convert the traces of hydrogen
cyanide (HCN) and carbonyl sulfide (COS) by the following hydrolysis reactions:
HCN + H O = CO + NH
2 3
COS + H O = CO + H S
2 2 2
4-35

Figure 4-3
Shell Gasification Process as used at NUON, Buggenum
The gas is further cooled and subsequently desulfurized in a selective acid gas removal process
using the Sulfinol M solvent, which reduces the sulfur compounds down to 20 ppmv. The HS
2
rich solvent is stripped in a regenerator column and the HS rich gas is converted into elemental
2
sulfur in a Claus plant equipped with a Shell Claus Off-gas Treating Unit (SCOT). The Claus
plant is operated with oxygen and also removes traces of NH and HCN resulting from the
3
wastewater stripping columns. Over 99% of the sulfur is recovered as liquid elemental sulfur.
The off-gases are incinerated.
The wastewater treatment plant receives water from the wet scrubbing system and the slag
system of the slag bath. After wastewater stripping a buffer system allows the removal of
suspended solids and after dewatering by a filter press these are discharged as chemical waste.
In the next stage of treatment, fluoride and carbonate sedimentation is promoted by the addition
of Calcium Chloride. The heavy metals are removed as sludge from a flocculation unit to which
sulfide is added. The water then passes over a sand bed where additional suspended solids are
removed before moving to the evaporation unit. Condensate is returned to the gasification
process and the soluble salts crystallize out and are removed in solid form. There is a build up of
complex cyanides in the discharge salts and the condensate from the water treatment unit.
The coal gas is mixed with nitrogen from the ASU and then saturated by direct contact with hot
water in the saturator tower and preheated with hot boiler feed water to 300°C (572°F) before
being fed to the gas turbine combustors. In this manner the lower heating value of the coal gas is
reduced from11 kJ/kg to about 4.3 kJ/kg.
4-36

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
The gas turbine combined cycle equipment supplied by Siemens is based on a 155 MW V94.2
gas turbine and a 130 MW condensing steam turbine with reheat in a single shaft arrangement.
The gas turbine has two vertical silo combustion chambers on either side of the shaft. The hot
zone of the flame cylinder is lined with spring mounted ceramic tiles and is followed by a mixing
zone with metal tiles which direct the hot gases into the metal tiled inlet casing. In order to
accommodate the need for low emissions on both coal gas and the secondary fuels (natural gas
and fuel oil), a triple fuel burner with coal gas admission added at the outer perimeter of the pre-
existing low emission hybrid burner design. The coal gas is admitted through a ring shaped swirl
nozzle with a large cross section around the natural gas and fuel oil nozzles. These burners can
also operate with two fuels at the same time.
A full description of the gas turbine combined cycle plant and the control system used for
integrated control is contained in a Siemens/NUON paper given at the 2002 Gasification
Technologies Conference.
Integration and Gas Turbine Operating Experience
The NUON plant was designed with full integration of the ASU and the gas turbine. In other
words the entire feed for the ASU is supplied (extracted) from the gas turbine compressor
discharge. This tight integration has led to some operational sensitivities and complexities so
that in the future only partial integration is recommended. Although the optimal degree of
integration is particularly dependent on the design characteristics of the gas turbine and its
compressor, this conclusion accords with EPRI’s general analysis of the merits of various
degrees of integration. At Wabash and Tampa, the ASU’s are supplied by their own separate
compressors so this problem does not arise. For many years the main problem encountered at
this plant was combustion-induced vibrations and overheating. In contrast to the multiple can
type combustors on the GE F gas turbines the Siemens V94.2 has two external vertical silo type
combustors situated each side of the shaft. GE/EPRI/Shell/DOE conducted full-pressure full-
load syngas combustion tests on the F combustors in 1990-2 prior to installation in the US IGCC
projects. Unfortunately, Siemens obviously could not conduct full-scale silo combustion tests
before installation, and it was necessary to utilize multiple empirical “cut and try” design
changes in the field to alleviate this problem.
In order to maximize efficiency, the pressure drop across the fuel gas control valve was only
about half that normally used for natural gas. This probably contributed to some of the problems
of maldistribution, lack of combustion control, and vibrations. The combustor design
improvements made from 1997 onwards have reduced the vibration problem and the availability
in subsequent years has been greatly improved.
Gasification Process Operating Experience
The coal milling and drying unit has functioned as designed after some problems were solved in
the initial operating period. A spare unit has been installed to permit maintenance activities
while the IGCC is in operation. The coal is typically dried to ~2% moisture.
4-37

The coal pressurization and feeding has sometimes been a factor that has limited plant capacity.
Periodic problems occur with hang-ups of coal flow in the bottom cones of the lock hopper
vessels. Changing coal blend properties can cause flow interruptions and it is important to avoid
any opportunity for ambient temperature changes or other process variables to create
condensation. Some recent system modifications have improved coal feeding.
The Shell gasifier has generally performed well and the successful scale up from the 250 mt/day
gasifier at Houston (SCGP-1, which was operated 1987-91) to the 2000 mt/day unit at
Buggenum has been amply demonstrated. Many types of coal (from Australia, China, Colombia,
Poland, South Africa and Venezuela) have been successfully gasified as well as many blends.
The coals gasified at Buggenum were not initially specifically selected for gasification, but were
blended for lowest possible costs and general use in the Dutch Pulverized Coal (PC) power
station boilers. These coal blends therefore did not have the preferred ash compositions for
gasification and because of their generally high ash fusion temperatures the addition of flux was
nearly always required. However, currently this situation has changed since the PC plant at the
Buggenum site is no longer in operation and the coals for the IGCC can be selected on other
criteria. The main conclusions from the coal tests at Buggenum are:
• A large variety of coals can be gasified all with carbon conversions of 99% or more.
• In most cases bituminous coals will require steam injection (~7-10% steam in oxygen) and
oxygen/MAF (moisture and ash free) coal ratios of 0.95- 1.05, producing syngas with 1-3%
CO.
2
• Cold gas efficiency is very coal dependent in the range 75-83% and is very close to the
design values.
• The total energy (syngas + steam) recovered from coal is also coal dependent in the range 93-
97% (LHV).
The coal burners (fuel injectors) have passed their initially anticipated lifetime of >8000 hours
and a lifetime of up to 16,000 hours is realistic (one burner has just achieved 21,000 hours!).
Much shorter lifetimes are experienced with the coal/water slurry fed GE/Texaco and E-Gas
gasifiers.
There were some early problems with the discharge of slag lumps in the slag sluicing system,
which were initially solved by increasing the size of the lines and valves. The amount of slag
fines in the bottom slag discharge was initially higher than design and so a vacuum filter was
added to capture the fines for recycle. Further adjustments to the gasifier burners resulted in a
lower production of bottom slag fines and avoidance of lumps. The main slag has a carbon
content < 0.5 % and is sold as a replacement for sand in concrete, cement and asphalt. The
recycle quench system has worked well and while deposition in the syngas cooler has sometimes
occurred it has not been a major cause of outage. There have been problems with high ash coals
and certain blends causing slag agglomeration. If future plants are to be designed for similar
coals and for rapid load following then it is recommended that a slag crusher be used. There has
also been a failure of the refractory lined heat skirt below the slag tap. In future units, a cooled
membrane wall will be substituted for the refractory lined skirt.
4-38

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
Gas Filter
The gas filter had several early failures of the ceramic candle filter elements and operation was
rather sensitive. However, since the addition of a bottom fixation construction, which restricts
the lateral movement of the bottom ends of the suspended elements in July 1996, it has not been
necessary to replace any of the candles. The plant has elected to stay with the Schumacher
silicon carbide based candles and, unlike the Wabash project (which also originally had a
Schumacher designed filter), has not changed over to metallic elements. Originally, Schumacher
Type 40 candles were used but they currently use Type 20. There has been very little increase in
the 30-millibar pressure drop and a 5-year life for the candles is currently forecasted. In June
2003 the ceramic elements had ~20,000 hours of operation. The elements have been cleaned
periodically with water but their strength has not yet deteriorated. The captured fly ash is very
fine and low carbon (<1%). It is currently sold as a replacement for cement.
Gas Cleanup
The raw gas from a dry coal fed gasifier such as Shell has lower water content than the raw gas
from the slurry fed gasifiers of GE/Texaco and E-Gas. Dewpoint corrosion in the lower
temperature range is less likely to occur and it has not been a problem at Buggenum.
The wet scrubbing, sour slurry and sour water stripping systems encountered some early
corrosion problems due to a failure to recognize pH probe defects. The corrosion damage to the
syngas scrubber column was due to not recognizing (in time) that the pH probes had failed.
Additional attention to correct pH control should prevent recurrence of this corrosion.
The COS hydrolysis and Sulfinol M units have generally worked well achieving up to 99%
desulfurization.. Fouling of the upper layer of the HCN/COS removal catalyst bed by iron
containing particles originating from corrosion upstream of the catalyst bed has occurred. The
solution has been to bypass the first part of the bed and then wait till the second part is also
plugged by fouling. Another solution would be to apply radial flow through the cylindrical
catalyst bed instead of axial flow or to add a guard bed.
However, the Sulfinol system is sensitive to contamination and foaming during regeneration if
the reboiler temperature gets too high. Heat stable salts also build up in the circulating solvent
(as they have in the MDEA process at the other IGCC plants) and need to be purged or
regenerated. Occasionally there has been a significant carry-over of Sulfinol solvent to the
saturator, which has interfered with its operation and led to some corrosion. The condition of the
Sulfinol solvent is now carefully monitored to prevent recurrence of these problems. The HS
2
content of the clean fuel gas is <10ppmv.
Water Clean up
The amount of wastewater produced has been greater than design, which has given rise to overall
water management problems. However, the plant operates within the permit requirement of zero
wastewater discharge. Unfortunately the treatment step intended for the removal of heavy metals
from the stream going to the brine concentrator has not performed adequately and the process
needs further improvement in order to produce salt of the quality needed for reuse. The
4-39

wastewater treatment process has been described as being rather sensitive. The flocculation unit,
which uses iron hydroxide and sulfide, does not remove enough of the trace metals (including
Se) to permit saleable salt crystals to be made in the brine evaporation unit. The wastewater
treatment sludge can be recycled back to the gasifier so that its ash content ends up in the slag
and fly ash. The blowdown from the saturator goes to the wastewater treatment and on the
occasions when the Sulfinol has been carried over to the saturator this has created some
complications in the wastewater treatment.
Overall Operational Experience
From January 1, 1998 through most of 2003, the plant was dispatched as a commercial
production unit including some daily load following. As part of the transition to commercial
operation the owners conducted Failure Modes Effects Analyses (FMEA) on the most critical
systems and merged the Operations and Maintenance teams to one operational organization. The
plant, as usually dispatched, was turned down to 50% at night and brought up again to near full
load at ~7 a.m. The target is 80% coal gas availability. A ramp speed of 1.5 MW/minute was
demonstrated in gasifier load following mode. By adding natural gas the ramp could be
increased to 3.5 MW/minute over a limited load range. In 2004, the plant returned to base load
operation. Nuon’s acquisition of several NGCC plants in 2003 enabled the load following to be
handled more readily by these new NGCC plants.
The main problem areas and their solutions are further discussed as follows:
• ASU drain blockages and poor valve quality. Improved mol sieves and valve quality.
Nitrogen and LOX quality trips have been solved by improved ASU control. Some IP steam
has also been introduced as replacement of some of the nitrogen for NO control
x
• Slag Lumps: The key solution here is knowledge of the slag characteristics and consequent
adjustments to the gasifier operating conditions. They do not stop between coal blend
changes.
• Erosion and corrosion in the circulating slag bath water. Better quality piping has been
installed
• SGC Deposition (Pluggage) has been only an occasional problem area. In early 1999, the
SGC was plugged within 3 days on an Indonesian coal. Lumps formed on the support beams
(hot surfaces) at the top of the SGC. The solution was to change recycle gas rate to adjust the
gasifier exit temperature and to change the SGC rapper frequency.
• SGC Leakage: The leaks were in various locations and were not really caused by erosion or
corrosion but were due to construction defects. Some pipes should be more flexible and were
of insufficient expansion length. Many of the construction welds had not had enough stress
relief. In the superheat section, the plate between pipes had concrete that vibrated and cut the
pipes.
• Syngas Scrubber Corrosion: Caustic is used in the scrubber at Buggenum since the hydrogen
chloride in the raw gas has to be captured as NaCl for sale. The pH of the circulating
solution should be at a level such that very little CO and HS are captured. However, the
2 2
attempts at on-line pH measurement have been plagued with problems and they have now
4-40

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
built an off-line system. They now try to keep the pH at 8-9 and take care of the
neutralization later.
• Sulfinol Fouling/Aging: The nitrogen sent to the saturator contains 1-2% oxygen and because
there is some small carryover of Sulfinol to the saturator some sulfate is formed. A bleed
stream of the saturator water is no longer recycled to the Sulfinol unit and is now sent to
waste water treatment to eliminate one of the sources of Sulfinol degradation. There is some
thermal degradation of Sulfinol in the regenerator reboiler and the solvent has been
periodically reclaimed by vacuum distillation.
• Gas Turbine Natural Gas to Syngas Changeover: The changeover from natural gas to syngas
at start up is still difficult to control and the consequent vibrations and high NO are
x
troublesome. In 1999, such changeovers were ~75% successful with the failures attributable
to incorrect control valve settings. In the first quarter of 2000, the changeovers were 100%
successful. In normal operation the vibrations are manageable. However, at shutdown
damage has occurred at the location where the combustion chamber is connected to the main
body of the turbine. Vibrations have also caused damage to the burner air swirlers. The
modifications to the gas turbine burners and integrated control system (as described in the
2002 paper at the Gasification Technologies Conference) have reduced the vibrations to a
low level.
SEP/Demkolec’s role ended as of January 1, 2001 and the plant was subsequently bought by
Nuon, one of the Netherlands former distribution companies that, under deregulation, has moved
into the power generation business.
From 1999 -2003, the plant operated in a daily load following mode with 15 minutes notice from
the grid but was returned to base load operation in 2004. In April 2002, a continuous run of
>2500 hours was completed with a scheduled shutdown for gas turbine inspection.
The operating statistics for the NUON IGCC plant for 20004 and 2005 are shown in Table 4-8.
Note that in the Netherlands an annual gas turbine overhaul is required. This helps to account for
the relatively high % of time in planned outages.
