Current Operations at Buggenum
The Netherlands, as part of the European Community is a signer of the Kyoto protocol. In
partial response to this, the Netherlands has undertaken a coal covenant with coal users. This
requires the Buggenum plant to make a CO emission reduction of 200,000 mt/year which is
2
equivalent to 28-50 MWe derived from biomass. NUON has stated that if the plant cannot run
biomass as covenanted then the plant would not run at all on syngas and would only run as a
NGCC. Their target is 30 wt% of co-gasification by the end of 2004 and 50wt% by the end of
2006. The 50 MWe is equivalent to ~ 300,000 mt/year of biomass. Nuon has applied for a new
permit and accordingly will have to increase their attention to the measurement of heavy metals.
Sewage sludge has significant mercury content. Based on test measurements made in August
2002, Nuon believes that over 60% of the mercury reports to the fly ash (where leaching tests
show it to be immobilized) and > 30% to the sulfur so that in total about 98% of the mercury is
captured. Nuon has estimated that the use of 100,000 mt/year of sewage sludge solids at the
Buggenum IGCC plant would result in only 1.2 kg/year of mercury emissions. Whereas if this
amount of sewage sludge solids was fed to a PC plant or a cement plant the mercury emissions
would be 12-30 kg/year and 36-60 kg/year respectively. The ability to control mercury may be a
unique niche advantage for Buggenum.
The use of biomass as partial feed has been tested and more is planned to produce this “green
power”. This biomass feed is planned to be mostly wood dust and dried sewage sludge. The use
of this much biomass will increase the nitrogen usage, dilute the syngas and increase the syngas
CO content possibly adversely affecting the Sulfinol AGR and Claus plant operation. The plan
2
is to mill the wood to <1 mm at a central location and add sieved out wood dust from the wood
board industry and to transport it by ship to Buggenum where it will be stored in silos and
injected into the outlet of the coal mill (coal is milled to 90%< 100 microns). It will be a closed
system apart from the single transfer from the ship. Sewage sludge, when dried to a similar level
can be fed below the classifier of the coal mills. A gasification test was conducted at a pilot
plant in Freiberg, Germany on a 20/80 blend of dried litter/coal. The blend flowed very well and
the test was judged to be very successful. Further tests of up to 12.5% co-gasification have been
successful at Buggenum.
4-42

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
A test run adding 4-5% sewage sludge solids to the coal feed was conducted in April 2003 at
Buggenum. This showed some increased fouling of the heat exchanger surfaces as judged by the
increased outlet temperatures from the various sections and an increased outlet SGC outlet
temperature from 250-252°C to 265-268°C. Inspection of the deposits showed enrichment in
Iron species rich in sulfur and phosphorus. However, there was no perceptible rise in the
pressure drop across the flyash filter. One possible solution under consideration is to inject water
to the suction of the recycle gas compressor in order to increase quench gas cooling capacity.
Other possible solutions are blending and SGC cleaning modifications.
The lessons learned from the biomass test program at Buggenum from 2001-2004 have been well
summarized in a paper by M. Kanaar at a workshop held in conjunction with the 6th European
Gasification Conference in Brighton, UK in May 2004. The plant exhibited good fuel flexibility
with the main restrictions being the feed system (nitrogen supply), the syngas CO content
2
(affects AGR performance), permitted emissions and by-product quality.
In 2005, Nuon tested direct syngas start-up (no back-up fuel), but after 6 weeks of testing
concluded it was not successful. The Ansaldo designed burners overheated. One positive note
from the experience was that Nuon staff learned how to quickly switch out gas turbine burners.
Luckily for Nuon the natural gas market has changed in the Netherlands, and natural gas
contracts are not as expensive for peak demand as before, so some of the motivation for doing
away with back-up fuel is gone. However, about 7 weeks of operation was lost due to the syngas
start-up testing, they totaled 5700 hours of syngas production in 2005 (65% on-stream factor,
75% if the 7 week testing period is excluded).
In 2006, Nuon has new senior management and a new director. They are targeting 7200 hr/year
of operation at Buggenum (82%), and the plant has been running well so far - 88% availability of
power, >90% on-stream factor. They have limited gas turbine inspections to one weekend, but a
5 week outage was taken in May for gas turbine inspection and replacement of gas turbine
blades. They also installed a new cooled heat skirt design in the gasifier, which will eliminate
the need for annual inspection. Going forward they are premising only 3 weeks of shutdown
time per year.
The Buggenum gasifier can now handle up to 30wt% biomass. Nuon receives a 53 euro/MW-hr
credit for biomass-derived power; however, the Dutch peak power price can exceed 100
euro/MW-hr during the winter, and then it is better to maximize output by running only on coal
(with 30% biomass the plant output falls from 250 to 215 MW).
Staffing
The Buggenum plant reduced staff by 25% in 2004. They now have 140 employees including
maintenance personnel. Operations now run with 6 people per shift plus a team leader. Nuon’s
experience has been that it takes 4 years for an IGCC operator to be fully trained in all areas of
the plant. Nuon now hires new operators with the equivalent of a US bachelor’s degree.
4-43

Nuon – New Plant (Magnum) Planned
Beyond Buggenum Nuon has announced a project to build a 1200 MW plant (Magnum) that will
be based on IGCC technology. They have selected a site at Eemshaven in the Groningen area of
North Holland for the project. Shell technology has been selected. ABB Lummus is doing the
engineering work for Nuon and a go/no go decision is expected in mid-2007. Current premise
for NO emission is 40 mg/Nm3.
x
ELCOGAS IGCC Plant in Spain
Project Description
The second European coal based IGCC project is the ELCOGAS project in Puertollano, Spain.
The gasification unit was first commissioned in December 1997 and in March 1998 the gas
turbine was first operated on coal gas. The ownership of this project is by a consortium of
European utilities including Endesa, Iberdrola, and Hidroelectrica del Cantabrico (Spain), EDF
(France), ENEL (Italy), and EDP (Portugal). The EEC has provided some financial support.
The major owners are Endesa (40.88%) and EDF (31.39%).
Plant Description
The IGCC plant comprises an ASU, an oxygen blown, dry coal fed, water/steam cooled, Prenflo
(Krupp-Uhde) entrained flow gasifier (similar to the Shell design), associated syngas cooling,
gas and water cleanup and a power block based on a Siemens V94.3 gas turbine combined cycle.
As at Buggenum, this plant is designed as a fully integrated plant with all of the air for the ASU
being supplied (extracted) from the gas turbine compressor. NO control is accomplished by fuel
x
gas saturation and dilution with nitrogen from the ASU – again as has been practiced at
Buggenum. At Buggenum, the earlier V94.2 model has lower firing temperature than the V94.3
at Puertollano. It should be noted that Siemens often quotes an ISO firing temperature and has
given the expander inlet temperature of the Puertollano gas turbine as 1120°C. This number
differs from the firing temperature as defined and reported by GE. The silo combustors of the
V94.3 are also different from the V94.2 in that they are arranged horizontally on each side of the
shaft with a shorter more direct straight transfer line to the turbine.
For the fuel handling, 2600 mt/day, 50% local Spanish coal and 50% petroleum coke, are milled
and dried in two parallel coal preparation trains. Heat for drying is supplied by IP and LP steam
and natural gas.
The plant has been designed for a wide range of fuels as shown in Table 4-9.
4-44

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
Table 4-9
ELCOGAS Fuel Feedstock Range
Minimum Design Maximum
Lower Heating Value 4,500 5,522 7,000
Kcal/kg
Ash wt% 3 20.68 25
Nitrogen wt% 1.36 3
Sulfur wt% 3.21 4
Volatile Matter Wt % 13 15.92 35
Chlorine Wt% 0.02 0.5
Coal is pneumatically fed with nitrogen to the Prenflo gasifier together with 85% purity oxygen
from a high pressure ASU (supplied by Air Liquide). The raw gas is quenched at the outlet of
the gasifier with particulate free warm recycle gas and subsequently cooled to about 235°C
(445°F) in two syngas coolers arranged in series generating HP and IP steam. The gas is
dedusted in two vessels (LLB design) with ceramic candle filter elements of the Schumacher
model. Fly ash is discharged through lock hoppers into trucks for sale. The granulated and non-
leachable slag is transported to extractors via the slag crusher and a lock hopper system.
Halogens, alkalis, and ammonia are removed from the dedusted raw gas in a water wash venturi
scrubber separator at about 125°C (257°F). Desulfurization of the gas is conducted in the
COMBISULF process which comprises a COS hydrolysis unit followed by absorption of the HS
2
in MDEA (MethylDiethanolamine) solution. The H S rich gas from the MDEA regenerator is
2
sent to a Claus plant for production of saleable elemental sulfur. The waste gas from the Claus
plant is hydrogenated and recycled to the COS hydrolysis unit to avoid the use of an incinerator.
The combined cycle is based on Siemens V 94.3 gas turbine with an output of 200 MW (based
on ISO conditions) followed by a three pressure level HRSG supplied by Babcock & Wilcox
Espanola and a Siemens reheat steam turbine of 135 MW. The clean coal gas is saturated and
mixed with nitrogen compressed from the ASU and burnt in the silo combustion chambers of the
gas turbine (8 burners in each of the two silos). The gas turbine compressor supplies compressed
air for the gas turbine combustors and for the ASU. The sensible heat of the extracted air is used
for clean gas saturation (hot water generation), low-pressure steam production, and preheating of
the nitrogen.
The 17-stage compressor creates a pressure ratio of about 16:1. The first three stages have
variable inlet guide vanes. These are closed at part load, reducing the compressor air mass flow
down to about 75% of the base load mass flow, which results in a constant turbine exhaust
temperature down to about 60% load. The air control enables high combined cycle efficiency
even at part load. The four-stage turbine is air-cooled, except for the last turbine blade. The
cooling air is taken from different compressor stages in order to reduce air leakage within the
turbine. Vanes and blades of the first turbine stage are cooled with compressor discharge air,
which in turn is cooled by an external air intercooler, thus eliminating the need for film cooling
holes.
4-45

The HP and IP steam from the Prenflo gasifier is superheated in the HRSG and sent to the steam
turbine. Boiler feed water heating is also conducted in the HRSG and LP steam is extracted for
several consumers in the gasification island (GI). The steam turbine is of a two casing design.
In the first casing HP and IP steam is expanded. In the second casing LP steam is expanded
through a double flow turbine. Steam from the HP section is reheated and used again in the IP
part of the turbine. LP steam from the HRSG is added in front of the LP casing. The exhaust
steam flows to a condenser, which is cooled via a cooling tower.
The ASU is designed to produce very stringent specification product gas streams - 85% purity
oxygen for gasification and high purity nitrogen containing < 0.1% oxygen for coal transport and
inert blanketing of vessels. The balance of the nitrogen for dilution of the syngas must be < 2%
oxygen content. 300,000 Nm3/hr of 14 barg (203 psig) pressure air are cooled and purified
before separation in a dual column distillation system. The first column operates at medium
pressure and yields pure liquid nitrogen at the top. The low-pressure column yields oxygen at
the bottom and both gaseous impure and pure nitrogen at the top. The ASU is operated and
controlled in load following mode, i.e., oxygen and nitrogen production follows gasifier
requirements. Rapid response of the plant to variations in product requirements is achieved by
specific controls. The total integration of the ASU with the IGCC leads to particular operating
conditions. When the load is raised from 50 to 100% the pressure of the air feed to the ASU
rises from 10 bar to15.5 barg (145 to 225 psig) and the reverse occurs on turndown. The plant is
designed to maintain constant product pressures and purities even though the inlet air pressure
fluctuates with gas turbine load. Storage of both liquid oxygen and nitrogen is also provided.
A full-scope training simulator with application for engineering analysis of control concepts and
operational procedures was developed. Major emphasis was placed on the non-conventional
plant systems. The simulator was developed on a first principles simulation approach for the
processes in the combined cycle, ASU and gasification island. The simulator was a joint
development of ELCOGAS with ENEL, EDF and TECNATOM (Spain). The training sessions
are conducted under a high physical and functional fidelity regarding plant controls and Man
Machine Interface (MMI), as well as high flexibility through a friendly management of the
instructor station capabilities (malfunctions, remote parameters, trainee intervention lists, replay
etc). A rigorous configuration control assures the integrity of the simulator over the plant life
cycle.
Operating Experience
The Siemens V 94.3 gas turbine was first fired on natural gas in April 1996, and its 100-hour test
was completed in September 1996 achieving full design load. Since that time it has operated as
required on natural gas. In early ‘97, a silo tile broke loose and some turbine blades were
damaged. A similar problem occurred at Buggenum in ‘94. This was one of the first V94.3’s to
be commissioned. It is noteworthy that in their latest designs, Siemens (e.g. with the V 94.3A)
has moved away from the silo type combustors and now uses can annular type combustors.
The first operation of the gas turbine on coal gas was March 20 1998. The first introduction of
coal to the four main fuel injectors of the gasifier took place on December 19, 1997. The highest
output from the gas turbine and combined cycle was achieved on January 12, 2000 at 196 MW
and 330 MW (gross) respectively.
4-46

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
The operating statistics through July 2003 are shown in Table 4-10.
Table 4-10
ELCOGAS Production Statistics GWh 1998 - 2003
Year IGCC Hours Natural Gas GWh Syngas GWh Total GWh
1998 37 744 9 752
1999 1368 836 335 1171

2001 5144 321 1391 1712

2003 4692 301 1371 1672
The plant operations markedly improved in 2001 and 2002 (See Table 4-10) as the various
problems were identified and in many cases satisfactory solutions found. The main activity was
to find solutions in the problem areas of reliable feed control to the gasifier, slag removal and gas
turbine combustor and control system design that can operate without excessive vibration and
overheating.
The grinding plant design exhibited problems in scale up of the equipment, weakness of
auxiliary equipment and control of size distribution. Blockages were experienced in the gravity
flow under high pressure and the fluidization in the cones of the vessels was unstable. Coal dust
flow measurement has been erratic with density transients and nitrogen consumption has been
higher than design. The flame monitors require a lot of maintenance and supervision.
The slag bath effectively shatters the slag but the fine slag is greater than design. A decanter,
valve modifications and rerouting of the slag water has been added. New thermocouples and
flame monitors were installed in mid 2001 to establish a better slag layer in the gasifier to
increase carbon conversion. Erosion of components has repeatedly decreased the capacity of the
slag removal system. Cold temperatures have sometimes led to blockage of the slag hole at the
bottom of the gasifier.
The syngas cooler (SGC) surfaces have experienced high fouling, although this has not been a
cause of outage. The rappers have not been as effective for surface cleaning as they have been at
Buggenum. A 2002 paper has given some interesting information on the SGC deposits showing
an unusual concentration of Germanium and Arsenic sulfides in certain areas of the deposit
layers. The same paper also gave a positive evaluation to the use of Sanicro 28 in SGC service.
The filter performance was not as expected and a progressive impairment of the ceramic filters
led to their replacement. A new set of filters was installed in October 2001 and since then the
performance has improved however the fouling factor increased and tripled over 5300 hours of
operation resulting in a high-pressure drop. The operating life of the gas filter elements is
shorter than expected from the Buggenum experience.
4-47

In early 2001, contamination and corrosion occurred between the water scrubber and the COS
hydrolysis reactor. The pipe materials in this location have subsequently been upgraded but
corrosion in the colder areas of syngas cleanup has continued.
Fouling of the COS reactor preheater by salts has limited the reactor temperature during long
runs resulting in increased COS in the syngas. A parallel preheater has been installed.
Replacement of the COS hydrolysis Alumina based catalyst by a Titania based catalyst has
increased catalyst life and has reduced formate production. This lower formate production
reduces the formation of Heat Stable Salts (HSS) in the MDEA and the overall MDEA usage. A
moisture separator has been installed to prevent gas condensate from entering the MDEA
absorption column. The rest of the gas cleanup has experienced only minor problems and gas
composition of 7ppm COS and 5 ppmv H S has been achieved.
2
The combined cycle has been a source of major problems. There have been a number of trial
and error changes of the syngas burners in an attempt to avoid humming and overheating,
particularly during fuel changeovers at startup and shutdown. The selection of fuel/air ratio,
turbine outlet temperature and establishing stable extracted airflow are crucial for a smooth
natural gas/coal gas switchover. The development of a functional gas turbine control system has
been troublesome with a large number of control design modifications. In early 2001, deposits
were found at the combustor burners that were later diagnosed as pipe scale. Because of a
history of burner overheating and broken tiles gas turbine inspections were conducted every
1000 hours for cleaning, tile gap adjustments and burner changes. As previously mentioned a
more recent problem was discovered during the 50,000 equivalent hours gas turbine overhaul in
April 2003. Deformation of the flange between the compressor and turbo expander was found.
During the subsequent 3 month outage, new burners and a new control system was installed.
New burners with less sensitivity to deposit formation were installed (using modifications
developed at the ISAB IGCC plant). A new control concept with pressure control on the
extracted air side over the full gas turbine load range was also added. This has resulted in much
reduced tile wastage and burner overheating. The new burners improved the operation and since
then the gas turbine has operated without humming or overheating using preventative inspection
every 4000 hours.
Nitrogen usage in commissioning and during operation is higher than design (it is also higher
than design at Buggenum). The complete integration of the gasifier oxidant delivery and the gas
turbine penalizes the time required for plant commissioning. The duration of the plant startup
procedure is mainly determined by the ASU product availability. Depending on the precondition
the startup can take 12-72 hours. The use of an independent air compressor would probably be
cost effective. Since demand for power is typically low at weekends a prolonged startup period
or inability to go to reduced load and cold/hot standstill would be a handicap for this plant in a
competitive market.
Some interesting tests were conducted in 2001 adding Meat and Bone Meal (MBM) (from the
large animal slaughter necessitated by the devastating foot and mouth and mad cow epidemics in
Europe) at 1% and 4.5% by weight to the standard feed mix. No particular problems were found
in running the plant with this amount of MBM material.

One major cause of outage in both 2004 and 2005 was a fault in the gas turbine main transformer
bushing. After a change the power block was only available for dispatch in open cycle with
natural gas only. There have also been water leaks in the gasifier membrane wall due to flow
obstruction. The design has been changed. In 2004 there were gas leakages in the raw gas piping
due to incorrect selection of materials.
Elcogas had an availability of 52% on syngas in 2005. Half of the unavailability was caused by
the power block. The gas turbine IGV actuator failed (broken bolt caused by long-term
operation under IGV control with a “weak” IGV design – Elcogas has since made several
changes in the IGV linkage), and a broken blade was found in one stage of the compressor.
Siemens says they have not seen this failure before in other V94.3 compressors.
Problems in the gasification block have included the COS hydrolysis catalyst. Elcogas changed
from an alumina-based catalyst to a titanium-based catalyst (at a cost of $600K). Unfortunately,
the titanium catalyst (supplied by Axens) was damaged by oxidation in August. Also, in January
2006, it experienced a temperature excursion >250ºC during the initial warm-up. Elcogas then
replaced it - and again at the end of April. They have decided to switch back to using alumina
catalyst which is only 1/8th the cost of the titanium catalyst.
4-49

Elcogas experienced two slag blockages in 2005. They were ultimately caused by water flow
distribution problems within the gasifier membrane wall. This mal-distribution eventually led to
tube leaks and the leaks caused local cool spots which created the slag blockage. The flow
distribution problem is related to the formation of magnetite within the system and those
particles block the restriction orifices which are supposed to ensure equal flow to each tube in the
membrane wall. They have changed the design of the distribution header to have all tubes
coming from the top of the header to minimize entrainment of particles into the tubes.
For 2006, Elcogas is planning a gas turbine overhaul in October – 25,000 hours since the
previous overhaul. They have also recently signed a 9 year maintenance agreement with
Siemens.
Other Operating Coal Gasification Projects
A database of the worldwide commercial gasification facilities has been compiled for the US
DOE and the Gasification Technologies Council (GTC). A report “Worldwide Gasification
Industry Report” and a data base package is available from DOE or GTC. The database contains
records for 161 real and planned commercial scale gasification projects, representing a total of
414 gasifiers with a combined rating of 446 million Nm3/day of syngas. If all this syngas was
converted to electricity using IGCC it would equate to ~33,300 MWe.
This report is not intended to give a comprehensive description of all commercial coal
gasification plants. Many of the largest plants have been in existence for over 20 years and
utilize the Lurgi dry ash fixed bed gasifiers. Because of the need for sized coal and by product
tar issues it is unlikely that this technology would be employed in new plants in the OECD
countries although there is some possibility in China. Sasol employs over 70 of these gasifiers at
three complexes in South Africa to make Fischer Tropsch liquids for transportation fuels and
chemicals. The Dakota Gasification plant in North Dakota uses 12 of these gasifiers to make 3
million Nm3 (140 Million scfd) of pipeline quality substitute natural gas and a variety of other
chemicals. These plants are well documented in the literature and will not be discussed further
in this report beyond what is discussed in Chapter 2.
The coal gasification plants discussed in the following paragraphs have some features that may
be of relevance when considering new gasification and IGCC applications.
SVZ GCC/Methanol Plant at Schwarze Pumpe, Germany
The Schwarze Pumpe site is located about 150-km southeast of Berlin in the area of large lignite
reserves. The town gas plant at this location started production in 1964 with 24 fixed bed dry
ash Lurgi type gasifiers and at its peak it supplied 75% of the town gas consumption of the
former GDR. After reunification the town gas from lignite was replaced stepwise by natural gas
until 1996 when the town gas production ceased and the conversion of the gas supply grid to
natural gas was completed. There are also two other types of gasifier at SVZ. In 1969, one of
the former fixed bed gasifiers was converted to a refractory lined entrained flow gasifier and
subsequently used for the gasification of tar and other liquid fuels. In 1998, Lurgi announced
that it would license this liquid fuel gasification technology as Multi Purpose Gasification
4-50

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
(MPG) technology. Starting in 1985, a 700 mt/day dry coal fed down flow entrained flow
reactor lined with a refractory covered water cooling screen was used for the gasification of
lignite. This dry coal fed technology has been previously described in the literature as the GSP
(Gaskombinat Schwarze Pumpe) technology. For a while it was marketed by Babcock Borsig
Power (BBP) as the Noell technology under a non-exclusive license. In 2003, Future Energy
Gmbh took over the gasification know-how from BBP including the various Noell entrained
gasifier designs and the test facilities and pilot plants in Freiberg. In 2005, the FutureEnergy
technology and the SCV plant was acquired by the Sustec Group of Switzerland. In June 2006,
Siemens acquired the FutureEnergy technology from Sustec however the facilities at Schwarze
Pumpe remain with Sustec. A scale-up of the gasifier to 500 MWth is planned in China and a
further scale-up to 1000 MWth is planned at Schwarze Pumpe to supply the syngas to a 600,000
mtpy methanol plant. For additional details see Chapter 2.
After the reunification of Germany, permits were sought for conducting gasification tests on
waste materials such as contaminated solids/liquids, sewage sludge, plastics, rubber, and other
difficult wastes. These tests were completed successfully and in 1996, the Berlin Water
Authority acquired SVZ.
The current SVZ plant is operated for the co- gasification of wastes with the local lignite (50-
60% wastes 40-50% lignite). There are receiving bunkers for sewage sludge, plastics,
contaminated wood, fluff, pelleted waste etc. and agglomeration facilities for sewage sludge
(piston press briquetting) and plastics (extruders). The solid wastes are gasified in seven fixed
bed gasifiers. Since these gasifiers need a lump feed (20-80mm) the wastes are briquetted or
pelletized for feeding through the lock hoppers to the fixed bed gasifiers. The gasifiers have an
inside diameter of 3.6 meters and operate at a pressure of 24 barg. Gasification is accomplished
by countercurrent operation of the descending fuel against the rising gasifying agents of
steam/oxygen introduced at the bottom.
Liquid wastes –such as tar/oil from the fixed bed gasifiers, used oils, solvents, and oil/water
emulsions- are gasified in the two entrained flow reactors. The gases from the entrained flow
reactors are partly conditioned in a sour shift reactor prior to removal of HS in a Rectisol unit.
2
The HS rich gas is burnt and the flue gases scrubbed with limestone slurry to produce gypsum
2
for sale. Some of the gas goes to a 100,000-tonne/year Methanol plant. The balance of the gas
plus the purge gas from the Methanol plant are fed to a GE 6 B gas turbine combined cycle
producing about 60 MW power plus hot water for district heating. The gas turbine can also fire
natural gas or fuel oil. Steam is produced at three-pressure levels 35 bar, 15 bar and 5 bar.
A 3.6 meter diameter British Gas/Lurgi (BGL) slagging fixed bed gasifier (2.5 times the cross
sectional area of the Westfield unit) is currently being tested at SVZ with some support from the
EU. This BGL gasifier replaces two of the older fixed bed dry ash gasifiers. The capacity of the
gasifier will vary with the waste feed properties and composition and is currently quoted as 24-
30 mt/hour for the waste/lignite mixture. The steam requirement for gasification is much
reduced from a steam /oxygen ratio of ~7/1for the dry ash Lurgi to ~0.6/1 for the BGL. The
BGL gasifier has no stirrer or distributor (unlike Westfield) and the gasifier has experienced
some problems with channeling and consequent outlet temperature and gas quality variations
(average outlet temperature 500-600°C). When run with coal it has been demonstrated to
perform in a similar manner to the Westfield unit although the outlet temperature is somewhat
higher since the SVZ unit is refractory lined. The raceway has a temperature at ~2000°C and the
4-51

slag is tapped about 8 times an hour. The reported slag quality is good with only 1.3% loss on
ignition (LOI) and extractable metals of 0.2% meeting the relevant disposal requirements
according to TA Siedlungsabfall, Class 1.
During a performance test the following production and consumption figures were achieved:
• Mixed fuel rate (75% waste 25% lignite) 27 mt/hour
• Specific Oxygen consumption <0.2 Nm3/Nm3
• Gasification Stem/Oxygen ratio <1.0 kg/Nm3
• Raw fuel gas production (dry) 31,000 Nm3/hour
The BGL technology is now owned by Envirotherm Gmbh (An Allied Technologies Group
Company). The Allied Technologies Group has formed the Allied Syngas Corporation located
in Wayne, Pennsylvania that has an exclusive license to commercialize the BGL process in
North America. See Chapter 2 for more details of the BGL technology.
SUV Project in Vresova, Czech Republic
In 1996, two GE 9 E gas turbines were installed at the Sokolovska Uhelna Vresova (SUV town
gas plant at Vresova in the Czech Republic to run on syngas produced from the Lurgi type fixed
bed gasifiers using the local lignite thus creating a 400 MW IGCC plant. The gasification plant
has 26 fixed bed reactors and although they are working reliably they produce an undesirable tar
byproduct and need a sized coal or briquettes as feedstock. It has therefore been proposed that
two oxygen-blown HTWinkler fluid bed gasifiers replace these Lurgi gasifiers. The HT Winkler
gasifiers should have no tar formation and should be able to utilize all the mined lignite. The
gasifiers would be designed for 82 mt /hour of lignite, 27.5 bar pressure and a gas outlet
temperature of 920°C (1700°F). However, there has been no significant progress since the
project was first described in a paper the 1999 Gasification Technologies Conference.
Eastman Chemical - Coal to Chemicals
Plant Description
The Eastman Chemical coal gasification plant at Kingsport, Tennessee has been in operation
since 1983. The plant comprises two GE/Texaco Quench type gasifiers (one operating, one
spare on hot standby) at ~1000 psig (69 barg). 99% purity Oxygen is supplied by pipeline from
an ASU owned and operated by Air Products. The ASU also produces Argon has 10-15 minutes
of gaseous oxygen storage. Molten slag is cooled in the quench and discharged through lock
hoppers and is currently land filled (non-hazardous). The quenched syngas is scrubbed to
remove particulates and cooled to generate 100 psig (6.9 barg) and 20 psig (1.4 barg) steam. The
cooling train and downstream units are single train. The blow down of the quench and scrubber
water is flashed and then clarified. The separated gray water is recycled to the scrubber and the
solids separated in vacuum drum and sent with the rest of the slag to landfill. The raw syngas is
split into two streams (called shifted gas and process gas). About one third is routed to a shift
reactor to produce enough hydrogen to make the correct CO:H2 ratio for methanol production.
4-52

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
Both streams pass through beds of pre-sulfided activated carbon for As and Hg removal at
system pressure and ~30°C, and are further cooled and sent to chilled methanol Rectisol
absorbers for removal of HS, COS and CO. The HS rich stream is sent to a Claus/SCOT unit
2 2 2
for recovery of the sulfur. CO is stripped from the Rectisol solvent by nitrogen so that the CO
2 2
stream is 70-80% CO with the balance nitrogen. The CO stream is usually vented to
2 2
atmosphere but it has also sometimes been purified, liquefied and sold.
The process gas is sent to CO/H cryogenic separation plants to produce CO used in downstream
2
synthesis units. The hydrogen rich stream from the CO/H cryogenic units is mixed with the
2
shifted gas and sent to the Methanol plants.
The chemicals production area contains the methanol, methyl acetate, acetic anhydride and
catalyst recovery plants. Methanol is produced in a conventional Lurgi unit as well as a 250
ton/day “Liquid Phase” unit, which is a joint venture between Eastman, Air Products and the
DOE. The methanol is reacted with acetic acid to produce methyl acetate in a proprietary
Eastman process. Methyl acetate is reacted with CO in a proprietary catalytic process to produce
acetic anhydride with co-production of acetic acid for recycle. The acetic anhydride is used to
make cellulose acetate, a major product of the Eastman complex with many uses.
Current Operations
By debottlenecking key areas of the gasification plant, the production rate has been increased
from 128% of the original design (Phase II expansion in 1991) to recent levels of 140% of
original design (currently 1240 tons/day of coal).
The coal is ~2.5% sulfur and has an ash content of 6-7%. There are three sources all within 50 –
100 miles. They prefer to run without flux and need a low chloride content coal since much of
the gray water piping is 316 stainless. There are two rod mills each of 60% capacity and two
Geho slurry pumps with an additional spare. They currently run at ~67% dry solids in the slurry
and they do use Ammonium Ligno-Sulfonate (ALS) as a slurry additive. They run the gasifier at
an indicated temperature of ~1350°C with an O/C ratio of ~1.0 that gives a percent carbon
conversion in the low 90’s (as a result of optimizing for CO production). They do not recycle
the unconverted material and all the slag is land filled. There are three layers of refractory with
the hot face (high Cr content) typically lasting 7-9000 run hours. The wear rate is typically
approximately linear with throughput. The bottom neck of the gasifier is a high wear area.
Eastman has its own brick masons and utilizes a removable cone for the bottom neck area that
requires only 20 hours for change out (ignoring time for cool down and reheat). A working
thermocouple is needed for startup and it typically lasts 50-70 days but most of the time the CH4
content of the gas is used as an indication of temperature (as in other Texaco units). The average
gasifier run length is now ~40 days (up from 20 days in the 1980’s) with the longest run 122
days (recent – previously 91 days). Fuel injector life is key so the injector cooling water is
monitored for CO content to judge when change out must occur. If an operating gasifier gives
such warning, then the spare gasifier can be brought on line in ~2 hours and take over production
without ever losing gas flow to the downstream plants, while the original gasifier is shut down.
The activated carbon beds remove >90% of the Hg in the syngas stream (Radian report 1999).
The Hg is in the form of HgS and elemental Hg. Some small amounts of Hg are also found in
4-53

the blow down water and plated out on heat exchange surfaces. Arsenic, As and some other
trace metals are also absorbed, with varying degrees of efficiency. Eastman has conducted some
test runs on petroleum coke but did not find any Nickel on the carbon beds. It appears unlikely
that Nickel carbonyl (Ni (CO)4) would be formed at the 250°C temperature of the scrubber at
1000 psig pressure (69 barg). For Hg alone the carbon beds would probably last ~10 years
however the absorption of water and other trace compounds requires a bed change out every year
or two.
Reliability/Availability
The on-stream time is defined as the days running divided by the total number of days for that
period. The Eastman gasification block has operated at 98.1% on-stream time for the three years
from September 2000 to September 2003. Of the 1.9% downtime, 0.8% was planned and 1.1%
was unplanned. This resulted in a forced outage rate (Unplanned outage hours/On-stream hours
+ unplanned outage hours) of 1.1%. Eastman estimates that single train gasifier reliability would
be ~90% with the refractory being the largest outage contributor at ~50% of the total outages.
The other miscellaneous causes of outages include some fouling, corrosion/erosion in the black
water circuit, slurry plugging, and electrical and instrumentation faults at startup (the latter
seemingly random). They do record all statistics on equipment and component failures.
Safety
The rate of OSHA recordable injuries (injuries requiring more than minor first aid) at the
Eastman gasification area has been 1% over the past three years. (This rate formula is about the
same as the percentage of employees who receive an injury of some sort over a year’s time.).
The last injury that required a day away from work occurred 10 years ago and was unrelated to
the process. The Kingsport site recently received re-certification as a Tennessee Volunteer Star
site. This involved a rigorous weeklong audit of the entire facility by Tennessee OSHA. The
gasification area was one of the areas targeted for a specific audit and no deficiencies were
found.
Future Development Plans
Eastman has agreed with DOE to host some improvement test programs at the Kingsport site.
These include slip stream experiments for hydrogen membranes and warm gas clean up
processes and new candidate refractory testing, including a test section based on the work at
DOE’s Albany, Oregon research center. Other gasification improvements may also be tested
possibly involving new feed system, new refractories, gasifier containment cooling and slag
removal systems. Eastman is also considering additional gasification based chemical projects at
other locations.
Eastman Chemical Gasification Services
Eastman is offering O&M services to the emerging gasification industry. They will also offer
technical consulting, the supply of critical spare parts and patented operating know-how
4-54

Coal-Based IGCC Demonstration Plants
(including a sulfur emission free start up). They have developed a reliability simulation model
that can be of great assistance in determining the sparing and operating philosophy for various
applications and project objectives. Twenty-two years of operating experience has resulted in
the streamlining of many operating procedures and practices. The average experience level of
their operators is 10 years. They have their own ASME qualified shop for pressure vessel and
component manufacture and they have their own team of brick masons. Lost production during
extended startups represents a major loss of income and it is in this area that Eastman believes it
can offer valuable insight and services. They may consider equity participation in projects;
however they would probably not offer equity if power was to be the only product.
In March 2003, Eastman Chemical Company announced that it had signed an agreement with
Texaco Development Corporation that enables Eastman to provide operational, maintenance,
management and technical services, as well as parts fabrication and sales for gasification plants
of Texaco Development licensees. Although nothing has been announced it is anticipated that
this business arrangement will continue in cooperation with the new GE Energy ownership of the
Texaco gasification technology.
4-55

5
BIOMASS AND WASTE GASIFICATION
Overview
The main challenge facing potential projects based on the gasification of biomass or wastes is the
diseconomy of scale. Because of the logistics of gathering and supply biomass and waste
projects are generally limited to <50 MWe. In Europe and Japan, there are several subsidy
programs available that can help to support certain biomass projects however there are
classifications of biomass that qualify for different levels of subsidy. Even with these subsidies
there has been a trend to look at co-firing and co-utilization of these fuels in larger coal based
boilers or IGCC units. This provides a more effective way of using these fuels to obtain both a)
the subsidy and b) a better efficiency of use and economy of scale. Probably the least cost option
is co-firing in an existing boiler if this can be done without significant additional corrosion or
other problems. The major biomass co-firing or co-feeding projects using gasification
technology are reviewed in this Chapter. There are three operating plants in Finland,
Netherlands and Belgium with atmospheric fluid bed gasifiers of 50-80 MWth based on biomass
and wastes situated adjacent to large coal fired boilers where the gasifier fuel gas is fed to the
coal boiler so that the biomass/waste ash is, to a very large extent, kept from contaminating the
coal fly ash. The Tampa Electric, Nuon (Buggenum) and ELCOGAS (Puertollano) IGCC
projects have each conducted test runs co-feeding various biomass/waste materials with coal.
The Nuon IGCC plant is targeting 10-20% of its power output to be from biomass/wastes by end
2006 (See Chapter 4 for more details).
In the US, about 20 states have Renewable Portfolio Standards (RPS) that requires a certain
percentage of the power produced should be from renewables by certain dates. In 2005, EPA
extended the renewables production tax credit to the end of 2007 and increased the value
to19$/MWh. The main candidates for fulfilling RPS requirements are Wind and Biomass, with
wind power probably being a more likely contributor in many locations. For biomass, co-firing
in existing boilers is the least expensive method of use provided that additional corrosion wear
and tear is minimized. In Europe, most of the coal boiler fly ash is used industrially and some
biomass ash (with e.g. its high potassium content) can make the fly ash unusable and, with its
increased leachability, more costly to dispose of. However, in the US less than 30% of the fly
ash is used industrially so that this less of a concern than in Europe.
At the 2002, Gasification Technologies conference, J.Schwager and K.Whiting of Juniper
Consultancy Services Ltd., UK presented a very good summary of biomass/waste gasification
technology and the related technical and policy issues. Gasification is being evaluated in many
European countries for the role it can play as an alternative to incineration and landfill.
However, in Europe, it appears that waste gasification processes will continue to be regulated as
incineration processes for the immediate future. Japan is also pursuing gasification for waste
5-1

utilization. In the US, there is an EPA initiative to position gasification outside the Resource
Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) and combustion regulations for hazardous waste (and
possibly ultimately for MSW). A number of commercial biomass/waste gasification plants have
been built. Juniper identified over 150 organizations worldwide promoting gasification and
pyrolysis technologies and selected 80 of these for further appraisal. Of these 80 a majority
employ gasification sometimes in conjunction with pyrolysis, melting or combustion. The
leading processes are shown in Table 5-1 by market segment.
Table 5-1
Leading Waste Gasification Processes by Market Segment
Market Segment Process
MSW (medium to large scale) Ebara, Nippon Steel, Mitsui, Technip, Thermoselect
MSW (small to medium scale) Brightstar Environmental, Compact Power, PKA
Industrial Wastes Alcyon, Global Energy, Krupp Uhde, Lurgi, Noell, Thide and
various Plasma processes
Post – Consumer Waste (Plastics, Auto Ebara, Enerkem, Lurgi, Takuma, GE/Texaco
Shredder residue, Elec.)
Forest, Pulp and Paper Battelle Ferco, Foster Wheeler, Kvaerner Chemrec, MTCI,
Thermochem, TPS
Relatively few of these processes can be considered commercially proven since there are only a
few operating plants. The following gasification processes have been identified by Juniper to
produce syngas and slag products according to the proposed US EPA rule – Thermoselect, PKA,
Global Energy (now ConocoPhillips and Allied Resources), Krupp Uhde (now Shell), Lurgi
(now Envirotherm/Allied Resources), Noell (now Siemens), Texaco(now GE Energy) and
Kvaerner Chemrec.
The European situation is currently much more inductive to biomass/waste gasification than the
US. The cost of land filling is much higher and legislation has been enacted to require the
diversion of all organic waste from landfill over a prescribed timeframe. The production of
electricity from sustainable fuel sources such as MSW and some industrial wastes is also
encouraged.
Recent news items over 2005-6 have been:
• A 400-600 ton/day MTCI gasifier has been built at Georgia Pacific’s mill at Big Island,
Virginia to be fed by non-sulfur containing caustic/carbonate liquor.
• A test program on gasification of wood waste is planned in the KBR transport pilot plant
gasifier at the DOE/Southern Company site in Wilsonville, Alabama in 2007.
• Chemrec built a pressurized pilot plant at Pitea near Lulea, Sweden for the gasification of
black liquor with oxygen at 30 bar pressure. A test program was initiated in September 2005.
Gasification of black liquor is a major challenge for refractories and other materials.
• There are no pressurized fluid bed biomass gasification projects in operation. The Varnamo
project ended in 1999 but there is an attempt to move it to a new location.
5-2

Biomass and Waste Gasification
• Carbona has supplied a biomass gasification plant to I/S Skive Fjernvarme in Denmark.
Carbona is a licensor of the GTI U Gas pressurized fluid bed gasification technology. A
Combined Heat & Power (CHP) plant is under construction that will produce 6 MW of
power and 20 MW of district heat for the town of Skive in Denmark. First of a kind
subsidies are being provided by the US DOE, the EU and the Danish Energy Agency.
• The gasification of wastes at Lahti, Finland (atmospheric Foster Wheeler fluid bed gasifier)
with the fuel gas fired in an adjacent coal boiler has continued to operate very well. A
similar sized project for Electrabel in Ruien, Belgium stated up in early 2003 and is operating
satisfactorily. A project using the Lurgi CFB technology at Amercentrale, Netherlands has
been less successful to date despite modifications to avoid tar condensation. This approach
has the advantage of keeping the waste ash separate from the coal ash and the larger boiler
enables a more efficient and cost effective use of biomass gas.
• The co-gasification of various wastes has been tested in some of the coal based IGCC plants
(Eucalyptus wood at Tampa, Meat and Bone Matter at ELCOGAS and wood waste and
sewage sludge solids at Buggenum).
• The co-gasification of wastes and lignite (up to 75/25 ratio) is being conducted in the 3.6 m
diameter BGL gasifier at Schwarze Pumpe. Plans for larger units in the US by Global
Energy are stalled at this time.
• Another possible use of biomass is through pyrolysis. Yields of bio-oil can be up to 75% by
weight. It generally has a density ~1.2kg/l and has a heating value about half that of
petroleum. However, in some areas the production of this transportable liquid fuel may find
uses in small engines and for heating. The ENSYN fluid bed process has found a few
customers (e.g. in Finland, and Italy). There is an upsurge in interest in bio-diesel and the
pyrolysis oils from some types of biomass may qualify for this nomenclature and use
• Atmospheric downdraft fixed bed gasifiers for small-scale applications <1.5 MWth have a
market in both developed and developing economies. However, although the tar problem is
less than with updraft countercurrent gasifiers it continues to be a real issue. Perhaps
catalytic tar cracking can be applied. There are many vendors of such systems - both real and
potential.
• A new initiative was launched in Europe in 2004-2005, and particularly centered in
Germany, for the production of Fischer-Tropsch diesel from the gasification of biomass.
Several projects on this topic were described in papers presented at the “IGCC & XTL”
conference held in Frieberg, Germany in June 2005.
Background
The conventional use of biomass fuels for combustion has always been an important component
of energy use and one that is being given increasing attention in view of global climate concerns
about the continued use of fossil fuels. There are programs underway sponsored by the US DOE
and the European Commission on the more efficient use of biomass. In the 1990’s, there were
major demonstration programs for the co-firing of biomass in conventional coal boilers in both
the US and Europe. It is currently practiced commercially in several European countries where
green power subsidies can be obtained.
5-3

The incentive for gasification of biomass is generally attributed to the potential for much higher
power generation efficiency with biomass IGCC than can be accomplished with direct
combustion boilers and steam turbines in the smaller size range appropriate for dedicated
biomass projects. Because of the nature of biomass, and the economics and logistics of its
gathering and supply, such projects are generally considered in the much smaller 10-60 MW size
range than the larger 500 MW coal based IGCC plants or other coal fired units. As a result of
the heightened concern over global warming there is an increased interest in renewable energy
projects including those associated with biomass. There are a number of biomass gasification
projects under development in the US and Europe. Most of these are based on the use of
fluidized bed gasification technologies. Entrained high temperature gasifiers are less suitable
due to the high alkali content of most biomass ash, the difficulty of feeding fibrous solid biomass
feedstocks of sufficient fineness to such gasifiers and the probable need for oxygen gasification.
Historically, there has been a large number of small fixed or moving bed gasifiers (both
downdraft and updraft) used fairly widely throughout the world in both developed and
developing nations on a variety of biomass feedstocks particularly wood chips. Some of these
have used the produced gas in diesel or IC engines for power generation generally in about the 1
MW size or less. However, these fixed bed gasifiers do have several limitations and drawbacks.
They need a sized feedstock and can tolerate only small amounts of fines without experiencing
maldistribution problems. The gases contain particulate matter and tars that can produce
problems in the downstream cleanup equipment and must be disposed of or possibly recycled as
briquettes or pellets. In many applications, the gasifiers are close coupled to kilns or to the
engines to minimize the opportunities for deposition and fouling. Because of these limitations
such gasifiers are not generally considered as prime candidates for biomass to power conversion
in the OECD countries where more stringent emissions regulations would require very
considerable additional cleanup equipment, labor and operating costs.
Forest Products Industry
One of the major potential markets for biomass gasification is with the pulp and paper (forest
products) industries where the biomass (wood) is already gathered to a central location for
processing so that no additional fuel transportation costs need be attributed to the biomass. The
two applications that seem to be most worthy of further consideration are fluid bed gasification
of tree bark, trimmings, foliage and branches (to the extent that they are not required for
replenishment of the forest floor) and the gasification of black liquor. These applications for
biomass gasification would potentially replace the direct burning of bark etc. in inefficient hog
boilers and the direct combustion of black liquor in Tomlinson boilers (a troublesome and high
maintenance operation).
A test program on gasification of wood waste is planned for 2007 in the KBR transport pilot
plant gasifier at the DOE/Southern Company PSDF site in Wilsonville, Alabama.
The main gasification experience with black liquor has been obtained on the Kvaerner Chemrec
gasifier. This is an air blown down-flow refractory lined single injector entrained flow reactor
system. An atmospheric unit with a capacity of 75 mt dry solids/day was operated at the
Frovifors kraft mill in Sweden 1991-3. The results from this unit provided the basis for the scale
up to the 300 mt dry solids/day unit that Kvaerner supplied to Weyerhaeuser at their New Bern,
5-4

Biomass and Waste Gasification
NC site in the US. This unit was commissioned in 1997 but its operation has been limited due to
materials related issues particularly with the refractory. Chemrec built a pressurized pilot plant
at Pitea near Lulea, Sweden for the gasification of black liquor with oxygen at 30 bar pressure.
A test program was initiated in September 2005. The black liquor gasification takes place at a
lower temperature, about 1000°C (1832°F), than the temperature in coal fed slagging entrained
gasifiers. At the 2006 Gasification Technologies Conference, Chemrec made a presentation on
this technology and their vision for the incorporation of the black liquor gasification into the
Pulp mill to produce DME/Methanol as a transportation fuel.
Potentially the GE/Texaco and Siemens/Future Energy gasifiers could also be used for black
liquor gasification.
In 1998, the American Forest, Wood and Paper Association endorsed and launched a
Gasification Combined Cycle initiative. In January 2000, the US DOE issued a solicitation
entitled “Development and Demonstration of Black Liquor/Biomass Gasification in the Forest
Products Industry”. One of the projects submitted in response to this solicitation has been
constructed. Georgia-Pacific Corporation together with StoneChem Inc plan to demonstrate
Pulse Enhanced TM Steam Reforming of non-sulfur caustic carbonate liquors for chemical
recovery (~200 mt/day liquor solids or 4-600 mt/day of liquor) at Georgia-Pacific’s mill in Big
Island, Virginia. The technology was developed and patented by Manufacturing and Technology
Conversion International (MTCI) and is currently licensed to StoneChem Inc for use in North
America. Pilot studies were carried out on a 23 mt/day unit at Inland Container’s Ontario,
California mill and on a 45 mt/day unit at Weyerhaeuser’s New Bern, North Carolina mill. The
Big Island unit has had a test program ongoing for several years and quarterly reports are
available. However, the main problems of poor carbon conversion (~60%), tar and soot
formation remain unsolved.
Pressurized Biomass Gasification Experience
Most of the larger biomass gasification projects in both the US and Europe are based on fluidized
bed gasification under either atmospheric or pressurized conditions.
Varnamo
Foster Wheeler Energy International and Sydkraft have been developing an air-blown
pressurized circulating fluid bed IGCC for biomass since 1991. A demonstration plant at
Varnamo, Sweden (6 MWe + 9 MWth for district heating) was operated by Sydkraft from 1993
to 1999. The plant logged over 8500 hours of gasifier operation and ~ 3500 hours of gas turbine
operation on biomass gas with individual runs over 250 hours. The demonstration program was
concluded at the end of 1999 and the plant is now shut down and mothballed.
The normal wood fuel was dried using a separate flue gas dryer to moisture content of 5-20%.
The fuel feeding system used a lock hopper, pressurized fuel silo and screw feeders primarily
designed for wood chips and fuels of equivalent density. About 10% of the air was extracted
from the gas turbine (Typhoon from Alstom) compressor, further compressed in a booster
compressor, and injected into the bottom of the gasifier. The gasifier operated at a pressure of
5-5

~18 barg. The biomass gas was cooled in a down flow firetube syngas cooler and the
particulates removed in a ceramic candle filter at 350-400°C. Tests were conducted with various
bed materials (originally limestone/dolomite) in the fluid bed gasifier to prevent re-carbonization
of limestone/dolomite and the tar and char in the product gas from creating deposits in the syngas
cooler. Magnesite (MgO) was eventually selected as being the most effective in controlling
deposition and fouling.
The ceramic candle filter elements also experienced some failures, and in the summer of 1998,
the project replaced them with metallic filter elements supplied by Mott Corp. The filter
continued in operation for another 2200 hours with good filtration efficiency and stable pressure
drop. An important aspect concerning hot gas filtration in this biomass application is that the
gaseous tar components should pass through the filter, the other tars should stick to the filter
cake, and not pass into the fine structure of the filter itself. A metallic police filter was also
installed downstream of the main filter to protect the gas turbine in case of a hot gas filter
breakage. A slipstream system for the catalytic removal of ammonia from the gas stream was
also tested with sponsorship support from the EC Joule program.
The gas to the gas turbine was of low calorific value of ~5-6 MJ/Nm3, however this proved to be
no problem for the gas turbine and a stable flame was established even when heating value was
lower than normal. 100% gas operation without liquid fuel support was demonstrated over an
operating range from ¼ to full load. The NO emissions were ~140 ppmv when operating on gas
x
from a high nitrogen fuel such as bark but were reduced to ~40 ppmv when operating with lower
nitrogen hardwood. Internal inspection of the gas turbine revealed no significant damage to the
key components.
During the early years of testing, forest residues and wood chips were generally used. However,
additional tests were also conducted on pellets (necessary because of the feed system design) of
other biomass such as straw, willow, bark, sawdust and refuse derived fuel (RDF). All of these
fuels were gasified satisfactorily.
Krister Stahl, formerly of Sydkraft, is developing a project that may involve the transfer and use
of the Varnamo equipment at another location utilizing oxygen for the gasification of wood chips
rather than air (as used at Varnamo) and to use the syngas for the synthesis of Fischer Tropsch
diesel.
IGT RENUGAS
A nominal 90 mt/day fluid bed gasifier based on the IGT RENUGAS technology was built at the
Paia sugar factory on the island of Maui in Hawaii. The unit comprised an air blown gasifier
(designed for 30 bar pressure) with a SUNDS barrel screw injector feeder, a primary cyclone, a
slipstream Westinghouse candle filter and a gas flare. The feedstock was bagasse and some
initial tests were performed in the last quarter of 1995. The feeder was a major problem area and
only a few short runs at low throughput and low pressure were made. In 1996, Westinghouse
assumed the role of project manager with the objective of long term testing of the hot gas filter at
full 30 bar pressure. The feed system was modified by the addition of a better feed chopper and
a lock hopper system. A few hundred hours of operation were completed in late 1997 however
5-6

Biomass and Waste Gasification
feed system problems persisted and only half pressure was attained. The project was halted in
1998.
The MnVAP (Minnesota Valley Alfalfa Producers) project to be located in Granite Falls,
Minnesota was a biomass IGCC project also based on the IGT RENUGAS fluid bed gasifier
(also sometimes referred to as the Carbona gasifier -- Carbona is the successor organization to
Tampella Power, an IGT licensee). This project was a combined protein and power project to
process alfalfa into a flash dried leaf and to use the stalks for biomass IGCC power generation of
60-75 MW capacity. In May 1997, gasification tests on alfalfa stalks were conducted on the
Carbona pilot plant gasifier in Tampere, Finland. The project was supported in part by the US
DOE NREL and a team of Stone & Webster and Westinghouse (who planned to supply the gas
turbine and hot gas filter). A Phase 1 design was completed with some additional support from
Enron. The project held a power purchase agreement (PPA) with Northern States Power (NSP)
to come on line by 2001. However, in April 1999, Enron withdrew its support and MnVAP
initiated termination of the PPA. DOE NREL later also withdrew its support.
Carbona
Carbona has signed a contract for a biomass gasification plant with I/S Skive Fjernvarme in
Denmark. Carbona is a licensor of the GTI U Gas pressurized fluid bed gasification technology.
A Combined Heat & Power (CHP) plant is under construction that will produce 6 MW (3x2 MW
gas engines) of power and 20 MW of district heat for the town of Skive in Denmark. First of a
kind subsidies are being provided by the US DOE, the EU and the Danish Energy Agency. The
size of this plant is about the same as the earlier Varnamo project in Sweden.
Choren Industries Carbo-V Gasification
Initial work on Choren Industries Carbo-V gasification process was conducted at a 1 MW pilot
in 1998-2001. The first step in the process is the autothermal pyrolysis of biomass in a kiln at
~550°C to produce pyrolysis gas (containing oils and tars) and a char. The pyrolysis gas is
gasified with oxygen in a downflow refractory lined vessel and the raw gas from the base is
directed cross flow to an upflow entrained refractory lined gasifier fed at the base with pyrolysis
char. The raw gas, allegedly free of tar is cooled, dedusted and water scrubbed. A pressure of
30 barg is envisaged for the gasification. The main objective is the synthesis of Fischer Tropsch
(F-T) liquid transportation fuels via biomass gasification. A Beta facility ~15 MWth has been
built and operated at Freiberg, Germany with support from the EU 6th Framework program. A
larger gasification plant of ~45 MWth, including F-T synthesis, is planned for the first quarter of
2007. The planned product annual output is 16.5 Million liters of “Sun diesel” from 75,000
mt/year of Biomass.
Atmospheric Pressure Biomass Gasification Experience
Ahlstrom (now Foster Wheeler Oy.) and Lurgi have both supplied operating atmospheric
pressure air-blown circulating fluidized bed (CFB) gasification units for over a decade. The
units have usually been close-coupled to a boiler or kiln with no gas clean up other than the
primary cyclone of the CFB so that the tars and any entrained char are burnt together with the
5-7

fuel gas in the boiler. The units have therefore generally relied on the boilers or kilns to provide
environmental clean up of the combustion gases. Both Lurgi and Foster Wheeler atmospheric
pressure CFB gasifiers have also found application in waste gasification projects located adjacent
to coal fired boilers with the gas being fed into the boiler fire box. (See following section).
Lurgi
Based on the results of pilot plant tests (including EPRI tests on wood and lignite in 1985), a 27
MWth Lurgi CFB gasification plant was commissioned in 1987 at Pols, Austria producing a fuel
gas for under-firing a rotary lime kiln. After several years of operation the unit was shut down
and the kiln converted to allow oil firing. In 1996, the largest to date Lurgi CFB gasifier of 100
MWth was commissioned producing hot fuel gas for direct use in the pre-calciner of the cement
kiln at Rudersdorfer Zementwerke (now Readymix AG). The fuels have included carbonaceous
lignite grate ash, coal, wood, and paper mill rejects. A maximum of four separate fuels can be
fed at one time through four separate hopper and feeding systems. Preheated air from the ash
cooling system is supplied as gasification air. After gasification of the residual carbon content
the ash (<2% carbon) is utilized as an aggregate in cement production.
Foster Wheeler (formerly Ahlstrom)
The first of Foster Wheeler’s Pyroflow gasification units started operation in 1983 on a limekiln
at the Wisaforest Oy’s cement plant in Finland. There are three other Foster Wheeler units in
operation, two in Sweden and one in Portugal, all of which are firing lime kilns as listed in Table
5-2. A portion of the generated gas is combusted to dry the bark and wood wastes from 50% to
15% moisture content prior to gasification.

MWth
