TPS Termiska Processer AB
In 1986, a 2 MWth CFB gasification pilot plant was commissioned at Studsvik Nykoping in
Sweden and tests on various biomass and waste feedstocks performed. TPS Termiska Processer
AB continued development of this Studsvik process. In 1989, TPS sold a license to Ansaldo
Aerimpianti SpA for the construction of a waste-fueled gasification plant in Greve-in-Chianti,
Italy. This plant has a capacity of 200 mt/day of RDF (Refuse-Derived Fuel) pellets with two
5-8

Biomass and Waste Gasification
CFB gasifiers each of 15 MWth operating at close to atmospheric pressure. The raw gas passes
through two stages of solids separation before being fed to a boiler. Alternatively the gas can be
fed to a cement kiln. The boiler flue gas is scrubbed in a three-stage dry scrubber system. This
plant was commissioned in 1992.
FERCO SilvaGas (formerly Battelle)
The Battelle biomass gasification process, licensed in North America by Future Energy
Resources Corporation (FERCO) of Atlanta, Georgia produces a medium heating value (HV) gas
(15-18 MJ/Nm3 or 4-500 Btu/scf) without the need of an oxygen plant. It is currently called the
FERCO SilvaGas process. It uses two physically separate fluid bed reactors - a gasification
reactor in which the biomass is converted via pyrolysis and steam gasification into a medium HV
gas and residual char, and a combustor that burns the residual char to provide heat for
gasification. Heat transfer is accomplished by circulating sand between the gasifier and the
combustor.
The process takes advantage of the high reactivity of biomass and throughputs in excess of
14,600 kg/hr-m2 (3000 lb/hr-ft2) can be achieved. Following successful tests on a 10 mt/day
atmospheric pressure Process Research Unit (PRU) at Columbus, Ohio a 182 mt/day
atmospheric pressure unit was built at the McNeil Generating station in Burlington Vermont.
The existing station comprises a 50 MW boiler fired with wood chips. In the first phase of the
gasification project, wood is to be gasified, conditioned for tar removal by a low cost proprietary
catalyst DN34 (slip stream only) and a water scrubber, and the product gas fired in the existing
boiler. In the second phase it is intended to add a gas turbine. The unit starts up on compressed
air blowers with in-line natural gas fired heaters. Once the refractory reaches ~550°C sand
circulation is initiated to bring the sand to the same temperature. At this point, wood is added to
the gasifier and combusted raising the temperature to ~830°C. When all of the upstream wood
feeders and downstream scrubber systems are lined up wood flow is increased to create a
condition of partial oxidation. The gas is burnt is the McNeil boiler and the char that is not
consumed in the gasifier is transferred to the combustor where it is burnt to increase the sand
temperature. Full steam gasification is initiated by turning off the natural gas and air supply.
Some of the tests conducted were in the partial oxidation mode with air and steam addition and
during which the heating value of the gas was 6.7 MJ/Nm3. Several runs in the full steam
gasification mode confirmed the predicted gas composition and heating value of 11-14 MJ/Nm3
and the predicted production rate. The process can handle varying moisture content woods (10-
50%) without significantly affecting gas heating value. One of the major problems encountered
has been tar plugging in the downstream water scrubber. Several test campaigns were conducted
(the latest in mid 2001) but the plant is not in regular use at this time.
EC THERMIE Biomass IGCC/CHP
The Directorate General for Energy of the European Commission (DG XVII) initiated a targeted
activity on the gasification of biomass for the production of electricity and heat within the
framework of the THERMIE program in 1992. Five proposals were submitted and three were
5-9

approved for financial support - “Biocycle” in Denmark, “Energy Farm” in Italy and “ARBRE
“in England. Of these only the ARBRE project was actually built.
The ARBRE (ARable Biomass Renewable Energy) project in the UK was developed by Arbre
Energy Ltd of Leeds, UK. This enterprise is led by the Kelda Group Company First Renewables
along with partners TPS Termiska Processor AB of Sweden and Royal Schelde of the
Netherlands, who constructed the plant. From 1993-7 TPS conducted many further CFB
gasification tests on various woods and wood residues and has developed a second circulating
fluid bed for the catalytic cracking of tars in the raw gas. The project is located in Eggborough
in North Yorkshire. The flow scheme comprises a fuel dryer, gasifier, tar cracker, gas cooler,
gas compressor (it is an atmospheric pressure gasifier), gas turbine, HRSG and steam turbine.
An 8 MWe IGCC plant using an Alstom Typhoon gas turbine (as used at Varnamo) with short-
rotation copse woods as feedstock was commissioned in 2001. The initial operations revealed
problems in several plant areas. The venture declared bankruptcy and there has been no further
operation of this plant.
RepoTech , Gussing, Austria
A 50 mt/day fluid bed atmospheric pressure gasifier has been supplied to the European Center
for Renewable Energy in Gussing, Austria for a program that plans to test the production of SNG
and Fischer Tropsch liquids and diesel as part of the new European initiative on bio derived
transportation fuels.
Co-firing of Biomass Gas in Boilers
There are also several biomass gasification projects in Europe in which the product gas from
atmospheric fluid bed gasification is co-fired in existing boilers. This approach has the
advantage that it avoids having to build a completely dedicated biomass power plant. Given the
vagaries of biomass supply this could be a prudent way of utilizing biomass and wastes. It also
keeps the highly alkaline biomass ash separate from the coal ash so that any sale, use or disposal
of the coal ash is not compromised by the presence of additional soluble alkalis. In many cases,
it is also not necessary to pre-dry the raw biomass fuel or to clean the gases so that the
investment cost is markedly lower than for a dedicated biomass IGCC and the overall system is
much simpler.
Foster Wheeler
Lahti, Finland
A project with Lahden Lampovoima Oy at a coal fired CHP plant in Lahti, Finland started up in
1998. The gasifier unit supplied by Foster Wheeler to the 350 MWth Kymijarvi power plant
(now owned by the city) is ~70 MWth input. It can substitute for ~15% of the annual energy
input to the Benson type once-through boiler, which is capable of 167 MWe if optimized for
power production or a maximum of 240 MWth of district heat production. The boiler operates
~7000 hours/year. In 1986, the plant was re-powered with a 49 MWe gas turbine the exhaust of
5-10

Biomass and Waste Gasification
which is used to preheat boiler feed water. The biomass available in the region includes sawdust,
dry wood and forest residues and bark. Recycled fuel (REF) composed of plastics, paper,
cardboard and wood equivalent to 300 GWh/year is also found in the region. Foster Wheeler
considers that in Europe there will generally be 30-150 MWth of this type biofuels available
within a 50-km transportation radius. The biomass feed is not pre-dried. There are two
receiving stations. REF and wood wastes are crushed, screened and fed into a large storage silo
where homogenization occurs with the biofuels and peat from a second receiving station. The
gasification takes place in a refractory lined steel vessel at ~850°C. The hot gas flows via a
uniflow cyclone through the air pre-heater to two burners located below the coal burners in the
boiler. When the fuel moisture is ~50% the heating value of the gas is only~2.2 MJ/Nm3.
The first operating period was from January 9 to June 2, 1998 during which ~2700 hours of
operation was achieved. The unit was in continuous operation from week 4 to June 2, 1998.
During 1998, 4730 hours of gasification were logged with an availability of 82%. Some
operational problems at the fuel processing plant created outages during the first half of 1998.
With regard to the gasifier, the problems have mostly been related to the use of shredded tires as
fuel to the gasifier when the accumulated wires blocked the ash extraction system. To date no
indication of abnormal fouling or corrosion of the heat exchange surfaces were seen.
Through March 2002, the gasifier had 21,000 hours of operation. In 2001, it operated 7089
hours at 96% availability and delivered 445 GWh (the original design value was 300 GWh).
Due to the high moisture content of the feed (up to 58%), the product gas heating value has been
relatively low, typically 1.6-2.4 MJ/Nm3 (43-64 Btu/SCF). With 60% moisture fuel, the LHV of
the gas is only ~1MJ/Nm3 and the output ~ 30 MWth but with 25% moisture fuel the LHV is
~4.5 MJ/Nm3 and the output is 70 MWth. However, the stability of the steam cycle, coal
burners and product gas burners was reported as excellent and there have been no abnormal
deposits on the heat transfer surfaces.
In 1998, the feedstock was 63% wood derived and 15% refuse but by 2001 the balance had
shifted to 38% wood and 34% refuse. The main boiler emissions of NO decreased by
x
~10mg/MJ (~5-10%) and the SO emissions decreased by 20-25 mg/MJ. CO emissions did not
x
increase. Due to the use of low chlorine coal the HCl emission increased ~5mg/Nm3 because of
the chlorine originating from the REF fuel and shredded tires that were used in the gasifier
during the test. There was some increase in the trace metal content of the boiler filter ash,
however, because the contribution of gasifier fly ash is small the filter material still meets the
leachability tests and it is permitted to be used in similar applications as used prior to the gasifier
addition.
Corenso Oy, Varkhaus, Finland
Foster Wheeler has also supplied a 40 MWth bubbling bed gasifier to Corenso Oy in Varkaus,
Finland that is used to recover Aluminum (2100 mt/year) from aluminum foil coated waste
materials. The hot raw fuel gas is fired in a new gas boiler equipped with a baghouse.
5-11

Electrabel, Ruien, Belgium
A 50 MWth CFB gasifier has also been built for Electrabel in Ruien, Belgium where the hot raw
fuel gas is sent to an adjacent PC boiler. The overall concept is similar to the Lahti project. The
gasifier is rated at 50 MWth for a fuel with 50-percent moisture and contributes 17 MWe of
power in the adjacent Unit 5 boiler. When fired entirely on coal, Unit 5 produces 1,330 GWh
and consumes 500,000 mt of coal per annum. Hence, the gasifier displaces 45,000 mt/annum of
coal, a reduction of 9 percent. This reduction in coal use is estimated to reduce coal derived CO
2
emissions by 120,000 mt/year. These reductions will be greater for fuels with lower moisture
contents and the maximum thermal rating of the gasifier is 86 MWth for a 20-percent moisture
fuel.
The installed cost was reported as 1,000 Euros/kW. The design electrical output is stated as 17
MWe so the total installed cost is 17 Million Euros. Hence, the capital charge for saving
120,000 mt/year of CO , spread over 20 years, is approximately 7 Euros/mt.
2
To obtain Green Certification, the gasifier can only operate on uncontaminated wood residues.
In Europe, these are categorized as Type A materials and consist only of wood containing no
paint, preservative, or glue etc. The annual wood requirement is estimated to be 100,000 dry
tons per year and because of the local chipboard industry, there is sufficient wood of this quality
within 20 km of the plant. The material used is presented in Table 5-3 below.
Table 5-3
Wood Materials Used in the Gasification Plant at Ruien, Belgium
Percent Moisture Content, LHV, MJ/kg
of total %
Saw dust and recycled wood 10 45 to 55 7 to 10
Forest residues such as bark and chips 40 45 to 55 7 to 10
Wood residues from chipboard industry 50 10 to 20 16 to 19
The wood normally has an ash content of 1 to 2 percent by weight. The wood residues from the
chipboard industry are mainly derived from sanding surfaces and are sufficiently fine to be fired
directly into a PC boiler. The fibers in this material are too short to be recycled back to the
chipboard manufacturing stage. For all materials the supplier is responsible for removing metal
and other forms of debris.
The gasifier and its solids handling facilities are included in a new building with a small footprint
attached to the Unit 5 power plant building. Some equipment is incorporated into the existing
structure. The gasifier vessel is approximately 30-m tall with an outer diameter (OD) of
approximately 5 m for the upper half. The OD of the lower half is closer to 4 m, giving the
gasifier a shape typical of that for circulating fluidized-bed combustors. The OD of the cyclone
also appears to be approximately 5 m with a 1-m OD line returning the collected dust back to the
gasifier at a location just above the air distributor plate. All these sections are refractory lined.
5-12

Biomass and Waste Gasification
The syngas leaves from the bottom of the uniflow cyclone, unlike a reverse cyclone where the
gas leaves from the top. The syngas duct below the cyclone contains a tubular air heater that
increases the inlet air temperature while decreasing the syngas from 850 to 750°C.
Sand is used as the bed material, introduced intermittently through its own feed system. This
consists of a feed hopper with a pneumatic delivery system and internal baghouse, with a rotary
seal feeding the sand into a screw feeder that delivers it into the gasifier. Limestone has been
used as bed material, but this breaks down too rapidly and has to be replenished more frequently
than sand.
The bed sand removed from the gasifier typically contains les than 1-percent unburned carbon
and is suitable for use in cement manufacture. Because of its high alkali content the amount used
has to be controlled to avoid exceeding alkali content limits. It is important that applications for
the ash be found, as landfill charges are prohibitively high. Only a small amount of biomass ash
and sand are transmitted into the boiler and this is insufficient to adversely affect the properties
of the coal ash.
From the exit of the air heater a 2-m OD pipe approximately 55-m long, snakes through the
existing structure to deliver the syngas to Unit 5 boiler. The syngas enters through two specially
designed burners set in opposite walls of the furnace below the lowest of the four rows of wall-
fired coal burners. The gasifier is operated at its maximum capacity and load is controlled and
followed by modulating the boiler coal flow. Of 50 MWth fed to the boiler from the gasifier
approximately 31 MWth is potential heat in the syngas, 18 MWth is sensible heat, and 1 MWth
is potential heat in the carbon.
Commissioning started late in 2002. In the period January to May 2003, 31.3 GWh were
generated with a 51-percent capacity factor. Factors affecting availability included the
following.
• Gasifier start-and-stop sequences as part of operator training.
• A 9-day outage for the final plant inspection prior to gasifier hand over.
• The PC boiler was out of service for 10 days.
• A 5-day outage to repair an air heater expansion joint leakage.
• A 2-day start-up delay to reprogram PLCs.
These factors are not expected to re-occur, so the capacity factor is expected to increase during
the remainder of the year.
Lurgi
Essent, Amercentrale, Netherlands
A Lurgi CFB gasifier project was built by Essent in the Netherlands at the coal fired co-
generation unit Amercentrale 9 that has a net production 600 MWe and 350 MWth heat. An 85
MWth Lurgi CFB gasifier was originally installed with downstream gas purification for removal
5-13

of HCl and ammonia. The feedstock is demolition wood. The raw gas is de-dusted in a cyclone
and cooled from 900 to 220 C in a syngas cooler, filtered in a baghouse and then water scrubbed
for the removal of ammonia and tar/oils. The cleaned gas is then fired in new burners installed
in the existing power plant unit. It was projected that in this mode ~ 70,000 mt/year of coal
would be saved corresponding to a CO reduction of about 170,000 mt/year. The annual wood
2
requirement is about 150,000 mt/year.
However, the initial operations (~ 1000 hours through 2001) showed that severe fouling of the
syngas cooler occurred in the region of 500°C and below, the bag house was frequently fouled
and the water scrubber produced liquor that had to be shipped off site to a waste dump. For these
reasons, plant modifications have been made to reduce the size of the SGC and eliminate the
baghouse filter and water scrubber so that the hot raw gas at ~470°C will pass through a new
cyclone and then be delivered directly to the boiler. In this respect, the project becomes more
similar to the Lahti concept except for the cyclone and SGC. The tars will then be combusted in
the boiler and it is anticipated that the ammonia in the raw gas will not increase NO emissions.
x
The operations should be considerably simplified with the new arrangement and the availability
and reliability should be much improved. These design modifications were made in the period
December 2001 to August 2002. Following a second commissioning period, this new scheme
was put into operation in November 2002 and in December 2002 had an availability of 70%.
When running at 60% load Lurgi’s process guarantees were achieved except for nitrogen usage.
Sand is fed continuously to the gasifier. There is a small amount of bottom ash (10-20% with
some sand) but there is mostly fly ash (80-90%). The fly ash can be added to asphalt and
perhaps cement, but the plant does have to pay to get rid of it. The fly ash from the main coal
plant is of higher quality and is sold to cement companies. The SGC has rappers and they
generally have worked well.
The plant continued to experience problems in 2003 with CO leakage and a blockage of the fly
ash silo with old commissioning material. However, the more serious problem of severe wear, in
fact actual disappearance, of the new cyclone lining after only ~1000 hours of operation.
Apparently the gas attacked the binder used between refractory tiles. They have now changed to
a phosphate binder. After the cyclone repairs, the plant restarted in June 2003.
Austrian Energy (formerly subsidiary of Babcock Borsig Power (BBP))
BIOCOCOMB, Austria
The “Biofuel preparation for Co-Combustion " (BIOCOCOMB) project in Austria at Daurkraft’s
Zeltweg 137 MWe power plant features a 10 MWth CFB gasifier supplied by Austrian Energy.
The biomass and wastes (plastics, sewage sludge) substitute for ~3% of the coal input. The
biomass is not dried and is typically 50-55% moisture and is only partially gasified so that a
mixture of fuel gas and char fines is fired in the adjacent boiler. There is no gas clean up but the
fuel gas is used as a reburn fuel in the boiler to reduce NO emissions. There is no significant
x
effect on efficiency but there is about a 15% reduction in the ammonia usage in the NO control
x
unit of the boiler. The air to the gasifier is preheated to 270° C by the boiler air pre-heater.
5-14

Biomass and Waste Gasification
When the biomass feed is 55% moisture the gas fired in the boiler has a very low heating value
of ~2.9 MJ/Nm3 on a dry basis (as fired the gas stream is ~33% water).
Electrabel and ENEL were also participants in the BIOCOCOMB project and there was support
from the EU. It was largely through Electrabel’s participation in this project that led them to
undertake the larger project at Ruien in Belgium.
Co-Gasification of Biomass in IGCC Plants and Coal Gasifiers
Small amounts of biomass and wastes can probably be added to the feed in coal gasifiers without
compromising the operations and quality of the slag. However, some biomass ashes are high in
alkalis such as Na and K and at some percentage usage could increase fouling and could
negatively impact the slag leaching properties.
ELCOGAS
In 2001, the ELCOGAS IGCC plant conducted some tests with MBM (Meat and Bone Meal-
from the animals slaughtered in the Mad Cow and Foot and Mouth disease epidemics). Tests
were conducted at 1% and 4.5% of total feed. At 1% there was no perceptible effect on
operations. During the 4.5% test there was some drop off in fouling attributable to the limestone
content of the MBM and some increase in the chloride content in the wet scrubber. There was
no effect on the slag discharge and no change in hazardous emissions.
Tampa Electric
In December 2001, the Tampa Electric IGCC conducted a short run adding ~1% of eucalyptus
wood chips to the gasifier feed for a 24 hour test. No adverse (or any other effects!) on
operations were noted. They may conduct some future test runs of up to 5% biomass.
NUON at Buggenum
(See Chapter 4, Specific Plant Reports, for more information)
The Netherlands, as part of the European Community is a signer of the Kyoto protocol. In
partial response to this, the Netherlands has undertaken a coal covenant with coal users. This
requires the Buggenum plant to make a CO emission reduction of 200,000 mt/year which is
2
equivalent to 28-50 MWe derived from biomass. NUON has stated that if the plant cannot run
biomass as covenanted then the plant would not run at all on syngas and would only run as a
NGCC. Their initial target was 30 wt% of co-gasification by the end of 2004 and 50 wt% by the
end of 2006. The 50 MWe is equivalent to ~ 300,000 mt/year of biomass. NUON has applied
for a new permit and accordingly will have to increase their attention to the measurement of
heavy metals. Sewage sludge has significant mercury content. Based on test measurements
made in August 2002, Nuon believes that over 60% of the mercury reports to the fly ash (where
leaching tests show it to be immobilized) and > 30% to the sulfur so that in total about 98% of
the mercury is captured. Nuon has estimated that the use of 100,000 mt/year of sewage sludge
5-15

solids at the Buggenum IGCC plant would result in only 1.2 kg/year of mercury emissions.
Whereas if this amount of sewage sludge solids was fed to a PC plant or a cement plant the
mercury emissions would be 12-30 kg/year and 36-60 kg/year respectively. The ability to
control mercury may be a unique niche advantage for the Buggenum IGCC plant.
The biomass feed has been mostly wood dust and dried sewage sludge. The use of this much
biomass increases the nitrogen usage, dilutes the syngas and increases the syngas CO content
2
somewhat adversely affecting the Sulfinol AGR and Claus plant operation. The wood is milled
to ~1 mm at a central location and sieved out wood dust added from the wood board industry
then transported by ship to Buggenum where it is stored in silos and injected into the outlet of
the coal mill (Coal is milled to 90%< 100 microns). It is a closed system apart from the single
transfer from the ship. Sewage sludge, when dried to a similar level can be fed below the
classifier of the coal mills. A gasification test was conducted at a pilot plant in Freiberg,
Germany on a 20/80 blend of dried litter/coal. The blend flowed very well and the test was
judged to be very successful.
A test run adding 4-5% sewage sludge solids to the coal feed was conducted in April 2003 at
Buggenum. This showed some increased fouling of the heat exchanger surfaces as judged by the
increased outlet temperatures from the various sections and an increased outlet SGC outlet
temperature from 250-252°C to 265-268°C. Inspection of the deposits showed enrichment in
Iron species rich in sulfur and phosphorus. However, there was no perceptible rise in the
pressure drop across the fly ash filter. One possible solution under consideration is to inject
water to the suction of the recycle gas compressor in order to increase quench gas cooling
capacity. Other possible solutions are blending and SGC cleaning modifications.
The lessons learned from the biomass test program at Buggenum from 2001-2004 have been well
summarized in a paper by M. Kanaar at a workshop held in conjunction with the 6th European
Gasification Conference in Brighton, UK in May 2004. The plant exhibited good fuel flexibility
with the main restrictions being the feed system (nitrogen supply), the syngas CO content
2
(affects AGR performance), permitted emissions and by-product quality.
The Buggenum gasifier can now handle up to 30wt% biomass. Nuon receives a 53 euro/MW-hr
credit for biomass-derived power; however, the Dutch peak power price can exceed 100
euro/MW-hr during the winter, and then it is better to maximize output by running only on coal
(with 30% biomass the plant output falls from 250 to 215 MW).
SVZ at Schwarze Pumpe
See also Chapter 4, SVZ GCC/Methanol Plant at Schwarze Pumpe, Germany, for an account of
the several years of waste co-gasification experience in the various gasifiers at the SVZ plant
(Fixed bed dry ash Lurgi, MPG for liquids, GSP/Noell for lignites, solid and liquid wastes and
the new BGL unit).
5-16

Biomass and Waste Gasification
Rheinbraun HT Winkler
In the mid/late 1990’s, a program of co-gasification tests was funded through EU THERMIE.
Probably the most important of these was a series of three 3-day tests on the 140 MWth 10 barg
pressure HT Winkler at Berrenrath near Cologne with a mix of RDF pellets and lignite. The
process operated as normal even up to 50% RDF of the mass input. A 20 mt/day HT Winkler
gasifier at 1.5 bar pressure has been built and has been in operation since 1999 in Niihama, Japan
using Municipal Solid Waste (MSW) feedstock. The technology was licensed to Sumitomo
Heavy Industries (SHI) who built the facility. The bottom ash leaving the gasifier is converted to
slag in a “Short” kiln using part of the fuel gas. The raw fuel gas is cooled in a syngas cooler.
An account of this work was given at the 2000 Gasification Technologies Conference.
5-17

6
GASIFICATION OF PETROLEUM AND TAR SANDS
RESIDUALS
Background
Over 75% of the World’s known petroleum reserves are heavy oils. The average refinery crude
slate is becoming heavier and higher sulfur content. Accordingly, the hydrogen usage per barrel
of oil processed is also increasing. In addition, emission regulations have become more stringent
and the permissible sulfur contents of fuel oils (particularly residual fuel oil) have been reduced
to generally below 1%. This combination of circumstances has meant that the market for
residual fuel oil (e.g., No. 6 fuel oil) has shrunk considerably and many refineries are faced with
having to add conversion and hydrotreating units to satisfy the demand for cleaner lighter fuels.
With natural gas prices rising worldwide (currently $6 -10 /MBtu in the US), the production of
hydrogen for the refineries by Steam Methane Reforming (SMR) is becoming very expensive.
This has led to several IGCC projects now in operation adjacent to refineries and many other
projects in various stages of construction and development. The gasification of petroleum
residuals can supply the refinery’s needs for power, steam and hydrogen. It can also serve as a
source of synthesis gas for ammonia, methanol, acetic acid, oxo- alcohols, and Fischer-Tropsch
liquid transportation fuels.
Heavy oil gasification has been practiced commercially since the 1950’s. GE/Texaco and Shell
both offer heavy oil gasification technology and each have licensed over 100 units worldwide.
Most of the applications prior to 1990 were to supply synthesis gas for chemical manufacture or
hydrogen to refineries. The IGCC application only appeared in the 1990’s. Both the GE/Texaco
and Shell heavy oil gasifiers are based on a single fuel injector, down flow, oxygen blown,
refractory lined, entrained flow reactors. In fact, both technologies have a common ancestor and
there was a contentious litigation battle that was resolved in the 1950’s. Texaco licenses the
technology either as quench units or with heat recovery from the syngas. For many years, Shell
had an arrangement with Lurgi for the marketing of its technology. The Shell/Lurgi gasifiers
were usually licensed with the heat recovery steam generators. However, in 1998, Shell
terminated its licensing arrangement with Lurgi and is now licensing the technology itself with
Krupp Uhde. Lurgi is now offering for license a very similar technology called Multi Purpose
Gasification (MPG). This is based on a gasifier design that has been operated for many years at
the former town gas plant at Schwarze-Pumpe, Germany (now SVZ). The heat recovery units
for GE/Texaco, Shell and Lurgi gasifiers are available from several suppliers. They are upflow
fire tube designs with helical tubes. Currently Texaco offers larger unit gasifier sizes than Shell.
In Europe and Asia, most of the refinery IGCC projects are based on various refinery heavy oil
streams (e.g., vacuum resid, deasphalter bottoms, visbreaker residue). US refineries generally
6-1

have more heavy oil conversion units, particularly cokers, and accordingly, petroleum coke is
more often considered as IGCC feedstock in the US.
Petroleum Coke Gasification
GE/Texaco
Ube Industries, Japan
The gasification of petroleum coke in GE/Texaco quench gasifiers has been practiced
commercially for 20 years at the four 500 mt/day units at Ube Ammonia in Japan. Petroleum
coke has a lower reactivity than most bituminous coals and the carbon conversion per pass is
lower, typically about 90%, so recycle of unconverted material may be required if another use
for it cannot be found e.g. in cement kilns.
El Dorado, Kansas
The first refinery IGCC project to start up was a small petroleum coke gasifier unit at the El
Dorado refinery in Kansas in 1996 (at which time the refinery was still owned by Texaco). The
El Dorado project featured a 150 mt/day petroleum coke quench gasifier (plus about 15 mt/day
of other low value refinery waste streams) operating at ~ 25 Bar, an MDEA system with the
cleaned syngas fed to a GE 6B gas turbine. About one third of the fuel value to the turbine was
syngas and the balance natural gas. The project was fully integrated and extraction air from the
gas turbine compressor was used as feed to the ASU (supplied by Praxair). The ASU supplied
oxygen to the gasifier and nitrogen to the gas turbine for power augmentation and NO control.
x
Products of this refinery unit were 35 MW net power, 81,650 kg/hour of steam, and pressurized
air, oxygen and nitrogen. This unit provided the total power needs of the refinery with no
normal export power, about 25% of the steam requirements, all of its nitrogen requirements and
supplementary oxygen and instrument air for its refinery operations. The unit used existing
wastewater treatment, sulfur recovery and flare systems in the refinery.
During the development of this project at El Dorado, Texaco asserted that the refinery sludges,
tank bottoms, and other secondary materials, which would be considered as hazardous waste if
disposed of, could be used as a feedstock at the refinery in the Texaco gasification system under
existing regulations without the necessity of a hazardous waste permit for the gasifier. Both the
Kansas Department of Health and Environment (KDHE) and the US Environmental Protection
Agency (EPA) agreed with Texaco’s conclusion. This ruling made it possible for various
refinery low-value and negative-value secondary materials to be part of the feedstock mix for the
gasifier.
In addition, the EPA also concurred that the syngas produced from these secondary materials can
be utilized as a clean fuel in a gas turbine without the requirement of a hazardous waste permit
for the turbine.
6-2

Gasification of Petroleum and Tar Sands Residuals
After some initial problems with coal feeding and slag lock hopper plugging, the plant operated
satisfactorily. Texaco reported values of equivalent availability of syngas from the gasifier
facility as 89% in 1999, 87% in 2000 and 91% in 2001. In 2002, the El Dorado refinery changed
ownership and the plant is no longer in operation.
Valero (formerly Motiva) IGCC in Delaware
A 2000 mt/day petroleum coke (fluid coke) gasification facility (equivalent to 250 MW IGCC)
has been built at the Motiva refinery in Delaware City. Motiva was originally a joint venture of
Texaco, Shell and Saudi Refining, however when Texaco was acquired by Chevron, Texaco had
to then divest itself of some ownership in refining and the Motiva plant became a Shell/Saudi
Refining joint venture. The refinery was acquired by Premcor in early 2004 and sold to Valero,
the current owner, in 2005. This plant repowers an existing cogeneration unit that produces
steam and power for the refinery and some export power. Some older petroleum coke fired
boilers and a Wellman Lord FGD unit were shut down when the IGCC plant was built. There
are two trains of quench gasification and two GE 6FA gas turbines. The gas turbines can each
produce 90 MW on syngas and 75 MW on diesel. The steam production is 136,200 kg/hr
(300,000 #/hr) of 83 barg (1200 psig) steam from each gas turbine HRSG and 68,100 kg/hr
(150,000 #/hr) of 12 barg (175 psig) steam per gasifier train. There is a single ASU of 2268
mt/day (2500 tons/day) capacity producing 99% purity oxygen (with oxygen, nitrogen and
Argon byproduct sales). The ASU main air compressor motor is 60,000 HP. A single MDEA
unit produces a syngas of < 350 ppmv sulfur compounds. The relatively high sulfur content is in
part due to the absence of a COS hydrolysis reactor. The Claus unit has a Tail Gas Treating Unit
(TGTU). Nitrogen dilution of the syngas is used for NO control from the gas turbine (at
x
<9ppmv?). The EPC contract with Parsons was a lump sum turnkey (LSTK) with liquidated
damages. The plant initially started up in 2000.
The gasification is conducted at 69 barg (1000 psig) and the quenched, scrubbed syngas is
reduced in pressure to 23 barg (330 psig) before entering the MDEA absorber at 38°C (100°F).
The gasifier initially experienced slag accumulation problems in the lock hopper system.
Unfortunately, no slag crusher was provided at the base of the slag discharge from the gasifier.
The refinery also had a set back in 2001 due to an unrelated fire and explosion at another
location in the refinery. Plant modifications, including addition of a slag crusher, were made in
May –July 2002 and the plant returned to service.
The GUA visited the plant on November 1, 2003 and at that time they were still having various
problems and had only achieved a few hundred hours of run time. Many of the problems were
common to those experienced with other Texaco solid fed units (i.e. injector tip failures, high
pressure syngas piping corrosion/erosion, black water valve wear). The search for a better flux
was ongoing and the nuclear level detectors and gage glasses in the slag level detection were
plugging up. The vacuum flash drum wear plate had experienced aggressive wearing of the
ceramic tiles. The CO content of the syngas was ~30% higher than design and there was
2
therefore a need to reduce the CO in the acid gas to the Claus plant. The plan was to change
2
absorber tower internals and to add cooling of intermediate MDEA streams.
They had experienced some problems with syngas transfers on the gas turbines. The 6 FA gas
turbines were equipped with larger diameter combustor cans than those used on other FA
6-3

machines (all FA’s use basically the same combustor cans but more of them as you go from the 6
to the larger 7 and 9 frame size). The purpose of the larger diameter was NO reduction. There
x
had not been many hours of syngas operation when the GUA visited however they were
producing markedly lower NO on diesel than design and there were optimistic expectations that
x
the NO with syngas would be proven to be <9ppmv at 15% oxygen.
x
Eastman was retained to provide assistance to the operating team and the plant operations have
reportedly made quite a bit of progress since 2002, with availability near 95%. A large project is
underway to further improve reliability and increase the capacity of the gasification facilities
with a goal of 98% availability. Connectiv (a merger of Delmarva Power, Atlantic City Electric
and PEPCO) had the operations contract for many years (many of the operators were formerly
employed at the former pet coke boiler plant). However, starting in October 2006, Valero has
assumed operational responsibility for the entire IGCC plant.
Coffeyville Resources (formerly Farmland Industries)
A gasification plant was built for Farmland Industries at Coffeyville, Kansas. The project
obtained the two Texaco gasifiers (nominally 1000 mt/day each) from the Cool Water project
and they are fed with petroleum coke to produce syngas and then hydrogen for ammonia
manufacture. The plant includes shift reactors and CO removal via a multi-column Selexol
2
system. Some of the captured CO is used to form Urea through reaction with some of the
2
produced ammonia. The plant was started up in July 2000. A report on their operations to date
was presented at the October 2003 Gasification Technologies Conference and the GUA visited
the plant on October 17, 2003. Subsequently, the plant was sold in Spring 2004 to Coffeyville
Resources LLC and continues in operation. With natural gas prices at 6-10$/MBtu many natural
gas based ammonia plants in the US have ceased operations. The low value petroleum coke
feedstock offers Coffeyville Resources a real market advantage.
Leucadia IGCC at CITGO’s Lake Charles, LA Refinery
A major IGCC project was planned by a Partnership of TECO (Tampa Electric Company) Power
Services (TPS) and Texaco Power & Generation (TP&G) adjacent to the large CITGO (owned
by Petroleos Venezuelos -PetroVen) refinery at Lake Charles, Louisiana. This project was to
supply hydrogen, steam and power to the refinery and power for sale to the grid. Texaco and
TPS subsequently withdrew from the project. However, since late 2004 an investment group,
Leucadia, doubtless motivated by the current oil and gas prices, is attempting to continue
development of this project with CITGO.
Tampa Electric
As already mentioned, the Tampa IGCC plant in Polk County, Florida is currently running on a
mixture of 55% petroleum coke/45% coal in order to reduce feedstock prices (See Chapter 4, for
the recent operating experience.)
6-4

Gasification of Petroleum and Tar Sands Residuals
Sinopec
The recently announced GE projects in China with Sinopec as listed in Table 1-1 are to be based
on gasification of a mixture of coal and petroleum coke.
BP Carson Refinery
A major project is planned by BP and Edison Mission at the Carson refinery near Los Angeles.
About 5-6000 stpd of pet coke is to be fed to three GE Q gasifiers. The syngas is to be shifted
and the CO2 removed (5-6 million tons/year) for use in EOR. The power block is based on two
GE 7 FB gas turbines and some hydrogen and steam supplied to the refinery. More details of this
project are given in Chapter 1.
Shell/Prenflo
The ELCOGAS IGCC runs on a 50/50 mixture of petroleum coke and the local Spanish coal.
(See Section 3. for the recent operating experience) Shell and Krupp Uhde have merged their
solid fuel gasification technology as the Shell process. The Prenflo process is no longer offered
as a separate technology license. For petroleum coke feedstocks, Shell offers their dry coal fed
upflow entrained gasifier as used at the Nuon IGCC plant at Buggenum rather than the down
flow heavy oil gasifiers used at Pernis.
Shell is currently developing a project with the Indian Oil Co. at Paradip, India in which 2300
mt/day of petroleum coke would be gasified to produce 5 million Nm3/day of syngas for power
and hydrogen production.
COP E-Gas
Since the conclusion of the DOE demonstration program in January 2000, the Wabash plant has
been running on 100% petroleum coke (See Chapter 4 for the recent operating experience.) Due
to Global Energy’s business problems, the plant did not operate from February 2004 and only
restarted in June 2005. The gasification plant is now owned and operated by SG Solutions, a
50/50 venture of Global and Wabash Valley Power.
The former Global E Gas team, now owned by ConocoPhillips, has conducted preliminary
engineering studies for petroleum coke gasification at several potential US and Canadian sites.
ConocoPhillips has screened sixteen of its refinery, production and cogeneration facilities for E-
Gas applications. Design studies have been conducted for 11 potential internal and equity
projects. Refueling of existing combined cycle cogeneration plants is being investigated at
several locations.
The Mesaba IGCC project is to be designed for handling 100% of three feedstocks – PRB,
Illinois #6 coal and petroleum coke.
6-5

The Global Energy, Lima, Ohio 550 MW IGCC project originally planned to use the BGL
technology with coal and MSW, however, it is now to be based on E-Gas and Petroleum coke.
Work on the coal/pet coke receiving area is proceeding.
Heavy Oil Gasification
IGCC projects based on heavy oil gasification have been and are being developed in several
countries in Europe and the Asia Pacific region. The projects in Italy were some of the largest
and earliest to enter operation. Italy currently imports about 16% of its power and most of its
internal power was historically generated from the combustion of residual oil (typically about
3% sulfur) produced by the domestic refiners. The refiners were faced with major new capital
investments to conform with the new regulations requiring a reduction in the fuel oil sulfur
content to less than 1%. At the same time, ENEL faced a gap in generation capacity. The Italian
government therefore passed legislation to provide price incentives for independent power
production from indigenous fuels. In response to this situation, several heavy oil IGCC projects
have been developed adjacent to refineries.
GE/Texaco
Three Texaco based Heavy Oil IGCC plants in Italy entered full commercial service in 2001.
These were ISAB Spa 510 MW in Sicily, Api Energia 260 MW in Falconara and SARLUX 550
MW in Sardinia.
In 2000, three additional heavy oil gasification plants were commissioned. These units were at
Exxon Chemical at Baytown, Texas supplying syngas to Air Products, a150 MW IGCC at
Exxon’s Singapore refinery and a unit at a Celanese plant in Singapore supplying hydrogen and
CO for chemicals.
In 2003, a single train 342 MW IGCC entered commercial service at the NPRC (Nippon
Petroleum Refining Company) Negishi refinery in Yokohama, Japan.
ISAB Energy
Erg Petroli (80% Erg 20% Agip Petroli) and Edison Mission Energy formed a 51/49 venture
ISAB Energy S.r.l to develop, own, and operate the 510 MW IGCC power plant in Priolo, Sicily
adjacent to the ISAB refinery. Asphalt is fed to two high-pressure (67 barg) quench gasifiers.
Oxygen is supplied to the gasifiers from an Air Liquide ASU outside the plant battery limits.
The ASU is operated without airside or nitrogen integration. Medium pressure steam is
generated from the quenched gas by further gas cooling. Sulfur compounds are removed by
COS hydrolysis and an MDEA system. Elemental sulfur is recovered in a Claus plant. The
clean syngas is sent to a gas expander (from 67 to 23 barg) that recovers 10 MW (total) of power
and then to a saturator before it is fed to two Siemens V 94.2K gas turbines. Each gas turbine
has its own HRSG and steam turbine. To accommodate the non-integration with the ASU, the V
94.2K gas turbine compressors have two additional compressor stages over the standard design
provided for the IGCC plant at Buggenum. The heating value of the fuel gas, adjusted by
dilution with 35-40 mol% water vapor is 1700-2100 kcal/kg, or about double the value at
6-6

Gasification of Petroleum and Tar Sands Residuals
Buggenum. Siemens carried out extensive tests at atmospheric and pressurized conditions to
check out the Lean Stability limit and NO emissions. Siemens forecasts <25 ppmv at 15%
x
oxygen however the HRSG’s are also equipped with SCR units for stringent NO control when
x
firing fuel oils as auxiliary fuel. Snam Progetti and Foster Wheeler conducted the lump sum
turnkey EPC contract. The total cost was estimated at $1.2 billion.
Initial start-up was in July 1999. Some early problems occurred with the gas expander and it had
to be shipped back to the manufacturer for repair modifications. Hot spots were observed in both
gasifiers but were solved by injector and refractory changes. There has been abrasion and formic
acid corrosion in the gray water/soot water section. The piping was changed to “Super Duplex”
and valves and fittings replaced with higher grade materials in late 2001, and pH neutralization
capability has been added to the circuit.
Some ongoing problems were described during the GUA visit in May 2001. These included
Nickel deposits that caused fouling and overheating of the gas turbine burners. These were
probably caused by the decomposition of Nickel Carbonyl Ni(CO)4, a gaseous compound stable
up to 250-300°C (482-572°F). Some slipstream tests have been conducted on the syngas before
MDEA for absorption of the carbonyls on activated carbon and consideration is being given to
the addition of a full stream unit. There has also been a poor interface in the soot water/naphtha
decanters that has led to poor transfer of soot to the naphtha. There have also been vanadium
slag accumulations in the bottom cone of the gasifier refractory and at the bottom of the quench
chamber. (See also the notes on SARLUX operation below).
The COS hydrolysis unit operates at 220°C (427°F) and reduces the COS to 4-5 ppmv. The
MDEA (Dow Gas Spec) operates at 40°C (103°F) and high pressure of 55 bar (800 psig) and
reduces the syngas HS content to 6-7 ppmv for a total sulfur <15 ppmv. An ion exchange
2
reclaimer (supplied by NPR (USA)) is used to remove the heat stable salts (HSS) and other
accumulated impurities.
The first guarantee test (MPS - Minimum Performance Standard) was completed in April 2000.
It consisted of ten days of sustained load including 48 hours at 95% of guaranteed Net Power
Output (NPO). After this test the plant was taken over by the owner. A second test (GPS –
Guaranteed Performance Standard) was carried out in October 2000 consisting of 30 days at 93%
of guaranteed NPO including 48 hours at design production and consumptions. The 30-day test
was successfully conducted and the design rating of 510 MW was achieved during the 48 hours.
Asphalt and oxygen consumptions were 8-12% below guaranteed figures. The emissions were
below the maximum allowed and the NO lower than expected.
x
In 2004, the Edison Mission equity ownership in this project was acquired by the International
Power Group.
Api Energia
The Api group and ABB Sae Sadelmi formed a joint venture, Api Energia, in 1992 to conduct a
nominal 250 MW IGCC project located within the Api refinery in Falconara Marittima, Marche
region. In 1997, Texaco joined the project as an equity member (24%) of the Api Energia
consortium.
6-7

The plant feedstock is a visbreaker vacuum resid. The gasification section has two high-
pressure (65 barg) Texaco quench gasifiers and scrubbers (each rated at 56% of plant
throughput), one carbon extraction unit and one gray water treatment section. The gas cooling
and cleaning section is a single train with medium and low-pressure steam recovery, COS
hydrolysis, Selexol acid gas removal and a gas expander. There are two Claus units followed by
a tail gas treatment section. The ASU is licensed from Praxair and it produces oxygen for
gasification and the Claus units and nitrogen for gas turbine NO control by syngas dilution.
x
Under normal operating conditions 58 mt/hour of heavy residues are gasified to produce 130
mt/hour of clean syngas that is then mixed with 127 mt/hour of nitrogen to produce low NO
x
emissions when combusted in the gas turbine.
The gas turbine is an ABB 13E2 equipped with an annular combustion chamber. The axial
compressor has 22 stages and a pressure ratio of 16.7/1 and one row of variable inlet guide
vanes. The gas turbine exhausts into a HRSG that provides superheated steam to a condensing
steam turbine with export of medium and low-pressure steam to the refinery. ABB Lummus
Crest executed the lump sum EPC contract. The project has a power purchase agreement with
ENEL. Total project cost was quoted at an estimated $772 million.
The initial start up was in February 2000. There were an excessive number of equipment
component failures in the early months of operation. Gaskets needed upgrading to spiral wound
types with a center ring. Many valves had to be replaced with upgraded materials. Pump seals
failed and there were weld failures on the feed injectors.
The Selexol unit operates at a pressure of 51 barg (740 psig) and 38°C (100°F). It produces a
clean syngas of < 30 ppmv total sulfur (HS + COS) and an acid gas > 40 mol % HS. Formic
2 2
acid from the COS hydrolysis reactor is absorbed by the Selexol solvent and revaporized in the
reboiler where it was found to condense in the uninsulated instrument nozzles and produce shell
corrosion. The resolution has been to insulate the instrument nozzles and to practice pH control
and formate removal.
The MPS test was conducted in February 2001 and the 30-day Reliability Test was completed in
April 2001.
The following information on operations was obtained during a GUA plant visit in April 2006:
The GT is rated at 185 MW at 15°C and produces 200 MW in winter. The steam turbine
nominal output is 99.4 MW. The plant gross output is 288 MW in summer and 300 MW in
winter. The Auxiliary load is 42-43 MW for the gasification plant (including ASU) plus 2 MW
for the power block. Net output is therefore 243 MW summer and 255 MW in winter. The GT
capacity is the station’s output limit. 130 to 155 KPPH of steam is sent to the refinery.
Organization and Staffing
Alstom has a 12-year O&M contract for the power block plus the electrical distribution and
seawater (cooling water) supply. There are 24 shift workers (4/shift x 6-8hr shifts) plus 19
management, technical, & support staff. The shift teams consist of one console operator, two
field operators and one supervisor.
6-8

Gasification of Petroleum and Tar Sands Residuals
API operates and maintains the process plant and ASU. There are 53 shift workers (9/shift x 6-
8hr shifts) plus 15 management, technical, & support staff. The plant manager has 2 assistants, 4
process engineers, 2 contract administrators and 3 I&C people, among others.
Both Alstom and API work out of the same control room.
Both the refinery and Alstom O&M organizations are certified for ISO 14001 (environment) and
OHSAS 18001 (safety). Alstom O&M also has ISO 9001 certification (quality process).

The combined cycle availability has been very high since initial operation despite the fact that
the back-up fuel is oil. The machine has several features to eliminate distillate startup and
transfer problems encountered “elsewhere”.
Gasification system reported availability was excellent in 2004 and 2005, and API expects good
results in 2006 as well. After all, it is oil gasification, they have 2 gasifiers, and the reported data
is probably availability of syngas, not equivalent availability.
SCR
The HRSG includes an SCR which is required to be in use when operating on syngas.
Ammonium sulfate/bisulfate deposits in HRSG from SCR are a problem (though apparently
availability has not been significantly impacted). Plugging and fouling occur in last 3 HRSG
banks. The residual S in the syngas fuel is 10-15 ppmv in winter and 40-45 ppmv in summer.
Some further notes on the SCR experience are:
• Ammonia Consumption and Slip: 0.2 to 0.3 mg/Nm3 actual ammonia slip for 30% NO
x
reduction. Expectations were <1 mg/Nm3 NH slip for 30% reduction and 2 mg/Nm3 for 50%
3
reduction. Ammonia consumption is currently 50 liter/hr (unclear whether or how much this
is diluted with HO).
2
• HRSG Cleaning: They have to clean with dry ice blasting every 6000 hours (at CT
inspection intervals) or they see vibrations in the HRSG as well as higher gas-side pressure
drop. They found dry ice is not as effective as water washing but works well enough and is
much easier (water washing produces acidic wastewater which is difficult to collect and
dispose of properly and also causes corrosion). They are not set up for convenient HRSG
cleaning. They are trying acoustic blasting (6 horns per bank for last 3 HRSG banks, 3 times
per day for 2½ minutes – 20 seconds per horn). The recent GT upgrade will extend time
between inspections to 8000 hrs. The technique looks promising: the on-line PM monitor
showed increased particulate following blasts and the dP monitoring was encouraging. The
deposits are light and fluffy and “easily removed”.
• ISAB’s SCR Experience: API staff said ISAB has also been required to use their SCR
during syngas operation and are having much more difficulty. ISAB has to clean every 6
months (also have to replace CT burners at that interval due to metal carbonyl fouling).
6-10

Gasification of Petroleum and Tar Sands Residuals
During a previous GUA visit ISAB reported that they operated at 10 to 15 ppmv H S + COS
2
in their fuel gas with COS hydrolysis + MDEA.
SARLUX
SARLUX, a joint venture between SARAS (55%) and ENRON, was the original owner of a 550
MW IGCC plant in the SARAS refinery located in Sarroch, Sardinia. In 2005, the ownership
became 100% SARAS. The complex uses three lower pressure (38 barg) Texaco quench
gasifiers with a single soot recovery and water treatment section. There are two trains of gas
cooling and cleaning comprising low pressure steam recovery, hot water recovery for syngas
moisturization, COS hydrolysis, Selexol acid gas removal - but no gas expander. There are two
oxygen blown Claus units. The ASU, owned by Air Liquide, comprises two units and is outside
the plant limits and supplies oxygen “over-the-fence” without any integration with the gas
turbines. SARLUX claims that the capital cost of this lower pressure design is 3-5% lower than
the higher-pressure design with a gas expander. They also claim that the cost of oxygen from the
conventional pressure ASU without integration is 10% lower than it would have been with a
higher pressure ASU. A portion of the clean syngas is sent to hydrogen removal and recovery
units comprising a membrane section and pressure swing absorption (PSA) section to produce
pure hydrogen available to the refinery battery limits. The syngas and waste gas coming from
the hydrogen recovery unit and the non-permeate gas from the Hydrogen removal unit are fed to
the saturator for syngas moisturization. The moisturized syngas with a low heating value of
1700-1850 kcal/kg is sent at about 20 barg and 200°C to the three GE combined cycle units each
comprising a 9E gas turbine, HRSG and steam turbine. The project produces 550 MW of power,
100 mt/hr of MP steam, 85 mt/hr of LP steam and 40,000 Nm3/hr (1.4 Million SCFH) of
hydrogen. The plant was built by Raggruppamento Temporaneo d’Imprese (Consortium formed
by Snam Progetti, General Electric and Turbotecnica) under a lump sum turnkey construction
contract.
The initial start-up was in April 2000, however, the failure of a retaining wall at the refinery
caused a 3-month delay. The gasifiers were restarted in August 2000. One of the main problems
in initial operation was soot carry over from the syngas scrubber. This has subsequently much
improved by changes to operating practices. At gasifier shutdown, vanadium slags (VO) were
2 5
found obstructing the bottom gasifier throat area. Texaco has suggested that if soot production is
too low the soot is insufficient to move the vanadium out of the gasifier so that VO (melting
2 3
point ~2000°C) can be formed in the reducing atmosphere. Then during the change-out of the
process burner to the preheat burner with air, the VO is oxidized to V O (melting point
2 3 2 5
~800°C) which flows down to the throat and resolidifies in the area of the quench ring. A de-
slagging procedure has been developed using the preheat burner and controlling the air draft and
gasifier temperature.
The Selexol unit operates at 28 barg (407 psig) and 27°C (81°F) and produces syngas of 17
ppmv HS + COS and an acid gas of >60mol % H S. Some severe damage to the membranes in
2 2
the hydrogen removal system was found when minor amounts of Selexol solvent were entrained
with the clean syngas. New separators with high efficiency coalescing elements have now
replaced the conventional demisters.
6-11

The plant passed the MPS test in January 2001 and the 30-day reliability test in February 2001.
The GPS test was successfully completed in July 2001 during which the net power output was
560 MW with all the consumption figures below the guaranteed levels (feed consumption 7%
lower than guaranteed).
