Each of these projects experienced start-up delays and extended commissioning periods of 10-12
months. The refinery owners each sought recourse for the financial losses incurred in these
delays and litigation was initiated but the issue was soon settled out of court.
ISAB has been in commercial operation since April 2000 and Sarlux since January 2001. A
paper by G.Collodi of Snam Progetti at the 2003 Gasification Technologies Conference
described the operating history of these two plants over the first three years of commercial
operation during which the capacity factor, including the use of gas oil back up fuel, increased
from 75% to 90-95%.
Exxon Mobil Chemical Baytown, Texas
The Exxon Mobil Syngas project is located within the Exxon Mobil Chemical plant in Baytown,
Texas. There are two quench gasifiers fed with 1100 mt/d (1213 st/d) of deasphalter bottoms
from the adjacent Exxon Mobil refinery. The syngas is sold over the fence to Air Products who
supplies various syngas products throughout the region. The plant started up in April 2000 and
the guarantee tests were completed in July 2000.
6-12

Gasification of Petroleum and Tar Sands Residuals
Singapore Syngas Proprietary Ltd
The Singapore Syngas Proprietary project was originally a joint venture between Texaco and
Messer Industries. The project gasifies 570 mt/d (630 st/d) of heavy oil from the adjacent Caltex
refinery and sells syngas to chemical producers, including Celanese, in the area. The plant began
selling CO in 2000 and entered full commercial operation in July 2001. After Chevron’s
takeover of Texaco, Chevron acquired full ownership of the plant.
Exxon Mobil Singapore
These two gasifiers are fed with steam cracker (for ethylene production) tar and produce syngas
for an on-site cogeneration plant comprising two GE 6 FA combined cycle units. A smaller
amount of the syngas is sold for chemicals production. The GE 6 FA units have the unique
aspect of being designed to fire 40 different fuel combinations. The units were started up in
April and June 2001 and the guarantee test was completed in July 2001.
NPRC Negishi IGCC
This project at NPRC’s Negishi refinery in Japan has some important new IGCC features. It has
a single train GE quench gasifier at 69 barg (1000 psig) feeding a single train MHI (Mitsubishi
Heavy Industries) 701 F combined cycle. The gross output is 431 MW and the net output 342
MW. The 25.5 m3 (900 ft3) gasifier is fed with 2000 mt/d of asphalt. This gasifier has the
largest syngas output of any single gasifier which is sufficient to fully load the 701 F gas turbine.
This gas turbine is rated at 416.4 MW in combined cycle on natural gas with design natural gas
fuel demand of 2548 GJ/hour (2408 Million Btu/hour) LHV basis. In contrast the natural gas
fuel demand for a 7 FB on the same basis is 1760 GJ/hour. The ASU, supplied by Air Liquide,
produces 2300 mt/d of oxygen as well as nitrogen for the gas turbine and oxygen for the Claus
plant. The gas clean up train includes gas scrubbing, low temperature heat recovery, COS/HCN
hydrolysis, ammonia scrubbing and Shell ADIP acid gas removal. The sulfur recovery is a
single Lurgi OxyClaus (130 mt/d) equipped with a SCOT Tail Gas Treating Unit (TGTU). Total
sulfur recovery is 99.8%. The HRSG is equipped with SCR.
The EPC contractor was JGC (Japan Gasoline Company). The IGCC plant was completed on
schedule and it entered commercial operation in June 2003. The plant is operated at 100% net
power during daytime on weekdays and at 75% net power on weekends and holidays and during
night time on weekdays. The daily ramp of 80 MW is accomplished in 30 minutes morning and
night without flaring. The combined cycle flue gas emission requirements are < 3.4 ppmv SO , <
x
2.6 ppmv NO and particulate <1.4mg/Nm3 (all at 16% oxygen dry basis). The measured
x
emissions have all met these requirements. From the point of view of air emissions, this is
therefore the cleanest IGCC plant in the world.
For the first year of operation through June 2004, the IGCC availability was 69.3% with planned
outage of 14.7%, unplanned outage of 16% and forced outage rate of 18.8%. The maximum
recorded continuous run was 94 days ending with a scheduled feed injector replacement. The
main contributor to the 16% unplanned outage was due to a cracked 3rd stage impeller on the
ASU main air compressor (9.9%).
6-13

Other GE/Texaco Heavy Oil Gasification Projects
Several other GE/Texaco gasifiers operating on heavy oil/pitch have also been supplied to China
and India in the past decade. However, most of these are for ammonia, chemicals and fuel gas -
not for IGCC.
Shell
For many years Shell had a licensing arrangement with Lurgi for the licensing, design and supply
of Shell’s heavy oil gasification technology. However, this agreement was terminated at the end
of 1997 and Shell is now once again responsible for its own licensing of this technology through
an arrangement with Krupp Uhde. In turn, Lurgi now has a licensing arrangement with SVZ and
is offering the Multi – Purpose Gasification (MPG) technology. MPG is based on many years of
experience with a unit at SVZ in Schwarze-Pumpe, Germany. It is very similar to the Texaco
and Shell heavy oil gasification technologies.
Pernis
In late 1997, Shell started up a major gasification based expansion at their Pernis refinery in the
Netherlands. The plant supplies hydrogen to the refinery for a hydrocracker as well as steam and
power (sometimes called the tri-generation mode). A total of 1650 mt/day of vacuum flashed
visbreaker residues are gasified in three gasification trains (pressure 65 barg) each equipped with
a waste heat recovery heat exchanger (WHE).
The recovery of this sensible heat in the raw gas is an integral feature of the Shell process. Each
WHE contains spirally wound gas tubes in parallel in an upflow arrangement for steam
generation. Raw gas is cooled to 400°C (752°F) and high-pressure 92 barg (1334 psig) steam is
recovered in the syngas coolers. The gas then passes through an economizer into the soot
quench. Heavy metals (V, Ni) are recovered from the Soot Ash Removal Unit (SARU). The
sulfur species (mainly HS) are removed from the gas in a Rectisol unit.
2
Oxygen (about 1600 mt/day) is supplied to the complex by pipeline from an ASU owned and
operated by Air Products. Two of the three gasification trains are dedicated to the production of
255 mt/day of hydrogen primarily for the hydrocracker. In these two trains, the clean gas from
the Rectisol unit is sent to high temperature and low temperature shift reactors and subsequent
CO removal (Rectisol again) and methanation units. The syngas produced in excess of that
2
required for hydrogen production is saturated and fed to the two combined cycle units each
consisting of a GE 6B gas turbine (supplied by Thomassen), HRSG (NEM-Steinmuller design)
and steam turbine. The power plant produces 127 MW and 400 mt/hr of steam for the refinery.
Each 6B gas turbine has a power output of 43 MW (ISO). The gas turbines can run on syngas,
LPG or natural gas and NO emissions are controlled by steam injection. The HRSG’s are single
x
pressure (82 barg (1190 psig) 525°C (977°F) supplementary-fired natural circulation units,
which produce steam and superheat steam from the gasifier. They have a maximum steam
capacity of 238 mt/hr of high-pressure steam including 124 mt/hr of steam imported from the
gasification section.
6-14

Gasification of Petroleum and Tar Sands Residuals
The gas turbine was started up on natural gas in April ’97 and the combined cycle in June’97.
There were some initial problems with the cleanliness of the oxygen supply line and several
control valves had to be modified. Startup of the gasifiers was initiated in October’97 and there
were several months of instrumentation and controls problems. However, these were sorted out
and all three gasification trains were running during the GUA tour of the plant in May 1998. The
first firing of the gas turbine on syngas was accomplished smoothly apart from some minor
control problems in the gas manifold. Shell indicates that the high degree of reliability needed
for their refinery is being fully met. In 2001, the planned outages were 4%, unplanned outages
2%, outages due to external causes 8% and a yearly production 83% of the full design capacity.
The total time for hydrogen production outage was 0.5%.
The gasifiers are usually run at 98-99% carbon conversion so that the solids passing through the
syngas coolers are mostly carbon and fouling is therefore minimal. Shell claims that with their
blast-atomizing three stream co-annular burner residues from all crude oils known to Shell can
be gasified satisfactorily without serious fouling of the syngas coolers. They have also presented
an economic analysis to show that the high-pressure steam from the WHE is so much more
valuable than the medium-low pressure steam obtained with a quench unit that the WHE can be
paid for in one year. The soot formed is removed with the process condensate as soot slurry and
routed to a Soot Ash Recovery Unit (SARU- a process developed by Shell and Lurgi in 1991).
The soot and ash are filtered out and the filter cake subjected to controlled oxidation (<550°C) in
two parallel multiple hearth furnaces. The ash components (mostly V &Ni) are recovered as
oxides for use in the metallurgical industries.
Agip Petroli - Sannazzaro
A fourth IGCC project in Italy is under construction by Snamprogetti adjacent to the ENI R&M
(formerly Agip Petroli) refinery at Sannazzaro in northern Italy. Start-up is planned for late
2005. The plant is designed with essentially the same flowsheet as at Pernis except that there are
just two Shell gasifiers each fed with 600 mt/day of visbreaker vacuum tar residue. 95% purity
oxygen is to be supplied from an ASU outside the battery limits then preheated with superheated
(~50°C superheat) steam from the SGC and mixed with superheated steam prior to introduction
to the gasifiers. Gasification pressure is 62 barg (900 psig). The gasifiers produce ~325 kg/hour
of soot (~0.65% of the feed). Soot removal, slurry processing, filtration and combustion follow
the Pernis design. The syngas leaving the soot scrubber is preheated for COS hydrolysis then
cooled to 38°C and fed to the first stage of a two stage Amine AGR process (Dow Gas-Spec).
Condensates from the syngas cooling and filtrate from the soot filter are to be routed to a sour
water stripper. The syngas leaving the primary AGR absorber containing < 10 ppmv H S is to be
2
sent to an activated carbon unit for removal of metal carbonyls. The rich amine from the base of
the primary absorber is regenerated in the primary stripper and the acid gas is fed to the absorber
of the enrichment section where the HS is reabsorbed. The CO stream from the enrichment
2 2
column can be incinerated or sold. The rich amine from the enrichment section goes to a low-
pressure stripper where a stream of > 70 mol % HS is recovered and sent to the existing refinery
2
Claus unit. The 250 MW power block is based on a Siemens V 94.2K gas turbine (as used on
the ISAB project in Sicily) located some distance (~1km?) from the refinery on the Sannazzaro
industrial site. Some of the hydrogen contained in the syngas is to be recovered by means of a
membrane unit. The hydrogen rich permeate is to be routed to an existing PSA unit and
concentrated up to 99.9% purity for use in the refinery at a design rate of 38,000 Nm3/hour.
6-15

The EPC contract with Snamprogetti was finalized in July 2003. Construction started in October
2003 and commissioning started in November 2005. The test run was completed in June 2006.
The initial operations and causes of outages were detailed in a paper given at the 2006
Gasification Technologies Conference. Some of the initial issues were: a leak in the HP BFW
air cooler a leak in the newly developed superheater modules of the WHE, soot deposition in the
slurry tank and in the transport of solids. However, generally the start up was smooth and
reliable and the promised process performance has been achieved.
LOTOS Gdansk, Poland
Shell heavy oil gasifiers have been selected for the IGCC project at the LOTOS refinery in
Gdansk, Poland. There will be three gasifiers, each with asphalt feed of 550 mt/d. Start-up is
planned for 2008. The basic plan is very similar to that used at Shell’s Pernis refinery in the
Netherlands). Two thirds of the syngas is to be used to supply hydrogen to a large hydrocracker
unit.
For more details see the paper given at the 2004 Gasification Technologies Conference.
Fujian Refinery Ethylene Project
The Fujian Refinery Ethylene Project (FREP) is a joint venture of the Fujian Petrochemical
Company (50%), Exxon-Mobil China Petrochemical (25%) and Aramco Overseas (25%). The
overall project includes a refinery expansion from 80,000 to 240,000 barrels/day using sour
Arabian crude, an 800,000 mt/y ethylene steam cracker, polyethylene and polypropylene units,
700,000 mt/y para-xylene and a Shell gasification plant. There will be three Shell gasifiers each
fed with 1200 mt/d Shell of asphalt and each equipped with syngas coolers generating high
pressure steam at 120 barg (1740 psig). Most of the syngas will go to a sour shift and then
Rectisol and PSA for hydrogen production. Some syngas will go to a separate Rectisol unit and
then on to a gas turbine based cogeneration unit.
For more details see the Shell paper given at the 2005 Gasification Technologies Conference.
Other Shell based Heavy Oil Gasifiers
Shell gasification is used in many other heavy oil gasification projects around the world for
ammonia, hydrogen and chemical synthesis. Four heavy oil gasifiers have been licensed in
China for ammonia and chemical synthesis.
Tar Sands Applications
Gasification is also finding applications in the recovery of bitumen from the Canadian tar sands
and in the upgrading of the recovered bitumen. The recovery and refining of tar sands are very
energy intensive processes that historically have used large amounts of natural gas to provide the
needed steam, power and hydrogen. However, gasification of the less valuable and heavier tar
6-16

Gasification of Petroleum and Tar Sands Residuals
sands processing residues such as petroleum coke or asphaltenes can also provide the required
steam, power and hydrogen.
The OPTI/Nexen project uses Steam Assisted Gravity Drainage to recover the bitumen and
gasification of the asphaltene fraction to provide steam, power and hydrogen.
The Canadian Syncrude and Suncor projects (and others) mine the tar sands and separate the
bitumen from the sand by hot water treatment. Both companies have major expansion plans.
COP is one of the participating companies in the Syncrude partnership.
There are several bitumen upgrading schemes that are used including coking (both fluid and
delayed), deasphalting and residue hydrocracking. The coke, asphaltenes and hydrocracker
residues can all be used as gasification feedstocks.
OPTI Canada Inc. / Nexen Petroleum Inc.
This project in Long Lake, Alberta, Canada involves the extraction of 70,000 barrels/day of
bitumen from the Athabasca tar sands by means of steam assisted gravity drainage (SAGD).
OPTI’s “OrCrude” partial upgrading process will be used to convert the bitumen into sour
synthetic crude. This will then be hydrocracked to produce a premium quality synthetic crude
oil. The process also yields an asphaltene product that will be the feedstock for 4 x 1033 mt/day
gasifiers that will produce hydrogen for the hydrocracker, syngas for the cogeneration plant and
steam and syngas to drive the SAGD extraction process. The basic design is to be similar to
Pernis but about double the size. A 2007 start-up is planned. The project will enable all the fuel
and hydrogen needs at Long Lake to be satisfied without the importation of increasingly
expensive and sought after natural gas.
OPTI selected Air Liquide to supply a 3900mt/day ASU. This is to be a single train and will be
the largest single train ASU in the world. It is to be supplied by 2 x 50% Main Air Compressors
(MAC) and Booster Air Compressors (BAC) each on a single shaft to be used in parallel. There
are no oxygen compressors but instead liquid oxygen from the low pressure column is pumped to
system pressure and vaporized against the incoming high pressure air in the main heat
exchanger. The four gasifiers each have dedicated syngas coolers. The cooled syngas is to be
treated in a single Selexol unit and after treatment is mostly sent to a Linde PSA unit for
recovery of hydrogen. Two GE 7 EA gas turbines are used in a cogeneration system. They are
to be fed with pressurized syngas, compressed PSA tail gas or both.
For more details see the paper given at the 2006 Gasification Technologies Conference.
NorthWest Upgrading
In this project, the bitumen is subjected to atmospheric and vacuum distillation and the vacuum
residue is hydrocracked. The hydrocracker residue (~1200 mt/d) is to be fed to a Lurgi MPG
gasification unit. Lurgi is actually responsible for the supply of the entire Hydrogen plant
including MPG, CO Shift, gas cooling, AGR, Oxy Claus and Hydrogen purification. Lurgi will
also supply a Metal Ash Recovery System (MARS) and waste water treatment unit.
6-17

Northern Lights Upgrader Project
The Northern Lights Upgrader project is a joint venture of SynEnCo Energy and the SinoCanada
Petroleum Corp. partnership. SinoCanada is a subsidiary of Sinopec. The joint venture has
signed a license agreement with GE for the supply of a gasification plant for the production of
Hydrogen. The size has not yet been announced.
Suncor
Suncor is planning a major expansion of their tar sands recovery and processing. The
gasification of petroleum coke from the bitumen upgrading is incorporated in these plans.
Technology selection has not yet been made.
Orimulsion
Orimulsion is an emulsion of about 70% heavy oil from the Orinoco basin in Venezuela with an
additive and about 29% water. It is marketed by Bitor as a competitor for coal. Texaco
conducted several gasification tests on Orimulsion at the Montebello, California pilot plant and
commercial IGCC projects have been investigated. The original formulation, Orimulsion –100,
had a high content of Magnesium (~500 ppmw), which was added to make the fuel suitable for
combustion in conventional boilers. A new formulation, Orimulsion – 400, has a lower
Magnesium content (~5 ppmw), and is specifically suitable for gasification and remains liquid at
ambient conditions.
Orimulsion is a suitable feedstock for both the GE/Texaco and Shell gasification processes.
GE/Texaco is prepared to offer the quench version for Orimulsion but is more cautious when the
question is asked about the heat recovery gas coolers and has often said that it feels that the gas
coolers are limited to feedstocks of <500 ppmw metals. Shell is more confident in this respect
and states “Residues from all crude oils known to Shell can be gasified in the SGP process
without resulting in serious fouling of the WHE coils”.
At the Noordwijk Gasification Conference in April 2002, Shell, Krupp Uhde and PDVSA put
forward a proposed design for a large Orimulsion IGCC based on four Shell gasifiers with
syngas coolers and four GE 7 FA gas turbines with a net power output of 1163 MW at a 47.1%
LHV efficiency. However, with the current political unrest in Venezuela, there has been no
recent news of this proposed project.
6-18

7
PRODUCTION OF HYDROGEN, CHEMICALS,
SUBSTITUTE NATURAL GAS (SNG) AND FISCHER-
TROPSCH TRANSPORTATION LIQUID FUELS
Introduction
The high price of natural gas has made syngas from coal or petroleum residuals more attractive
for the production of Hydrogen, for chemical synthesis (Ammonia, Methanol, DiMethyl Ether
(DME)) and potentially for the production of Substitute Natural Gas (SNG).
The even higher price of crude oil has prompted serious consideration of gasification of low
value fossil resources to provide syngas for the synthesis of liquid transportation fuels via the
Fischer-Tropsch (F-T) technology (remote Gas-to-Liquids GTL, and Coal-to-Liquids CTL).
Hydrogen
Several Coal gasification (GE/Texaco, Shell) plants in China make Hydrogen for Ammonia
production. See Tables 1-1 and 1-6. In these plants the syngas is shifted and the CO removed,
2
often using the Rectisol (Methanol) process. In some of these plants the removed CO is reacted
2
with ammonia to form Urea: CO + 2NH = CO(NH) + HO.
2 3 2 2 2
Although coal gasification for ammonia production is widely used in China it is not currently
used in the US. Because of the high natural gas prices over the past two years about half of the
US domestic ammonia plants are shut down. The Coffeyville plant in Kansas (See Chapter 6)
uses petroleum coke gasification in 2x 1000 st/d GE/Texaco gasifiers and thereby has a real
advantage in the current ammonia marketplace.
Rentech has acquired the Royster Clark 1000 st/d ammonia plant in Illinois and plans to retrofit
it for use with syngas from E-Gas coal gasification. Co-production of 2000 bbl/d of Fischer
Tropsch diesel is also planned at this site. The State of Illinois has provided some funding to this
project for a FEED that is now being conducted by Kiewit/Worley Parsons.
Agrium, a fertilizer company operating in the Cook Inlet area of Alaska is about to embark on a
FEED study for the retrofit of their ammonia/urea complex based on the gasification of
nominally 5000 stpd of the local Usebelli low rank coal. The initial retrofit study was based on
the Shell technology.
7-1

With natural gas prices rising worldwide (currently $6-10 /MBtu in the US) the production of
hydrogen for the refineries by the traditional Steam Methane Reforming (SMR) process is
becoming very expensive. This has led to several IGCC projects now in operation adjacent to
refineries and many other projects in various stages of construction and development. The
gasification of petroleum residuals can supply the refinery’s needs for power, steam and
hydrogen. Several of these projects are described more fully in Chapter 6.
On July 18, 2006 BP and GE announced a plan to jointly develop and deploy hydrogen power
projects and collaborate on power, carbon capture, and sequestration technologies.
BP and GE will jointly participate in the previously announced hydrogen power projects in
Peterhead, Scotland (with Scottish & Southern Energy, based on NG reforming) and at Carson in
California (with Edison Mission Energy, based on pet coke gasification).
These two projects provide GE with orders for hydrogen-firing gas turbines in both 60 Hz and 50
Hz markets. BP and GE plan 10–15 hydrogen power plants over the next decade (subject to
appropriate regulatory and fiscal regimes being in place).
The IGCC project at the Carson refinery, located about 15 miles south of Los Angeles, is to be
based on the gasification of 5-6000 stpd of petroleum coke using three GE Quench gasifiers, sour
shift and CO removal and firing of Hydrogen in two 7 FB gas turbine combined cycles to
2
produce ~ 500 Net MW. The CO (5-6 Million stpy) is to be used for Enhanced Oil Recovery
2
(EOR) in oil fields owned by Occidental Petroleum. The project also plans to supply 50-60
Million SCFD of hydrogen to the refinery and 0-500 KPPH of HP steam. An application for tax
credits under EPAct 2005 has been filed.
The National Academy of Engineering (NAE) report on the Hydrogen Economy, completed in
2003, shows Hydrogen from coal competes with Hydrogen from natural gas whenever there is a
natural gas to coal fuel differential of 3$/MBtu or greater. The costs of Hydrogen production in
large centralized plants of 1200000 kg/ day are shown in Table 7-1 as reported in the NAE study.

7-2

Production of Hydrogen, Chemicals, Substitute Natural GAS (SNG) and Fischer-Tropsch Transportation Liquid
Fuels
Similar costs have been confirmed by separate EPRI and Texaco IGCC co-production studies.
The main conclusions of EPRI report 1004537 December 2003 on hydrogen co-production from
IGCC plants were:
• If just 1% of the produced syngas from a 500 MW IGCC plant is used to produce hydrogen
this is enough hydrogen to provide the fuel needs of 10,000 vehicles. This corresponds to ~4
filling stations each with 12 pumps and 350 fill-ups per day (total 1400 fill-ups/day). (N.B. 1
kg of Hydrogen is the same LHV as I US gallon of gasoline (113,700 Btus).
• Hydrogen produced in this manner could well be the lowest cost hydrogen because of the
economies of scale provided by the large gasifiers in an IGCC plant.
• Results showed Hydrogen costs of 0.8-0.9 $/kg when using 10-1% of the Hydrogen (60,000-
6000 kg/d) from a 500 MW Texaco Quench IGCC plant with CO Capture. This compares
2
with 1.0$/kg from a SMR with Natural Gas at $4.5/MBtu.
• Using syngas from a Texaco Quench IGCC without CO capture the costs of hydrogen co-
2
production were 0.95-1.2 $/K
The study by K.Sturm et al (Texaco) at the Gasification Technologies Conference 2003 came to
the following similar conclusions:
• A wide range of quantities of Hydrogen can be co-produced economically from an IGCC
plant
• Significant flexibility exists in configuring an IGCC for a given amount of Hydrogen co-
production
• Hydrogen co-production can increase the rate of return for an IGCC plant
• Hydrogen co-production can be added incrementally
• The cost of Hydrogen from coal via IGCC depends on the amount of co-production. At 100
MMSCFD from a 500 MW IGCC plant it competes with the cost of hydrogen via natural gas
SMR at natural gas prices of 4$/MBtu.
The DOE FutureGen initiative announced in early 2003 is a planned IGCC project that will
incorporate CO capture and sequestration and hydrogen co-production. An industrial
2
consortium, the FutureGen Alliance, led by AEP and the Southern Company, was formed in
September 2005 to manage the project. In response to a RFP potential sites have been selected in
Illinois and Texas. The final site selection is due for the 3rd quarter of 2007. Following an
evaluation of many potential configurations by EPRI staff four were down-selected for more
detailed evaluation by Worley Parsons as the engineering contractor for the FutureGen Alliance.
Any IGCC project that incorporates for CO capture, will produce a syngas with probably > 90%
2
hydrogen so that co-production of hydrogen would be a logical source of revenue from such
projects.
7-3

Methanol
The Eastman Chemical plant in Kingsport, TN has been making methanol and its derivatives,
methyl acetate and acetic anhydride, via Coal Gasification since 1983. (See Chapter 3)
There are many other GE, Shell and Siemens coal gasification plants in China for the production
of Methanol as listed in Tables 1-1, 1-6 and 2-3.
DiMethylEther (DME)
There are three Coal to Dimethyl Ether (DME) projects in China. One plant of 10,000 mtpy is in
operation. A 110,000 mtpy unit is in construction in Sichuan and a 210,000 mtpy unit is in
design for a location in Ningxia. The EPC for these three projects is Toyo engineering of Japan.
DME has several potential uses. Its use in domestic cooking and perhaps heating is being
encouraged as a means of backing out the direct use of coal in homes and thereby avoiding the
very adverse environmental effects of the low elevation pollution associated with domestic coal
use.
Two Siemens 500 MWth coal gasifiers are also being constructed for a DME plant in Ningxia,
China. See Table 2-3.
Syngas
The IPP company, Calpine, owns many NGCC plants in the refinery belt of Texas-Louisiana that
operate in cogeneration mode supplying steam and power to the nearby refineries. At the 2005
Gasification Technologies Conference Calpine announced that they are studying the refueling of
these NGCC plants with syngas from the gasification of petroleum coke with the possible co-
production of hydrogen for the refineries. ConocoPhillips is also studying the potential refueling
of NGCC cogeneration units at several of their refineries with syngas from the gasification of
petroleum coke.
Substitute Natural Gas (SNG)
SNG is synthetically produced methane (CH )—typically intended for pipeline distribution with
4
or in lieu of pipeline natural gas. SNG is produced from synthesis gas by the following
“methanation” reactions—typically over a nickel catalyst:
CO + 3H = CH + HO + heat (exothermic)
2 4 2
CO + 4H = CH + 2HO + heat (exothermic)
2 2 4 2
Methanation process temperatures generally lie in the range of 190-450ºC (375-840ºF). The
process can be readily operated at coal- or coke-derived syngas delivery pressures; e.g., about 20
barg pressure and higher. Nickel catalysts are poisoned by sulfur. Therefore, the syngas must be
purified to <0.1 ppmv sulfur. Nickel catalysts can also be irreversibly fouled by carbon, unless
the H/CO ratio is maintained above about 2.85 and/or excess steam is added. Actually, as
2
indicated by the stoichiometry of the above two methanation reactions, an H/CO ratio somewhat
2
7-4

Production of Hydrogen, Chemicals, Substitute Natural GAS (SNG) and Fischer-Tropsch Transportation Liquid
Fuels
higher than 3.0 is required, depending on the CO content of the syngas. Residual H, CO, N,
2 2 2 2
and argon in the product SNG reduce its HHV. The HHV of 100% CH is 1,012 Btu/scf (9,519
4
kcal/Nm3).
Methanation Process Options
Most commercial methanation plants employ two or more adiabatic equilibrium-controlled
fixed-bed reactors in series—with temperature control by recycling a portion of the product gas
and heat removal by cooling the exit gas. Steam can be generated in the exit gas cooling process.
Fixed-bed reactors with water-cooled tubes are also an option. Fluidized-bed reactors have also
been proposed, such as a Sasol Synthol CFB-type of reactor.
Two process options are commercially available—conventional and combined shift/methanation.
However, the latter has not yet been applied commercially. The two options are described
briefly below.
Conventional Methanation
The conventional approach involves methanation of purified syngas after it has passed through a
CO shift process to adjust the H /CO ratio and an AGR process to remove most of the CO .
2 2
CO + 3H = CH + HO + heat (exothermic)
2 4 2
CO + 4H = CH + 2H O + heat (exothermic)
2 2 4 2
After methanation, the SNG is dehydrated and compressed for pipeline transport. The Lurgi
process of this type is used in the Dakota Gasification SNG plant.
Combined Shift/Methanation
Methanation can be combined with the shift process to utilize the water that is formed in
methanation for the water-gas shift reaction:
CO +HO = H + CO + heat (exothermic)
2 2 2
This accomplishes a substantial portion of the methanation along with the CO shift process.
Consumption of the water as it is formed in the methanation reactions drives the methanation
reaction closer to completion. Nickel catalysts are also employed in the combined
shift/methanation process and steam must still be added for stoichiometry (balancing competing
reactions), temperature moderation, and/or to prevent carbon formation. Temperature is
controlled by using multiple reactor stages with cooling of the gas exiting each stage.
A final “conventional” methanation stage is employed after CO is removed in an AGR process.
2
An advantage of the combined shift/ methanation process is that the volume of gas that must be
treated for the final CO removal is less than half of that of a shifted gas (in the conventional
2
process). Also, the partial pressure of the CO from the combined shift/methanation is high, thus
2
7-5

facilitating its removal in the AGR process. Developers of such processes in the 1970s and
1980s included Conoco, R. M. Parsons, United Catalyst, ICI, and UOP.
Coal to SNG Projects
The process adds capital and reduces the energy yield since the reaction is exothermic, although
it does produce steam. It probably adds 20-25% to the cost of syngas. However a co-production
scheme would reduce the cost.
The Steelhead project under development in Southern Illinois by Madison Power and ArcLight
Capital plans a 600 MW IGCC plant with co-production of 2.7 Million Nm3 (95 Million SCFD)
of SNG (Substitute Natural Gas) using the COP E-Gas gasification technology and Siemens
SGT6-5000F gas turbines. The State of Illinois has provided funds for a FEED to be conducted
by the Fluor/Siemens EPC team. However there has been no news of this project for the past
year.
Another coal to SNG plant is planned in Illinois by Power Holdings. This would be a 150 million
scfd SNG plant in Jefferson County, IL, using GE technology to gasify ~10,000 stpd of Illinois
coal. The estimated capital cost was reportedly $1.3 billion but it is not clear what this number
represents. Ameren and Nicor have signed 20-year agreements to take ~65% of the plant output.
ERORA has plans for two IGCC plants co-producing 630 MW and SNG based on the use of GE
RQ gasifiers. One site is Taylorville, IL and the second is Cash Creek, KY. The operating
concept is to swing the syngas from one gasification train after AGR sulfur removal between
power and SNG. The SNG would be produced via sweet shift, CO capture, methanation, SNG
2
drying and compression. This would enable full operation of both gasifiers while increasing the
load following capability of the power plant and co-producing a storable fuel, SNG.
In late October 2006 Governor Mitch Daniels announced a plan to build a $1.5 billion coal
gasification plant in Indiana that would be the first in the country to make pipeline quality natural
gas from eastern coal. The plant is scheduled to be online in 2011. Indiana Gasification LLC, the
project developer and owner, has several team members, including E3 Gasification LLC, headed
by William Rosenberg, former assistant administrator of the U.S. Environmental Protection
Agency (EPA) and the Federal Energy Administration, and current senior fellow at the Harvard
University Kennedy School of Government; and Johnston Development Company, LLC, headed
by Bennett Johnston, former United States Senator from Louisiana. According to the letter of
intent for 30-year supply contracts signed by the utilities this week, about two-thirds of the SNG
produced by the new plant would be purchased by Indiana's three largest gas utilities, Vectren
Corporation, NIPSCO (Northern Indiana Public Service Company), and Citizens Gas to help
meet residential and commercial gas demand. NIPSCO would purchase the remainder of the gas
to fuel electric generation for its service territory to meet seasonal demands. The output is
planned at 40 billion SCF/ year or ~130,000 Million SCFD (slightly lower than the production at
Dakota Gasification in Beulah, ND. The project plans to use three GE gasifiers probably of the
Radiant quench design since the plant will have considerable power and steam requirements and
may export as much as 180 MW of power. With 20% project owner equity and the rest from
7-6

Production of Hydrogen, Chemicals, Substitute Natural GAS (SNG) and Fischer-Tropsch Transportation Liquid
Fuels
Federal loan guarantee (plan to file to back 1.2B$ in debt) the projected price is ~6$/MBtu for
SNG.
The Dakota Gasification plant in Beulah, ND has 12 Mark 4 Lurgi dry ash gasifiers and has been
in operation for the last 20 years making 150 Million SCFD of pipeline quality SNG and a whole
variety of other products including ammonia, CO for Enhanced Oil Recovery, phenols, rare
2
gases etc.. The capital for this was provided originally to a large degree by DOE. It was acquired
by Basin Electric Cooperative for a modest price so that the capital component of the SNG cost
is very minor. A few years ago the O&M costs including the lignite fuel cost were reported at ~
3$/ Thousand SCF.
Coal to SNG Economics
COP gave a presentation at the 2006 Gasification Technologies conference on the economics of
coal to SNG. The typical E-Gas flow scheme was used through the particulate filter followed by
low temperature heat recovery, chloride scrubbing, sour shift, Mercury removal, Rectisol AGR,
Methanation and SNG drying and compression. They showed about 65% of the coal’s carbon in
the byproduct CO stream. The study used 3 x 50% gasifiers and high purity 99.5% oxygen was
2
used to keep the nitrogen and argon content of the SNG to a minimum. Data was presented for
petroleum coke (Gulf Coast), Illinois #6 coal (mine mouth) and PRB (mine mouth). Some of the
results are shown below in Table 7-2.

The capital cost breakdown was estimated to be 16% ASU, 30% Gasification, 15% Gas

7-7

2

Fischer-Tropsch (F-T) Synthesis
The Fischer-Tropsch process was invented in Germany in the 1920’s and was used commercially
in Germany during the 1930’s and 1940’s to produce liquid transportation fuels from coal.
Production of F-T liquids from coal gasification has been commercially practiced
for the past 50 years in S. Africa by Sasol. China is now working with Sasol on two large CTL
projects in Shaanxi and Ningxia.
F-T Technology Description
The Fischer-Tropsch (F-T) reaction, which requires a H/CO ratio of ~2/1, yields a wide
2
spectrum of hydrocarbons and oxygenated compounds via the following reactions e.g.:
Paraffins (2n+1)H + nCO = CH + nHO
2 n 2n+2 2
Olefins 2nH + nCO = CH + nH O
2 n 2n 2
Alcohols 2nH + nCO = CH OH + (n-1)HO
2 n 2n 2
The F-T reaction is generally considered as a polymerization process and the product distribution
is described by the Anderson-Schultz-Flory (ASF) equation usually written as logWn/n = nlog +
(1- )2/ where Wn is the mass fraction of a particular product, n is the carbon number and is the
probability of chain growth (i.e. the ratio of the chain propagation rate constant to the chain
propagation plus the termination rate constants).
The product distribution is affected by temperature and catalyst. The effect of temperature on the
primary product distribution is shown in Table 7-3.
7-8

Production of Hydrogen, Chemicals, Substitute Natural GAS (SNG) and Fischer-Tropsch Transportation Liquid
Fuels

The Sasol reactors have mostly been high temperature because of their higher yield of gasoline
which was an important source of supply for their domestic market. The lower temperature
products in the gasoline boiling range are mostly straight chain paraffins which make very good
ethylene cracker plant feedstocks but their low octane ratings are of little gasoline blending
value. However many of the GTL projects currently in construction are aimed at the high quality
diesel market and have selected the lower temperature reactors. In these latter plants the wax
portion of the product is separated and hydrocracked to make more product in the diesel or jet
fuel boiling range.
Several organizations have developed their own versions of F-T technology employing different
types of reactors and catalyst formulations. The major F-T technologies available for license are
shown in Table 7-4.

Sasol’s F-T Reactor Developments
Sasol started reactor development from relatively small capacity reactors of 69 mt/d (500 bbl/d)
for tubular fixed-bed (TFB) so-called “Arge” reactors and 897 mt/d (6,500 bbl/d) for the Synthol
CFB reactors. The TFB reactors are also called multi-tubular fixed-bed (MTFB) reactors.
7-9

Developments over the years have led to development of slurry phase and fluid bed reactors now
being offered with capacities of 1,380 to 2,760 mt/d (10,000 to 20,000 bbl/d).
The synthesis reactions are highly exothermic, and the reactor systems must have provisions to
remove this heat as shown in Figure 7-1.
The Arge TFB reactors operate at about 25.5 bar (370 psi) and 232°C (450°F) and the CFB
reactors operate at about 22 bar (320 psi) and about 329°C (625°F). The former is designated the
LTFT (low temperature Fischer-Tropsch) process and the latter the HTFT (high temperature
Fischer-Tropsch) process.
Sasol has developed advanced fluidized bed reactors for both the LTFT process and the HTFT
process as shown in Figure 7-2.
Arge TFB Reactor Synthol CFB Reactor
Source: Sasol
Figure 7-1
Initial Sasol Reactors
7-10

Production of Hydrogen, Chemicals, Substitute Natural GAS (SNG) and Fischer-Tropsch Transportation Liquid
Fuels
LTFT HTFT
SSPD REACTOR SYNTHOL SAS REACTOR
Source: Sasol
Figure 7-2
Advanced Sasol LTFT and HTFT Reactors
In both designs the heat of reaction is removed by generating steam in heat exchange coils in the
reactor beds. For the HTFT process Sasol has developed the Sasol Advanced Synthol (SAS)
fluid bed reactor. In the Sasol Slurry Phase Reactor (SSPD) the reaction temperature is
sufficiently high that all products are removed. Condensed liquids form a slurry with the catalyst
and are removed separately from the unreacted gases and vapor products.
Observations on F-T Economics
Because of the need to shift the syngas for F-T synthesis, about half of the coal’s carbon is
removed as CO . Sequestration of this CO would entail only the marginal cost of drying,
2 2
compression, pipeline transportation and any associated monitoring and verification.
Coal to F-T is only ~50% efficient. Coal at $1.00/MBtu contributes ~$11/bbl (Sasol estimate), so
coal cost is a significant part of F-T cost and therefore mine mouth locations are preferred for F-
T plants.
Shell & Sasol believe that for CTL, you need the economies of scale at 60,000 bpd or more (3
million mtpy). The Shell/Shenhua announced project and the two other projects being studied in
China with Sasol are of this size.
7-11

Economies of scale decrease because of the multi-train nature of larger plants. An exponent of
0.85 -0.9 has been suggested (compare to the typical single train scaling exponent of 0.6–0.7).
Composition of F-T liquids is dependent on the specific F-T technology (catalyst Co or Fe,
temperature). Naphtha is less valuable and can be 20–30% of the production. The various
product slates obviously will be a major determinant of the economics. Generally F-T Diesel is
valued at 1.2-1.3 X crude oil in $/bbl. The naphtha is lower value and is probably about same
$/bbl as crude.
Several previous studies estimated F-T from remote gas to be viable at $20/bbl crude oil
equivalent and from coal gasification at $35-40/bbl crude (with $7/bbl premium for F-T Diesel
over crude) e.g. EPRI Report TR-1004066—“Assessment of Technical Innovations for the Co-
Production of Transportation Fuels and Electricity” (August 2001). However with the increases
in commodity and construction costs in recent years CTL in the US would probably require 50-
60$/bbl crude oil equivalent for it to be economical.
In a short document dated about January 2006 Sasol presented the following assessment of F-T
economics:
A nominal 80,000 bpd plant (without export power) would represent 84,200 actual bpd (5600
equivalent bpd LPG, 25,500 bpd naphtha, 53,100 bpd diesel) and require 8 -18 Million mtpy
of coal depending on quality. Sasol estimates the capital cost of 50,000 to 80,000 bpd CTL
plants as 60,000-80,000 $/ daily bbl. Capital cost of a 80,000 bpd plant is therefore estimated
at 5-6.5 Billion $. Coal at 1$/MBtu is ~11$/bbl and direct operating costs are ~10$/bbl.
A Sasol quote “The CTL process is capital intensive and requires some level of host
government support in the form of fiscal and other incentives to be viable and sustainable
even with crude oil prices in the 45-60$/bbl range.”
“American Energy Security” A Study by the Southern States Energy Board, July
2006
In July 2006 the Southern States Energy Board (an organization representing 17 states) issued a
report entitled “American Energy Security” that evaluated several technologies and policy
options that could markedly reduce the US dependency on oil and LNG importation from
unreliable sources. It examined Coal to Liquids (CTL), Oil Shale, EOR, Biomass and
Transportation Efficiency and Conservation.
Mitretek evaluated 16 CTL configurations for bituminous coal, sub-bituminous coal, lignite and
biomass at 10,000, 30,000, and 60,000 bpd of F-T liquids. Both Recycle and Once-through F-T
reactor configurations were evaluated. The Once-thru configurations have much greater power
export and should be considered as IGCC co-production plants. The viability threshold for CTL
was 35-55$/bbl of crude oil equivalent depending on plant size, coal rank and configuration. This
translates to finished diesel at 45.5-71.5$/bbl. Shale oil, EOR with CO and biomass were all
2
evaluated at similar costs to CTL. The results of the Mitretek cases are shown in Tables 7-5
(bituminous coal), 7-6 (lignite) and 7-7 (sub-bituminous coal)

In 2005-6 Scully Capital conducted a study on the potential costs of F-T technology for the US. This study was sponsored by several
organizations including the Department of energy (DOE), Department of Defense (DOD), EPA, EPRI, American Chemical Society
(ACS), and the Gasification Technologies Council (GTC). Scully used the Mitretek estimates from the SSEB report as a starting point
and added financing costs etc. The general conclusions were:
• Overall, the analysis suggests that FT fuel could be a competitive and assured source of transportation fuels.
• Scully used TCR case estimate for a 30,000 bpd plant from the SSEB report by Mitretek and added financing costs to give a
require investment in the range of $3.3 billion – $3.7 billion depending on type of coal and the use of carbon capture equipment.
• Crude-equivalent price (CE) for bituminous CTL without carbon capture is $52 – $56 and with carbon capture is $55-$59.
• Crude-equivalent price for lignite CTL without carbon capture is $54 - $58 and with carbon capture is $56-$61.
• Carbon capture appears to increase the price of FT fuel by $3.50 – $4.00 per barrel, or $2.69 – $3.08 in crude oil-equivalent price.
• Scully calculated the required diesel selling price and valued it as 1.3 x CE. They used only a low value of 30$/bbl for the Naphtha
product. If naphtha was valued as crude oil this would reduce the CE price ~7$/bbl from 56 to 49 $/bbl.
A second phase of the study has recently been initiated by the same sponsors looking at possible financial incentives for F-T
technology deployment and also at the co-production of Hydrogen, ammonia, Methanol and other chemicals from coal.
7-14

F-T Projects in Development
• Waste Management and Processors Inc. (WMPI). Gilberton, PA. A project selected by DOE
from responses to the1st round CCPI solicitation. It plans to use Shell gasification of
anthracite culm/coal to produce 5000 bpd F-T Diesel with Sasol technology. The State of
Pennsylvania has agreed to purchase a majority of the product. However there has been no
recent news in 2006. Will it happen?
• Rentech selected Worley Parsons (through Kiewit Energy) to conduct a FEED for a
polygeneration project in East Dubuque, Il. The project plans to use COP E-Gas technology
to produce 930 stpd ammonia (the site has an ammonia synthesis plant), 1800 bpd F-T using
the Rentech process 76 MW export power.
• Peabody and Rentech plan to develop coal-to-liquids (CTL) projects in the Midwest and
Montana at 10,000 and 30,000 bpd scale. Peabody is also evaluating the Black Mesa (AZ)
site for possible SNG production and New Mexico sites for both SNG and CTL
• Rentech and Baard plan a polygeneration plant in Ashtabula, OH. To produce 200 MW,
35,000 bpd F-T liquids, and 1500 stpd Ammonia.
• DKRW (Houston-based) plans a project at Medicine Bow, Wyoming to produce 11,000 bpd
F-T liquids initially growing to eventually 33,000 bpd F-T liquids. 350 MW. Arch Coal is a
partner and owns the coal. GE Gasification has been selected. DKRW and Arch have similar
plans for a project in Montana.
• Synfuel Inc. has announced a $5 billion gasification project to produce syngas, gasoline,
power, steam, and methanol from Louisiana lignite. GE is named for gasification and syngas
production, Haldor-Topsoe for methanol, and ExxonMobil for methanol to gasoline. North
American Coal and Sinopec are also mentioned.
• Shenhua is constructing a direct liquefaction project in Inner Mongolia. The first train is to
use 6000t/d coal to produce 1 million t/yr liquids and is due to startup in late 2007. Two Shell
gasifiers are under construction to supply the necessary Hydrogen
• Shell/Shenhua (50/50) announce CTL project to produce 3 million mtpy F-T liquids in
Ningxia, China (2 Million mtpy Diesel & 1Million mtpy Naphtha/LPG)
• Dow/Shenhua developing a Coal to Chemicals complex. Two routes to Olefins via F-T
(Naphtha cracking) or Methanol (MTO) are being examined.
• Three Coal to Dimethyl Ether (DME) projects 10,000 mtpy (operating), 110,000 mtpy (in
construction, Sichuan) and 210,000 mtpy (in design, Ningxia). EPC Toyo engineering, Japan.
DME use in cooking.
• Two Indirect Liquefaction Fischer–Tropsch (F-T) projects, each of ~ 3 Million mtpy liquids
are under study by Shenhua with Sasol in Ningxia & Shaanxi provinces
7-15

Large F-T projects are underway in Qatar and Nigeria with major companies (Shell, Chevron,
Sasol, Exxon) using syngas from Steam Methane Reforming (SMR) of the remote natural gas.
The Nigerian project with Chevron and Sasol is ~34,000 bpd of F-T liquids but the projects in
Qatar are huge, some of them up to 150,000 bpd of F-T liquids. The major companies involved
must have judged that the premium value of these liquids over time justifies the massive
investment.
7-16

8
ECONOMICS – IGCC WITHOUT AND WITH CO
2
CAPTURE
Construction Cost Increases 2004-6
Construction and commodity costs were relatively stable for the period 2000-2003. However the
rapid increases in crude oil and natural gas prices over the period from early 2004 through late
2005 also produced marked increases in several commodities and thereby the cost of
construction of power plants, refinery and chemical plants. These trends have been further
accentuated by the greatly increased worldwide demand for new construction of power plants,
refineries, chemical and industrial plants that have driven up costs. This worldwide construction
boom and the associated construction price increases have many causes including some
particular to North America:
• Massive addition of PC plants in China
• Many new refineries worldwide
• Major expansion of tar sands oil production in Alberta (Alberta labor cost x productivity
factor now 3–4x U.S. Gulf Coast)
• In response to Clean Air Interstate Rule(CAIR) ~50 GW of U.S. PC plants are adding FGD
and SCR
• Repair/replacement/rebuild work of the extensive damage to industry, commerce, oil and gas
rigs, and refineries in the US Gulf coast area as a result of Hurricanes Katrina and Rita
• Increase in all commodity prices driven by increased fuel prices and worldwide demand
• Ocean and other freight increases with increased fuel costs
Two commonly watched indices are the Chemical Engineering Plant Cost Index (CEPCI) and
the Marshall & Swift Equipment Cost Index. The changes in these indices over recent years are
shown in Figure 8.1. The CEPCI increased from 402 in mid 2003 to 468 Feb ’05. For mid 2000
to mid 2003 there was very little change at 394-402. From February to October 2005 the index
was also relatively flat. Examination of the components of the CEPCI show that the construction
labor component had been quite flat until October 2005 and that the increase was due to
equipment, steel, cement and other commodity cost increases. The Marshall & Swift Equipment
Cost Index increased from 1130 1st Q 2004 to 1241 in 1st Q 2005 and was1258 in 2ndQ 2005.
However from October 2005 the effect of hurricanes Katrina and Hugo was to produce acute
shortages of equipment, commodities and skilled labor. The overall result of which continue to
be felt as illustrated by the renewed upturn in these key indices from that date onwards.
8-1

Figure 8-1
Construction Cost Indices (Source Chemical Engineering Magazine October 2006)
As a consequence the capital cost estimates for all technologies are markedly higher than those
reported in the previous 2000-2005 annual Gasification Technology Status reports. There are
some compensating factors such as the use of larger more efficient gas turbines for the IGCC
reference plants and the qualification of Asian manufacturers for some key equipment that
should directionally reduce costs. These improvements, however, were more than offset by the
lower value of the US $ and the rise in ocean freight rates. The overwhelming effect is a marked
increase in capital costs driven by the worldwide demand for new construction and the greatly
improved economies of the Asian economies that have produced a billion or more new
consumers of commercial goods and services over the past two decades.
2006 IGCC Cost Estimates
It has been extremely difficult to obtain new and credible capital cost estimates in 2006. All costs
have been rapidly increasing and it is difficult to obtain credible estimates in such a rapidly
changing and high demand environment. All EPC firms are very busy and have declined to do
EPRI study work when they have so many specific client projects to pursue.
There have, however, been a number of studies completed in 2006 comparing IGCC, PC and
NGCC technologies with and without CO capture. The following studies are reviewed in this
2
section of Chapter 8:

Economics – IGCC Without and With CO2 Capture
• DOE NETL Reports (Review of May 2006 Draft)
• PSC Wisconsin DNR IGCC Draft Report
• EPA IGCC and PC (July 2006)
• Confidential ongoing studies with several companies
• PUC submissions and press announcements for proposed new plants
• EPRI for FutureGen Industrial Alliance evaluating multiple possible plant configurations
The range of estimates (for a given technology, plant size, coal, and location) available from the
listed sources is quite wide.. There is also some considerable uncertainty of what is represented
in the capital cost estimates in PUC submissions and press announcements. There are also some
uncertainties regarding 7 FB and 5000F gas turbine performance, particularly for hydrogen firing
on IGCC plants with CO2 capture.
The results of some of these studies are herein presented and discussed.
DOE NETL Draft Report 2006
In May 2006 Worley Parsons completed a draft report for DOE NETL entitled “Cost &
Performance Comparison of Fossil Energy Power Plants”. A number of external reviewers,
including EPRI staff were invited to comment on the draft report and a revised version is being
produced. In the meantime the results from the draft report have been presented by DOE NETL
staff in several public forums including the 2006 Gasification Technologies Conference.
IGCC, PC and NGCC designs were evaluated a) without capture and b) with Capture. The coal
used was Illinois #6 coal priced at $1.34/MBtu HHV basis. Natural Gas was priced at
7.46$/MBtu HHV.
Three IGCC technologies were evaluated - GE Radiant Quench, COP E-Gas Full Slurry Quench,
Shell Gas Recycle Quench. All designs were based on 2 x GE 7 FB GTs. The plants designed
with capture have additional coal gasification etc to fully load the GTs when firing Hydrogen.
Since no gas turbines were added for the capture cases there is a lower net output with capture.
In most of the presentations NETL showed results for IGCC as an average of the three
technologies.
Both PC sub critical (2400psig /1050F/1050F) and Supercritical (3500psig/1100F/1100F) PC
plant designs were evaluated. In contrast to the IGCC cases the PC designs with post combustion
amine (Fluor Econamine) scrubbing capture are much larger so that net output is same as the PC
designs without capture. This does give some advantage of scale to the PC designs however the
net MW of all coal cases with capture end up in the 500-560 MW range.
The NGCC cases without capture and with post combustion amine (Fluor Econamine) scrubbing
were also based on 2 x GE 7 FB GT’s.
8-3

The location was Green-field, Mid West, 0 ft. elevation, and design ambient temperature, dry bulb 59F, design ambient relative
humidity 60%. The TPC estimates were reported in January 2006 $. The draft results of this study are shown in Table 8-1.

As will be seen later on in this Chapter the cost estimates of all technologies are today much higher than the estimates in this table. For
both IGCC and PC plants of this size the TPC estimates are now in the vicinity of 2000 $/kW in Mid 2006 $. The work in this report
is the most comprehensive of the new public domain documents published in 2006. While the estimates are certainly too low the
percentage differentials between technologies shown in Table 8-1 are probably mostly valid.
8-4

The one exception may be that the percentage added costs for PC post combustion capture are on
the high side, representing ~ 68 % increase in LCOE. They are higher than those shown in the
IEA report PH 4-33 as reported in last year’s EPRI’s Gasification Update # 1010460. EPRI is
investigating the differences in these evaluations and currently the expectation is that the COE
increase is somewhere between the values in the two reports - IEA too low and these DOE
NETL numbers too high – with perhaps 60 % being more representative of the COE increase.
In general the main conclusions to be drawn from this DOE NETL study are consistent with the
findings from other previous studies on bituminous coals conducted by EPRI and DOE, namely:
• For IGCC plants without capture COP E-Gas is lower capital cost than GE RQ or Shell. This
is attributable to the lower height of the structure (no slag lock hoppers), the side by side
arrangement of gasifier and syngas cooler and the lower cost fire-tube syngas cooler
• GE RQ and Shell IGCC without capture are similar in cost. The water syngas coolers are
about four times the weight of the fire tube coolers
• For IGCC with capture GE RQ has lower added COE than COP E-Gas or Shell. This can be
attributed to the higher pressure 800 psig of the GE gasifier versus 600 psig for E-Gas and
Shell, and to the higher moisture of the syngas after the quench with GE than with the other
technologies. The Shell syngas has an additional disadvantage of higher CO content which
requires more steam for conducting the shift reaction. See later Table 8-
• When the plants are designed with capture the COE of the IGCC plants is significantly lower
than the COE of PC plants with post combustion capture
• When evaluated at the same CF the COE for NGCC without capture is about the same as the
coal plants when natural gas is ~6 $/MBtu. However at that high dispatch cost it is unlikely
that the NGCC would be dispatched at that high 80% CF.
In addition to incorporation of some of the reviewer’s comments the study results are to be
updated and it can be anticipated that the cost numbers will all be increased from those reported
above in Table 8-1.
Wisconsin PUC IGCC Draft Report 2006
In April 2006 the Wisconsin PUC published a draft report on IGCC. In this report IGCC was
treated generically, not as a specifically identified technology. The COE comparison of nominal
600 MW plants showed ~15% higher COE for IGCC than SCPC. The IGCC COE was
~$5/MWh higher with eastern bituminous coal and ~$7/MWh higher with western coal. This
differential is similar to that shown in several other studies.
The “Capital Cost” with eastern coal was reported as $1628/kW for SCPC and $1872/kW for
IGCC. It’s not clear whether this is meant to be TPC or TCR. The effect of lower capital and
higher availability for the IGCC COE were also estimated.
For CO capture increases for IGCC were quoted only as +35% capital (presumably stated as
2
$/kW) and +20% HR, and for SCPC +60% capital (presumably stated as $/kW) and +30% HR.
This COE comparison showed the IGCC COE to be ~$10/MWh less than the SCPC COE.
8-5

Although this report is lacking in details the results are consistent with other previous reports
comparing IGCC and PC with and without capture for bituminous coals.
EPA IGCC and PC Technology Assessments -July 2006
In this study conducted by Nexant for EPA IGCC and USC PC technologies were evaluated for
three coals – a bituminous coal similar to Illinois #6, PRB, and lignite. Slurry-fed IGCC was
used for the Illinois #6 and PRB; and dry coal feed IGCC for the lignite. The plant size was set
arbitrarily at 500 MW net. This size does not represent the net output of the current IGCC
offerings which are usually 600-630 MW net. Most other studies are based on the real gas
turbines, typically GE 7 FB’s or Siemens 5000 F’s, as contained in the current commercial
offerings. The main comparative figures from the EPA report are shown in Table 8-2. The cost
estimates in the EPA report were stated as 4th quarter 2004 $. In Table 8-2 the original estimates
are shown from the EPA report but they are also shown as updated to July 2006 $ using the
CEPCI index.

The heat rates for IGCC and USC look ambitiously low and are lower than other 2006 estimates.
When the TPC and TCR estimates are adjusted to July 2006 $ (CEPCI factor 1.092), they still
appear lower than most current estimates (particularly for the lignite PC).
No estimates on CO capture were included in the report. However the Executive Summary
2
states that IGCC is more effective for CCS than PC with post-combustion capture. Section 5 of
the report discusses CCS and cites DOE, EPRI, IEA, and Canadian CPC reports from 2000–03.
It should be noted that in the 2003 Canadian CPC report for lignite PC with CCS was evaluated
to have a lower COE than IGCC with CCS.
Southern Company Evaluation of KBR IGCC
The Southern Company presented an evaluation of the KBR Transport gasification technology at
the Pittsburgh Coal Conference in 2005. They evaluated IGCC cases for air and oxygen
gasification both with and without CO capture based on PRB coal. The capture cases were not
2
optimized and the cost and performance can probably be improved. The results of this study are
presented in Table 8-3. The original estimates were presented in January 2003$ for a South East

In this preliminary evaluation the oxygen cases are very much more costly and less efficient than
the air blown cases. The additional cost of capture is very high for both air and oxygen cases but
is particularly onerous for the oxygen case. The steam requirement of the KBR gasifier
considerably reduces the steam turbine and net power output. Further testing at the PSDF and
additional optimization of the cycle may reduce the additional capture costs.
PUC Submissions and Press announcements for New Plants 2006
There have been a number of submissions to various PUCs for new IGCC and PC plants that
include estimates for the required capital investment. It is often not very clear what is included in
these estimates. They may include financing costs and it seems that each company has its own
definition of what is included in the category of Owner’s costs. However the numbers quoted are
probably most similar to what EPRI defines as Total Capital Requirement (TCR). Some of the
recent announcements and the cost estimates are shown in Table 8-4.
It can be seen that the capital costs reported are significantly higher than those in the reports
from DOE NETL, Wisconsin PUC, and EPA even when adjusted with the CEPCI to more
current $. The large PC plants for AEP and Duke will have some significant economies of scale
but nevertheless these estimates are much higher than would generally have been estimated for
such plants.
Some more recent estimates have been made by EPRI for IGCC plants and a recently published
report of a study conducted by Burns & MacDonnell for City Public Service of San Antonio has
some updated estimates for Shell IGCC and USC PC based on PRB coal. The cost estimates in
these more recent studies are much closer to those from recent submissions in Table 8-4.

EPRI FutureGen Studies and 2006 IGCC Updates
Several of the CoalFleet IGCC experts team (Todd, Higman, Holt, Booras, Phillips, and
Wheeldon) are also participating in the FutureGen Technical Experts Group (TEG) and have
attended many of the presentations made by the various potential technology suppliers. Battelle
has been retained by the FutureGen Alliance for the general project management. The Alliance
and Battelle asked the EPRI members of the TEG to complete an estimated cost and performance
evaluation of 13 possible configurations for the FutureGen project designed for a range of coals
covering Northern Appalachian, Illinois and PRB coals. Since a primary purpose of the
FutureGen project is to demonstrate CCS including the sequestration of 1-2 million tons/year, all
of the design configurations included 90% capture of the coal‘s carbon as CO.
2
Worley Parsons is now conducting a design and cost estimate for 3 configurations selected by
the Alliance using the same 3 coals. These selected configurations include two single train
configurations – a slurry feed water quench and a dry coal feed water quench. The third
configuration selected was a Multiple Stream Hybrid – 100% train of Full Slurry Quench with a
30% train with the KBR gasifier with ITM ASU. The final actual designs will be established by
competitive bids in 2007.
The approach used by the EPRI staff in estimating the capital cost of the 13 configurations was:
• To use the knowledge base of previous studies (2002–03) and adjust the labor rates and
productivity to a standard Midwest location
• Scale up plant size to reflect the 7 FB and 5000F fuel requirements and improved
performance
8-8

Economics – IGCC Without and With CO2 Capture
• Bring all estimates up to May 2006$ using the CEPCI
From this work, EPRI separately developed cases both with and without capture for commercial
size two-train IGCC plants at ~620 MW net. This methodology will inevitably show the same
kind of percentage differential cost between technologies as in the 2002–03 studies.
However, in absence of new studies, this was the only viable approach available. In general
the more recent study results from DOE NETL have confirmed that the cost differentials
between IGCC technologies with and without capture are still similar to those found in the
previous studies.
Updated estimates were made for four IGCC technologies with and without capture. It must be
emphasized that these designs for capture are grass roots designs and do not reflect the costs for
adding capture to a plant not previously designed for capture. This latter topic, adding capture to
a plant not initially designed for capture will be discussed later in this Chapter.
The four technologies evaluated were GE Radiant Quench (RQ), GE Total Quench (Q), E-Gas,
and Shell Gas Recycle Quench. The designs were without spare gasifiers and were based on
Illinois#6 coal (but could also handle Northern Appalachian Pittsburgh seam coal).
Given the data base and methodology, together with the rapidly escalating construction costs the
error band on these estimates is wide. The calculated cost estimates are presented in Table 8-5.
However the TPC costs and 30 year Levelized COEs are presented in Figures 8-2 and 8-3 as a
band -5% to + 20% of the base cost estimates in Table 8-5.
The trends in differential costs for capture are similar to those in the DOE NETL 2006 Draft
report and to previous DOE, IEA and EPRI studies. The GE water Quench is the least cost way
of adding the moisture to syngas for the shift reaction. Higher pressure e.g. 800-1000 psig
decreases the cost of CO removal and compression through use of a physical absorption system
2
(e.g. Selexol) where solvent recovery is largely achieved through depressurization and without
large steam (energy) penalty. Some CO can be recovered at pressure reducing the needed MW
2
for compression. GE can offer high pressure designs and both Q and RQ provide substantial
amounts of moisture for shift. The COP E-Gas, Shell, Siemens and KBR gasifiers are at lower
pressure (< 600 psig) and the water wash contact with the syngas is at lower temperature
producing lower moisture in the syngas.

IGCC Designs for Capture
When considering the effects of adding the shift reaction for carbon capture and synthesis it is
instructive to look at the moisture content of the syngas from the various technologies as shown
in Table 8-6. The data used in this table are mostly taken from the 2006 DOE NETL report.
Table 8-6
Effect of Gasification Technology on Shift Steam Requirements and Steam Turbine MW
Technology Pressure H2O/CO Relative HP Steam Flow Steam Turbine MW
PSIG Ratio to Shift Output
GE RQ 800 1.3 1.0 270
COP E-Gas Full Slurry 600 0.4 2.0 216
Quench
Shell Recycle Gas 600 0.1 2.8 202
Quench
In the GE design the Radiant cooler outlet temperature is 1200-1300 F and the water quench puts
considerable moisture in the syngas so that less steam has to be added from the steam cycle to
accomplish the shift reaction. In the COP E-Gas flow sheet the syngas is directly contacted with
water (to remove chloride prior to COS hydrolysis) at a much lower temperature of about 400 F
so less moisture is added to the syngas and more steam has to be taken from the steam cycle.
Since the Shell process is dry coal fed there is less moisture in the raw syngas than with the

GE Radiant Quench GE Total Quench Shell Gas Quench E-Gas FSQ

slurry fed GE and E-Gas processes, and the CO content is higher at ~60%. As with E-Gas the
syngas is directly contacted with water (to remove chloride prior to COS hydrolysis) at a much
lower temperature ~ 400 F and therefore even more steam needs to be added to accomplish the
shift reaction. The extra steam demand for E-Gas and Shell has a marked effect on the output of
the steam turbine and the net plant output with capture.
In the case of a GE total Quench (Q) design the HO/CO ratio is ~3/1 and provides nearly all the
2
steam needed to drive the shift reaction to equilibrium.
Some additional discussion of the effects of technology selection on capture, and
recommendations for improvements to each of the major gasification technologies are given in
Chapters 1 and 2. The main recommendations are:
• GE larger size (1200-1800 ft3) HP Quench. New feed/design for low rank coals
• COP tall Cylinder design (possibly two diameters with truncated cone connection) for higher
pressure. Should also enable higher throughput for low rank coals
• Shell larger gasifier with water quench design. CO transport of coal feed for capture and
2
synthesis. Lower cost drying or new feeder for low rank coals
• Siemens needs to scale up to larger gasifiers
IGCC Design Options for Capture
There are three general levels of capture that can be designed for in IGCC plants.
• For slurry fed gasifiers the CO in the syngas can represent 20-25% of the coal’s carbon that
2
could be removed without using the Shift reaction. This relatively small amount of capture is
unlikely to generate much support from Federal or State Authorities with regard to making
any significant contribution to reduction of CO emissions. However it could be of some
2
value for production of CO for EOR or other use.
2
• All gasification technologies can use a sour High Temperature Shift followed by a two
column AGR. The syngas may still be able to use standard syngas GT combustors. This
could result in 60 -80 % CO capture which would satisfy California’s criteria that the
2
CO/MWH is no more than from NGCC. The COE would be lower than maximum capture
2
option.
• If > 90% removal is required then both high and low temperature shift beds can be used.
However this would require GT combustors designed for Hydrogen. The COE would be the
highest of the three options.
The effect of different degrees of capture on COE and the avoided cost of CO for GE Q designs
2
are shown in Figure 8-4. There is a large increase in the avoided cost of CO when shift is added
2
but it declines as the % CO removal is increased.

One of the main contributors to the additional cost of capture with IGCC is that the GE gas
turbines, whether 7 FA or 7 FB, no air extraction is possible when firing hydrogen. Although
there is added capital for the shift, CO2 removal and compression the major increase in TPC
$/kW net results from the additional auxiliary power requirement for capture. Since there is no
air available to the ASU from the GT compressor the main air compressor (MAC) must be sized
to provide all the air to the ASU. It is not yet clear whether the Siemens turbines will have the
same limitation. The additional cost of capture in IGCC plants would be considerably reduced if
gas turbines were available for hydrogen firing that could allow air extraction across the range of
ambient temperatures.
IGCC Pre-Investment Options for later Addition of CO Capture
2
Most of the IGCC designs evaluated in previous studies have incorporated capture in the initial
designs. However in view of the uncertainty, at least in the US of CO regulations it is probably
2
more appropriate to consider the addition of capture to an IGCC design initially without capture
or with various degrees of pre-investment. Depending on the owner’s perception of the extent
and timing of potential; regulation various degrees of pre-investment may be appropriate to
optimize the plant output and performance over time.

Several pre-investment options can be considered for later addition of capture:
• Standard Provisions. Leave space for additional equipment and tie-ins, BOP, and site access
at later date. There is a net power capacity, efficiency and cost penalty upon conversion to
capture.
• Moderate Provisions. Additional ASU, Gasification and gas clean-up is needed to fully load
the GT’s when Shift is added. If this over-sizing is included in the initial IGCC investment
the capacity can be used in the pre-capture phase for supplemental firing or co-production.
This version of “capture ready” would then permit full GT output with Hydrogen (at ISO
conditions) when capture is added. The cost and efficiency penalties are mitigated. However
when shift is added considerable AGR modifications will be required (See Table 8-6)
• Extensive Provisions. In this option the pre-investment design is with conversion-shift
reactors, oversized components, an AGR absorber sized for shifted syngas but no CO
2
absorber and compressor. There should be no need for a major shutdown to complete the
conversion to CO capture
2
When the shift reaction is added to a design the dry gas flow to the AGR is increased markedly.
This is illustrated in Table 8-7 which shows the relative increase in dry gas flow when the shift
reaction is added to the flow sheets of the GE, COP E-Gas and Shell technologies. Each of the
analyses is shown as a molar flow of 100 moles and the effect of the shift reaction on total flow
of dry gas is shown to increase the flow of dry gas by 44%, 46% and 60% for GE, COP E-Gas
and Shell respectively.

The addition of Sour Shift increases gas flow to the AGR particularly for the dry coal fed
gasifiers with high CO content. It is unlikely that the AGR as originally designed would be able
to take the extra flow unless there was pre-investment over-sizing. It would probably be
necessary to add a parallel absorber or to replace the entire AGR plant (with a new two column
absorption system) if capture is to be added to an existing IGCC designed without capture.
8-14

Economics – IGCC Without and With CO2 Capture
Alternatively the original AGR (focused in its design on HS Removal) could be retained and a
2
Sweet shift added after the AGR with a simpler bulk CO removal AGR (ADIP, MDEA, Selexol)
2
added after shift. This would minimize intrusion into existing plant. This trade off of Sour versus
Sweet Shift needs to be examined and may differ among the gasification technologies. Sweet
Shift may incur additional efficiency and output penalties. With water quench gasifiers Sour
Shift would most probably be selected.
CPS Energy (San Antonio) Study of IGCC and PC
CPS Energy (San Antonio) is constructing a 750 MW supercritical PC plant fired with PRB coal.
The plant is known as Spruce 2, southeast of San Antonio. The plant was opposed by some
environmental groups because of the projected greenhouse gas and mercury emissions. As part
of a settlement with the environmental groups, CPS Energy agreed to enhance its energy
conservation and renewable energy programs and to pay increased attention to CO emissions
2
from future coal plants. CPS Energy also committed to fund a study of IGCC with combinations
of fuels such as PRB and pet coke. Under the terms of the settlement, the IGCC study will be
made available to the public. CPS Energy selected Burns & McDonnell to perform the study,
with assistance from EPRI.
The study basis was an evaluation of Shell IGCC and SCPC plants 550 MW plants at a Texas
coastal location. The coal was PRB at a delivered cost of $1.65/MBtu HHV. An IGCC case
using a 50/50 by weight blend of PRB and Pet coke was also evaluated using a delivered cost of
pet coke of $1.12/MBtu. The Shell IGCC included Selexol as the AGR and 2 x GE 7 FB gas
turbines. The SCPC steam conditions were 3500 psi/1050°F/1050°F.
This study was one of the first that has evaluated the addition of capture to IGCC and SCPC
plants that were designed without significant pre-investment. Most previous studies of IGCC
and PC plants had evaluated designs with capture included from the beginning.
The IGCC retrofit for 90% CO recovery included replacement of COS/HCN hydrolysis reactor
2
with 2 stages of sour shift reaction, additions to syngas cooling train for the shift reactors,
additions to the Selexol AGR to recover CO as a separate by-product, upgrade of the
2
demineralizer water treatment and storage system for 450,000 lb/hr IP steam for water-gas shift
reaction, HRSG LP superheater modifications and addition of CO drying and compression to
2
2000psig.
The SCPC retrofit for 90% CO recovery included addition of Fluor’s Econamine FG Plus
2
(EFG+) process (MEA based chemical solvent), wet FGD upgrades to reduce SO to 7 ppmv
2
(98%) to reduce formation of heat stable salts in the MEA solvent, addition of a new cooling
tower and circulating water system for Econamine FG+ cooling and the addition of CO drying
2
and compression to 2000psig.
The CO capture capital costs are based on retrofit of the existing IGCC or PC facilities as
2
provided in the base case alternatives. The $/kW values reflect total installed cost (including
original costs provided in the base case) divided by net plant output with CO capture. The
2
results of this study are summarized in Table 8-8.

The capital costs are in mid 2006$ and are very much higher than had been expected for the
Texas Gulf coast location. The continued rise in equipment, commodity and labor costs are most
evident in these estimates. CPS Energy is a municipal utility that has access to low cost financing
so that the COEs in Table 8-8 are lower than those presented elsewhere in this Chapter. The
Shell IGCC for PRB has a COE ~ 15% greater than the SCPC COE with PRB coal. However,
when using a PRB/pet coke blend the IGCC COE is reduced by 9%. The IGCC COE with
capture is increased by 45% over that without capture. The SCPC COE with capture is 58%
greater than the COE without capture. However, overall the IGCC COE with capture is greater
by ~5.5% than the SCPC COE with capture.
The study is available on EPRI’s public web site. The report number is 1014510.
The study had to be completed by a pre-agreed deadline and there was not enough time to
optimize the designs. The IGCC Selexol design with capture and the SCPC Econamine process
would both benefit from some further optimization and this is planned. Additional assistance will
be requested from GE and Shell. It would also be beneficial to conduct a case based on the COP
E-Gas technology since the DOE NETL study results discussed earlier indicate that the
additional costs for adding capture to E-Gas would be lower than those for Shell.
However, the general conclusion from this study is that the Shell IGCC and SCPC designs with
capture for PRB sub-bituminous coal are very close in COE. This result is consistent with the
previous study conducted by the Canadian CPC as reported in EPRI Report 1004880 March
2004.
